Siegel Modular Forms — The Siegel Upper Half-Space, Eisenstein Series & the Saito–Kurokawa Lift

Deep dive into Siegel modular forms — the Siegel upper half-space ℃_g = {τ ∈ M_g(ℂ) | τ = τ^T, Im(τ) > 0} (a Hermitian symmetric domain of type III), the symplectic group Sp(2g,ℝ) acting on ℃_g by τ ↦ (Aτ+B)(Cτ+D)⁻¹, Siegel modular forms of weight k (holomorphic functions F: ℃_g → ℂ with F((Aτ+B)(Cτ+D)⁻¹) = det(Cτ+D)^k F(τ)), the Fourier–Jacobi expansion, the Siegel Φ-operator and the theory of cusp forms, Siegel Eisenstein series E_k^{(g)}(τ) = Σ_{(C,D)} det(Cτ+D)⁻^k summed over coprime symmetric pairs, Klingen’s Eisenstein series, the Maass lift (a correspondence between elliptic modular forms and Siegel modular forms of degree 2), the Saito–Kurokawa lift (a lifting from elliptic cusp forms of weight 2k−2 to Siegel cusp forms of weight k for Sp(4,ℝ)), the Ikeda lift (the generalization to higher degrees), and connections to the Langlands functoriality and L-functions.

“Siegel modular forms are the natural generalization of classical modular forms to several variables, living on the symmetric space of the symplectic group.”
— Carl Ludwig Siegel
Part 1

📐 The Siegel Upper Half-Space and Symplectic Group

Siegel Space
℃_g, the Symplectic Group Sp(2g,ℝ), and the Action
The Siegel upper half-space of genus g is ℃_g = {τ ∈ M_g(ℂ) | τ = τ^T, Im(τ) > 0} where Im(τ) > 0 means positive definite. For g=1, ℃₁ = ℓ (the usual upper half-plane). For g>1, ℃_g is a Hermitian symmetric domain of type III (Cartan’s classification), with automorphism group the real symplectic group Sp(2g,ℝ). The group Sp(2g,ℝ) = {M ∈ GL(2g,ℝ) | M^T J_g M = J_g} where J_g = (0 I_g; −I_g 0) is the standard symplectic matrix. It acts transitively on ℃_g by τ ↦ (Aτ+B)(Cτ+D)⁻¹ with the decomposition M = ((A,B),(C,D)) in g×g blocks. The maximal compact subgroup is K ≅ U(g) fixing τ = iI_g. The discrete subgroup Γ_g = Sp(2g,ℤ) (the Siegel modular group) acts properly discontinuously on ℃_g. The quotient ℃_g/Γ_g is a moduli space of principally polarized abelian varieties (the Satake–Baily–Borel compactification). The boundary components of the Satake compactification correspond to degenerate abelian varieties (products of lower-dimensional ones). The Fourier expansion of a Siegel modular form F(τ) = Σ_{T≥0} a(T) exp(2πi Tr(Tτ)) where T runs over half-integral positive semidefinite symmetric matrices (T = (t_{ij}) with t_{ii} ∈ ℤ and 2t_{ij} ∈ ℤ for i≠j). The theta series θ_S(τ) = Σ_{x∈ℤ^g} exp(πi x^T S x τ) for a positive definite integral quadratic form S gives Siegel modular forms.
The Siegel upper half-space generalizes the Poincaré upper half-plane to genus g, with the symplectic group replacing SL₂(ℝ).
Part 2

🏛 Siegel Modular Forms and Eisenstein Series

Siegel Forms
Siegel Modular Forms, Cusp Forms, the Φ-Operator, and Eisenstein Series
A Siegel modular form of weight k for Γ_g = Sp(2g,ℤ) is a holomorphic function F: ℃_g → ℂ satisfying F((Aτ+B)(Cτ+D)⁻¹) = det(Cτ+D)^k F(τ) for all ((A,B),(C,D)) ∈ Γ_g, with holomorphicity at the cusps (automatic for g>1 by Koecher’s principle). For g=1, we need holomorphicity at i∞. The Siegel Φ-operator (the Siegel analogue of the Ramanujan θ-operator) is defined for g≥2 by (ΦF)(τ') = lim_{t→i∞} F((τ' 0; 0 t)) where τ' ∈ ℃_{g−1}. The image of Φ is a Siegel modular form of degree g−1. The kernel of Φ is the space of Siegel cusp forms S_k(Γ_g). The space M_k(Γ_g) of Siegel modular forms is finite-dimensional. The Siegel Eisenstein series: E_k^{(g)}(τ) = Σ_{(C,D)} det(Cτ+D)⁻^k where the sum is over all coprime symmetric pairs (C,D) of g×g matrices (the set Γ_g,∞\Γ_g divided by the stabilizer of the cusp). The series converges absolutely for k > g+1. The Fourier expansion of E_k^{(g)}(τ) has rational coefficients (the Siegel formula: the constant term involves ζ(1−2k) and ζ(3−2k),…). For g=2, the Siegel Eisenstein series of weight k has Fourier coefficients a(T) = Σ_{d|ε(T)} d^{k−1} σ_{k−2}(ε(T)/d) where σ_r(n) = Σ_{d|n} d^r and ε(T) = gcd of the entries of 2T. The Klingen Eisenstein series lift a cusp form of degree g−1 to a Siegel modular form of degree g by summing over Γ_g. The Maaß space M_k^*(Γ_2) consists of those Siegel modular forms of degree 2 whose Fourier coefficients a(T) depend only on the GL₂(ℤ)-equivalence class of T (the discriminant and the content).
Siegel Eisenstein series are the building blocks of spaces of Siegel modular forms; their Fourier coefficients encode deep arithmetic information.
Part 3

🌐 The Saito–Kurokawa Lift

Lift
The Saito–Kurokawa Lift, the Maaß Space, and the Ikeda Lift
The Saito–Kurokawa lift (Kurokawa, 1978; Saito, 1977) is a linear map from elliptic cusp forms of weight 2k−2 for SL₂(ℤ) to Siegel cusp forms of weight k for Sp(4,ℤ). More precisely, given a normalized Hecke eigenform f ∈ S_{2k−2}(SL₂(ℤ)) with Fourier coefficients a(n), the lifted form F = Lift(f) ∈ S_k(Sp(4,ℤ)) has Fourier coefficients: for T = ((a,b/2),(b/2,c)) with a,b,c ∈ ℤ, a,b,c ≥ 0, the Fourier coefficient A(T) = Σ_{d|gcd(a,b,c)} d^{k−1} a(4ac−b^2/4d^2). The lift factors through the Maaß space: the image of the Saito–Kurokawa lift is exactly the Maaß subspace M_k^*(Γ_2). Andrianov proved that the Maaß subspace is characterized by the property that its Fourier coefficients a(T) depend only on the GL₂(ℤ)-orbit of T. The L-function of the lifted form F equals L(f, s) · ζ(s−k+1) · ζ(s−k+2). The lift is injective. The Ikeda lift (Ikeda, 2001) generalizes the Saito–Kurokawa lift to higher degrees: given an elliptic cusp form f ∈ S_{2k}(SL₂(ℤ)), the Ikeda lift produces a Siegel cusp form F ∈ S_{k+n−1}(Sp(2n,ℤ)) whose standard L-function is L(f, s) · ∏_{j=1}^{n−1} ζ(2s−2j). The Fourier coefficients of F are given by the Ikeda’s formula involving the Fourier coefficients of f and the Witt’s theorem on representation numbers of quadratic forms. The Ikeda lift is a crucial example of Langlands functoriality: it corresponds to the transfer from GL₂ to the symplectic group Sp(2n) via the symmetric square L-function.
The Saito–Kurokawa lift constructs Siegel modular forms from elliptic modular forms, realizing Langlands functoriality between GL₂ and GSp₄.
Part 4

🌟 Applications and Further Topics

Applications
L-Functions, the Bloch–Kato Conjecture, and the Gan–Gross–Prasad Conjecture
Siegel modular forms appear throughout number theory. The Andrianov L-function attached to a Siegel modular form of degree 2 is the spinor L-function Z_F(s) = ∏_p Q_p(p⁻^s)⁻¹ where Q_p(X) = (1−α_p X)(1−α_p⁻¹β_p X)(1−β_p⁻¹γ_p X)(1−γ_p X) and α_p,β_p,γ_p arise from Satake parameters. The standard L-function L(F, s, St) relates to the symmetric square L-function of the corresponding elliptic modular form under the Saito–Kurokawa lift. The Bloch–Kato conjecture for motives attached to Siegel modular forms relates special values of L-functions to Selmer groups. The Gan–Gross–Prasad conjecture (for symplectic groups) predicts the non-vanishing of periods of automorphic forms on GSpin groups, with applications to the Gross–Zagier formula and the Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture. The theta correspondence (Howe duality) pairs Siegel modular forms with metaplectic forms: the Shimura correspondence lifts forms of half-integer weight to integer weight. The lift of Yoshida (the Yoshida lift) constructs Siegel modular forms of degree 2 from pairs of elliptic modular forms. The Siegel–Weil formula connects Siegel Eisenstein series to theta series via the Weil representation; this is the key to the analytic continuation and functional equation of the Siegel Eisenstein series. The Kudla program (Kudla, Millson, Rapoport) relates Fourier coefficients of Siegel Eisenstein series to intersection numbers of special cycles on Shimura varieties, leading to arithmetic applications like the Kudla’s conjecture on central derivatives of L-functions. The paramodular conjecture (Brumer–Kramer) predicts that abelian surfaces with endomorphism ring ℤ correspond to Siegel modular forms of degree 2 with rational eigenvalues.
Siegel modular forms sit at the intersection of automorphic forms, arithmetic geometry, and the Langlands program, with applications to L-functions and cycle theory.

🎯 Practice

← Back to Math Map

The Fourier–Jacobi Expansion and Jacobi Forms

For a Siegel modular form F of degree g ≥ 2 and weight k, the Fourier expansion with respect to the last diagonal entry of the period matrix Z = ( τ z ; zt τ' ) yields the Fourier–Jacobi expansion

F(Z) = Σm≥0 φm(τ, z) e2πi m τ'

where each coefficient φm is a Jacobi form of weight k and index m on the Jacobi group ΓJ = SL2(ℤ) ⋉ ℤ2. The theory of Jacobi forms, developed by Eichler and Zagier (1985), provides a bridge between Siegel modular forms of genus 2 and classical elliptic modular forms. The Fourier coefficients of φm are indexed by pairs (n, r) ∈ ℤ × ℤ with discriminant Δ = 4nm − r2 ≥ 0, and the transformation law under the Heisenberg part of ΓJ forces the coefficients to depend only on Δ and the congruence class of r modulo 2m. This structure is crucial for the proof of the Saito–Kurokawa lift: the lifted Siegel cusp form has a Fourier–Jacobi expansion whose first non‑zero coefficient φ1 is the classical Jacobi form attached to the elliptic eigenform f via the Shimura correspondence. Moreover, the Maass space can be characterized as the subspace of Siegel modular forms whose Fourier–Jacobi coefficients φm vanish for all m > 1, a fact first observed by Maass (1979) and later refined by Arakawa (1996). The Fourier–Jacobi expansion also underlies the construction of the Ikeda lift, where the higher‑degree Siegel form is built from a compatible family of Jacobi forms of increasing index.

The Koecher Principle and Holomorphy at Infinity

The Koecher principle states that any Siegel modular form of genus g ≥ 2 which is holomorphic on the Siegel upper half‑space 𝔥g automatically extends holomorphically to the Satake compactification; in other words, there are no “cusp singularities” for higher genus. Concretely, if F ∈ Mk(Sp(2g, ℤ)) has a Fourier expansion F(Z) = ΣT≥0 a(T) e2πi tr(TZ) with T running over semi‑positive definite half‑integral matrices, the condition that F be bounded on a Siegel set forces a(T) = 0 unless T is positive semi‑definite. For genus 1 this is false: the classical Eisenstein series E2 has a non‑zero constant term but fails to be modular of weight 2. The Koecher principle is proved by estimating the growth of F on the standard Siegel set 𝔖 = { Z = X + iY : Y ≥ Y0 > 0, |X| ≤ ½ } and using the fact that the symplectic group acts transitively on 𝔥g. A key corollary is that the space of Siegel cusp forms Sk(Sp(2g, ℤ)) coincides with the subspace of forms whose Fourier coefficients a(T) vanish for all singular T (det T = 0). This vanishing property is used in the definition of the Maass lift and in the proof that the Saito–Kurokawa lift lands in the cuspidal subspace. The principle also implies that the Siegel Eisenstein series Ek(g) converges absolutely for k > g + 1 and defines a holomorphic modular form; its Fourier coefficients are given by representation numbers of quadratic forms, a result of Siegel (1935) that links analytic properties to arithmetic of quadratic forms.

Worked Example: Fourier–Jacobi Coefficient of a Saito–Kurokawa Lift
Let f(τ) = Σ a(n) qn be the normalized Hecke eigenform of weight 10 for SL2(ℤ) (the unique cusp form Δ·E4). Compute the Fourier–Jacobi coefficient φ1(τ, z) of the Saito–Kurokawa lift F = Lift(f) of weight 6 for Sp(4, ℤ).
The Saito–Kurokawa lift has Fourier expansion F(Z) = ΣT≥0 A(T) e2πi tr(TZ) with A(T) = Σd|gcd(a,b,c) dk-1 a( (4ac−b2)/(4d2) ) for T = ((a, b/2), (b/2, c)). The Fourier–Jacobi coefficient φ1 collects all terms with c = 1. Setting c = 1 gives discriminant Δ = 4a·1 − b2 = 4a − b2. For each Δ > 0 with Δ ≡ 0,1 (mod 4) there is a unique pair (a,b) up to the action of SL2(ℤ). The coefficient of e2πi (aτ + bz + τ') in φ1 equals Σd|gcd(a,b,1) d5 a(Δ/(4d2)). Since gcd(a,b,1)=1, the sum reduces to a(Δ/4) when Δ ≡ 0 (mod 4) and 0 otherwise. Thus φ1(τ, z) = ΣΔ>0, Δ≡0(4) a(Δ/4) ζΔ qΔ/4 where ζ = e2πi z, q = e2πi τ. This is precisely the Jacobi form of weight 6 and index 1 attached to f via the Eichler–Zagier correspondence.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the first Fourier–Jacobi coefficient of a Saito–Kurokawa lift, remember that only the index‑1 part survives and it is the Jacobi lift of the original elliptic eigenform. Write down the discriminant condition Δ = 4ac − b2 and use the fact that gcd(a,b,c)=1 for primitive T. This reduces the double sum to a single sum over Δ, a standard shortcut that saves time.

The Satake Parameters and the Spinor L‑Function

For a Siegel Hecke eigenform F of genus 2 and weight k, the local Hecke algebra at an unramified prime p is generated by the double cosets T(p) and T1(p2). The eigenvalues λp and μp satisfy the characteristic polynomial

Qp(X) = (1 − αp X)(1 − βp X)(1 − γp X)(1 − δp X)

with αpδp = βpγp = pk−3. The four Satake parameters (αp, βp, γp, δp) are determined up to the Weyl group of type C2. The spinor L‑function is defined by the Euler product

ZF(s) = ∏p (1 − αp p−s)−1 (1 − βp p−s)−1 (1 − γp p−s)−1 (1 − δp p−s)−1.

Andrianov (1974) proved that ZF(s) admits analytic continuation to ℂ and satisfies a functional equation relating s to 2k − 3 − s. For a Saito–Kurokawa lift F = Lift(f) with f of weight 2k−2, the Satake parameters factor as {αp, βp, pk−1, pk−2} where αp, βp are the Satake parameters of f. Consequently ZF(s) = L(f, s) ζ(s−k+1) ζ(s−k+2), matching the product of the elliptic L‑function and two shifted Riemann zeta factors. The standard L‑function L(F, s, St) is the degree‑5 L‑function attached to the standard representation of GSp(4); for a generic (non‑CAP) eigenform it is expected to be entire and satisfy a functional equation of the form s 1−s. The Ramanujan–Petersson conjecture for genus 2 predicts |αp| = |βp| = |γp| = |δp| = p(k−3)/2; this is known for Saito–Kurokawa lifts (where two parameters are powers of p) and for certain Yoshida lifts, but remains open in general. Numerical verification of the Satake parameters via the Hecke eigenvalues computed from Fourier coefficients is a standard computational exercise in the theory of Siegel modular forms.

The Theta Correspondence and the Weil Representation

The theta correspondence, also known as Howe duality, provides a powerful method to lift automorphic forms between members of a reductive dual pair inside a symplectic group. For the dual pair (Sp(2g), O(m)) inside Sp(2gm), the Weil representation ωψ of the metaplectic double cover Mp(2gm) decomposes as a direct integral of tensor products π ⊗ σ where π is an irreducible representation of Sp(2g) and σ of O(m). When m = 1, O(1) ≅ {±1} and the theta lift from Sp(2g) to the metaplectic group Mp(2) recovers the classical Shimura correspondence: a Siegel modular form of half‑integral weight k+½ on the metaplectic group corresponds to an integral weight Siegel modular form on Sp(2g). For m = 2, the dual pair (Sp(4), O(2)) yields the Saito–Kurokawa lift: the theta lift of a holomorphic elliptic cusp form of weight 2k−2 (viewed as an automorphic form on the split torus O(2) ≅ GL1 ⋊ ℤ/2) produces a Siegel cusp form of weight k on Sp(4). The Siegel–Weil formula, proved by Weil (1965) and extended by Kudla–Rallis (1988), identifies the Siegel Eisenstein series Ek(g) with the theta integral of the Siegel theta series attached to the standard lattice ℤ2g. This identity is the analytic engine behind the functional equation of Ek(g) and underlies the proof that the Fourier coefficients of Eisenstein series are representation numbers of quadratic forms. In higher genus, the Kudla program predicts that the Fourier coefficients of the derivative of the Siegel Eisenstein series at its central point encode arithmetic intersection numbers of special cycles on Shimura varieties of orthogonal type. The theta correspondence also explains the existence of the Yoshida lift, which attaches to a pair of elliptic eigenforms (f, g) of weights (k1, k2) a Siegel cusp form of genus 2 whose spinor L‑function is the product L(f, s) L(g, s). This lift is realized by the theta lift from the dual pair (Sp(4), O(2,2)) where O(2,2) ≅ (GL2 × GL2)/GL1.

The Paramodular Conjecture and Abelian Surfaces

The paramodular conjecture, formulated by Brumer and Kramer (2014), predicts a precise correspondence between isogeny classes of abelian surfaces A/ℚ with End(A) = ℤ and paramodular Siegel cusp forms of degree 2, weight 2, and level N equal to the conductor of A. A paramodular form is a Siegel modular form for the paramodular group K(N) ⊂ Sp(4, ℚ), which is the stabilizer of a certain lattice chain; it is the natural level structure for abelian surfaces with non‑split multiplicative reduction. The conjecture asserts that the spinor L‑function ZF(s) of the paramodular form F coincides with the Hasse–Weil L‑function L(A, s) of the abelian surface, and that the Fourier coefficients a(T) of F encode the local point counts #A(𝔽p) via the Eichler–Shimura relation for genus 2. Evidence includes the verification for all abelian surfaces of conductor up to 1000 (Poor–Yuen, 2015) and the proof of the conjecture for abelian surfaces with real multiplication by a quadratic field (Johnson–Leung–Roberts, 2019). The paramodular conjecture is the genus‑2 analogue of the modularity theorem for elliptic curves; its full proof would establish the Langlands correspondence for GSp(4) over ℚ for the discrete series representations of weight 2. Recent work by Boxer–Calegari–Gee–Pilloni (2021) on potential automorphy for symmetric powers of Galois representations provides a framework that may eventually yield a proof for a density‑one set of abelian surfaces. Computationally, the space of paramodular forms of level N can be accessed via the Hecke action on the Brandt module of quaternionic orders, and the Fourier coefficients are computed using the Siegel–Eichler mass formula.

  • Confusing the Koecher principle (holomorphy at infinity for genus ≥ 2) with the valence formula for genus 1; the latter does not hold for Siegel modular forms.
  • Assuming the Saito–Kurokawa lift produces all Siegel cusp forms of genus 2; its image is exactly the Maass subspace, a proper subspace when k > 2.
  • Misidentifying the spinor L‑function with the standard L‑function; the spinor L‑function is degree 4, the standard L‑function is degree 5.
  • Thinking the theta correspondence always yields holomorphic forms; the Weil representation can produce non‑holomorphic (Maass) forms unless the dual pair is in the stable range.
  • Believing the paramodular conjecture is proved for all abelian surfaces; it is verified only for conductors up to a few thousand and for special families.
  • Overlooking the role of the Satake parameters in the functional equation of the spinor L‑function; the functional equation relates s to 2k−3−s, not to k−s.
  • Confusing the Fourier–Jacobi expansion with the ordinary Fourier expansion; the former expands in the variable τ' and yields Jacobi forms, not elliptic modular forms.

These advanced topics illustrate how the analytic, arithmetic, and representation‑theoretic facets of Siegel modular forms intertwine, providing a rich source of problems for competitive examinations.

The Maass Lift and its Relation to the Saito–Kurokawa Lift

The Maass lift, introduced by Maass in 1979, constructs Siegel modular forms of genus two from elliptic modular forms of half‑integral weight. Let f be a Hecke eigenform of weight k − ½ on the metaplectic double cover of SL₂(ℤ) with Fourier expansion f(τ) = ∑n≥0 c(n) qⁿ. The Maass lift produces a Siegel modular form F of weight k on Sp₄(ℤ) whose Fourier coefficients are given by a convolution of the coefficients c(n) with representation numbers of binary quadratic forms. Explicitly, for a positive definite half‑integral matrix T = begin{pmatrix} a & b/2 b/2 & c end{pmatrix} the coefficient a(T) equals ∑d|gcd(a,b,c) dk‑1 c(ac − b²/4d²). This formula shows that the Maass lift lands inside the Maass subspace, the same subspace that the Saito–Kurokawa lift fills when the input is an integral weight elliptic cusp form of weight 2k − 2. The two lifts are related by the Shimura correspondence: a half‑integral weight eigenform f corresponds to an integral weight eigenform g of weight 2k − 2, and the Saito–Kurokawa lift of g coincides with the Maass lift of f. Consequently, the Maass subspace is precisely the image of the Saito–Kurokawa lift, and its dimension equals the dimension of the space of elliptic cusp forms of weight 2k − 2. The Maass lift is holomorphic because the input half‑integral weight form is holomorphic and the convolution preserves holomorphy; the Koecher principle then guarantees holomorphy at the boundary for genus two. This construction is a cornerstone for understanding the endoscopic classification of automorphic representations of GSp₄, where the Maass subspace corresponds to the non‑tempered CAP (cuspidal associated to a parabolic) representations attached to the Siegel parabolic.

The Ikeda Lift and Higher Genus Siegel Modular Forms

The Ikeda lift, proved by Ikeda in 2001, generalises the Saito–Kurokawa construction to arbitrary even genus g = 2m. Starting from a normalized Hecke eigenform f of weight 2k − 2 on SL₂(ℤ), the Ikeda lift produces a Siegel cusp form F of weight k on Sp₂ₘ(ℤ) whose spinor L‑function factors as a product of shifted L‑functions of f: L(F, s, spin) = ∏j=1⁽ᵐ⁾ L(f, s + k − j). The lift is realised via the theta correspondence for the dual pair (Sp₂ₘ, O(2m, 2m)) and can also be described by an explicit integral representation using the pullback of a Siegel Eisenstein series on a larger symplectic group. The Fourier coefficients of the Ikeda lift are given by a generalised Maass formula involving representation numbers of quadratic forms of rank 2m. When m = 1 the Ikeda lift reduces to the Saito–Kurokawa lift; for m > 1 the image is a proper subspace of the space of Siegel cusp forms of genus 2m, often called the Ikeda subspace. The Ikeda lift is compatible with the action of Hecke operators: the Hecke eigenvalues of F are symmetric polynomials in the Satake parameters of f. This compatibility is essential for the proof of the functorial transfer from GL₂ to GSp₂ₘ predicted by the Langlands program. Moreover, the Ikeda lift provides explicit examples of non‑tempered CAP representations of GSp₂ₘ attached to the Siegel parabolic, and its image is characterised by the vanishing of certain Fourier–Jacobi coefficients. The existence of the Ikeda lift for all even genera demonstrates that the endoscopic classification of GSp₂ₘ contains an infinite family of CAP representations parametrised by elliptic modular forms.

Worked Example: Computing the Spinor L‑Function of an Ikeda Lift
Let f be the unique normalized cusp form of weight 12 on SL₂(ℤ) (the Ramanujan Δ‑function). Its Hecke eigenvalues satisfy a(p) = τ(p). Construct the Ikeda lift of genus 4 (m = 2) and weight k = 7. Write down the factorisation of the spinor L‑function L(F, s, spin) in terms of L(f, s).
For m = 2 the Ikeda lift yields a Siegel cusp form F of genus 4 and weight 7. By the Ikeda formula, L(F, s, spin) = L(f, s + k − 1) · L(f, s + k − 2) = L(f, s + 6) · L(f, s + 5). Since L(f, s) = ∑ τ(n) n⁻ˢ, the spinor L‑function is the product of two shifted copies of the Ramanujan L‑function. This factorisation shows that the spinor L‑function has degree 8 (2 × 4) and its functional equation relates s to 2k − 3 − s = 11 − s.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the spinor L‑function of an Ikeda lift, first identify the genus 2m and the weight k of the lift. Then write the product ∏j=1⁽ᵐ⁾ L(f, s + k − j). Remember that the degree of the spinor L‑function is 2m × 2 = 4m, and the functional equation centre is at s = k − 3/2. Memorise the shift pattern (k − 1, k − 2, …, k − m) to avoid sign errors.

The Böcherer Conjecture on Central Critical L‑Values

The Böcherer conjecture, formulated by Böcherer in 1985, predicts a precise relationship between the central critical value of the spinor L‑function of a Siegel cusp form of genus two and the square of a certain period integral involving a theta lift. Let F be a Hecke eigenform of weight k on Sp₄(ℤ) with Fourier coefficients a(T). For a fundamental discriminant D < 0 such that (−1)kD > 0, consider the twisted spinor L‑function L(F, s, spin, χD) where χD is the quadratic character of ℚ(√D). The conjecture asserts that the central value L(F, k − 1, spin, χD) is, up to an explicit non‑zero algebraic factor, the square of the Petersson inner product of F with the theta lift of a weight k − ½ modular form attached to the discriminant D. Equivalently, there exists a non‑zero constant C depending only on k and D such that L(F, k − 1, spin, χD) = C · |⟨F, θD⟩|². This conjecture generalises the classical Waldspurger formula for elliptic modular forms to genus two. Partial results include the work of Böcherer and Schulze‑Pillot (1990) proving the conjecture for Saito–Kurokawa lifts, and the proof by Ichino (2008) for generic forms under certain local conditions using the relative trace formula. The conjecture is a special case of the Gan–Gross–Prasad conjecture for the pair (GSp₄, GL₂) and is intimately connected with the non‑vanishing of central L‑values and the existence of non‑zero Fourier coefficients of half‑integral weight forms. In the context of the paramodular conjecture, the Böcherer formula would provide a bridge between the spinor L‑function of a paramodular form and the arithmetic of the associated abelian surface.

Siegel Modular Varieties, Toroidal Compactifications, and the Geometry of the Boundary

The Siegel modular variety of genus g and level N is the quotient A₍g,N₎ = Sp₂g(ℤ/Nℤ)\ℌ₍g₎, where ℌ₍g₎ is the Siegel upper half‑space. For g ≥ 2 the variety is non‑compact; its compactification is essential for defining cohomology groups that carry Galois representations. The minimal (Baily–Borel) compactification adds a single point for each rational boundary component, yielding a normal projective variety with finite quotient singularities. The toroidal compactifications, constructed by Mumford et al. using the theory of toric varieties, resolve these singularities by replacing each boundary component with a divisor whose combinatorics is governed by a rational polyhedral cone decomposition of the positive definite cone. Different choices of cone decompositions give birationally equivalent smooth compactifications; the most common is the second Voronoi decomposition, which yields the Igusa compactification for g = 2. The boundary strata of a toroidal compactification are themselves Siegel modular varieties of lower genus, reflecting the recursive structure of the Siegel parabolic subgroups. The geometry of the boundary controls the behaviour of Siegel modular forms at infinity: the Koecher principle is equivalent to the fact that a holomorphic form on ℌ₍g₎ extends holomorphically across the boundary divisors of any toroidal compactification. Moreover, the Hodge bundle on A₍g,N₎ extends to a vector bundle on the toroidal compactification whose Chern classes compute the characteristic numbers of the moduli space of principally polarised abelian varieties. The cohomology of the boundary contributes to the Eisenstein cohomology, which is matched by the residues of Siegel Eisenstein series via the Langlands spectral sequence. Understanding the boundary geometry is therefore indispensable for the study of the cohomological automorphic representations of GSp₂g and for the construction of Galois representations attached to Siegel modular forms.

Endoscopic Classification and Langlands Functoriality for GSp(4)

The endoscopic classification of automorphic representations of GSp₄ over ℚ, completed by Arthur (2013) building on work of Weissauer, Gan–Takeda, and others, organises the discrete spectrum into packets associated to the elliptic endoscopic groups GL₂ × GL₂/ GL₁ (the split endoscopic group) and GL₂(𝔻) (the non‑split endoscopic group, where 𝔻 is a quaternion algebra). A cuspidal automorphic representation π of GSp₄(𝔸) is either generic (its standard L‑function has a pole at s = 1) or non‑generic (CAP). The generic packets correspond to functorial lifts from cuspidal representations of GL₄ with symplectic type, while the non‑generic packets arise from the Saito–Kurokawa lift (Siegel parabolic), the Yoshida lift (Klingen parabolic), and the Howe–Piatetski‑Shapiro lift (Borel parabolic). The classification implies that every cuspidal representation of GSp₄ is a functorial transfer from either GL₄ or an endoscopic group, confirming the local Langlands correspondence for GSp₄. The stable trace formula for GSp₄ matches orbital integrals on the group with those on its endoscopic groups; the fundamental lemma for the unit elements, proved by Ngô (2010) for all reductive groups, is a key input. As a consequence, the spinor L‑function of a generic Siegel cusp form satisfies the expected functional equation and is entire, while the standard L‑function of a CAP representation factorises as a product of L‑functions of GL₂ representations. This classification also yields a precise description of the image of the Saito–Kurokawa lift as the set of non‑generic representations whose local components are holomorphic discrete series at infinity and unramified principal series at almost all finite places. The endoscopic classification thus provides the representation‑theoretic framework that underlies all the explicit lifts discussed in earlier sections.

  • Confusing the Maass lift (half‑integral weight input) with the Saito–Kurokawa lift (integral weight input); they land in the same subspace but start from different objects.
  • Assuming the Ikeda lift exists for odd genus; it is defined only for even genus 2m.
  • Misstating the degree of the spinor L‑function of an Ikeda lift; it is 4m, not 2m.
  • Believing the Böcherer conjecture is proved for all Siegel cusp forms; it is known only for Saito–Kurokawa lifts and certain generic cases.
  • Thinking the minimal compactification of A₍g,N₎ is smooth for g ≥ 2; it has finite quotient singularities resolved only by toroidal compactifications.
  • Equating the standard L‑function of GSp₄ with the spinor L‑function; the standard L‑function has degree 5, the spinor L‑function degree 4.
  • Overlooking that the endoscopic classification separates generic and CAP representations; the Saito–Kurokawa lift produces CAP, not generic, representations.

Hecke Operators and Eigenforms on Siegel Modular Forms

The Hecke algebra for the symplectic group Sp(2g,ℤ) is generated by double cosets of the form Sp(2g,ℤ) diag(p,…,p,1,…,1) Sp(2g,ℤ) where the diagonal matrix has g entries equal to a prime p and g entries equal to 1. For each prime p there are g fundamental Hecke operators T(p), T₁(p²), …, Tg(pg) corresponding to the elementary symmetric polynomials in the Satake parameters. Their action on Fourier coefficients is explicit: if F(Z)=∑T≥0 a(T) e2πi tr(TZ) then (T(p)F)(Z)=∑ a(pT) e2πi tr(TZ) + pk−g−1 ∑ a(T/p) e2πi tr(TZ) with the convention that a(T/p)=0 unless p divides all entries of T. A Siegel modular form which is a simultaneous eigenfunction for all Hecke operators is called a Hecke eigenform. The eigenvalues λ(p), λ₁(p²), …, λg(pg) determine the local Satake parameters α₁(p), …, αg(p) via the relations λ(p)=pk−g−1∑ αi(p), λ₁(p²)=p2k−2g−2∑i<j</sub> αi(p)αj(p), …, λg(pg)=pgk−g(g+1)/2∏ αi(p). The spinor L‑function of a Hecke eigenform is then L(s,F,spin)=∏p ∏i=1g (1−αi(p)p−s)−1. For genus g=2 the Hecke algebra is commutative and generated by T(p) and T₁(p²); the eigenvalues satisfy the Ramanujan–Petersson bound |αi(p)|=p(k−3/2) for generic forms, a theorem of Weissauer. The existence of a basis of Hecke eigenforms in each space Mk(Sp(4,ℤ)) follows from the commutativity of the Hecke algebra and the finite‑dimensionality of the space. The eigenvalues are algebraic integers and generate a number field called the Hecke field of the eigenform. The Galois conjugates of the eigenvalues correspond to the other eigenforms in the same Galois orbit. This structure is essential for the construction of p‑adic families and for the proof of the endoscopic classification.

The Ring of Siegel Modular Forms of Genus Two and Igusa Invariants

For genus g=2 the graded ring of Siegel modular forms M•(Sp(4,ℤ)) = ⊕k even Mk(Sp(4,ℤ)) is a polynomial ring in four algebraically independent generators, a classical result of Igusa (1962). The generators can be chosen as the Eisenstein series E₄, E₆, χ₁₀, χ₁₂ of weights 4, 6, 10, and 12 respectively. The forms E₄ and E₆ are the Siegel Eisenstein series of those weights; χ₁₀ is the unique cusp form of weight 10 (the Igusa cusp form) and χ₁₂ is a cusp form of weight 12. Every Siegel modular form of even weight k can be written uniquely as a polynomial in these four generators. The dimension formula for Mk(Sp(4,ℤ)) follows from the Hilbert series of this polynomial ring: dim Mk = ⌊k/4⌋⌊k/6⌋⌊k/10⌋⌊k/12⌋ for even k≥0, with the convention that the dimension is zero for odd k. The cusp form χ₁₀ vanishes exactly on the locus of reducible principally polarised abelian surfaces (the product of two elliptic curves), and its divisor is the Humbert surface of discriminant 1. The form χ₁₂ vanishes on the locus of abelian surfaces with extra automorphisms. The Igusa invariants j₁, j₂, j₃, defined as rational functions in the generators, give a birational model of the moduli space A₂ = ℌ₂/Sp(4,ℤ). Explicitly, j₁ = 2⁵·3²·E₄³/χ₁₀, j₂ = 2³·3·E₄E₆/χ₁₀, j₃ = 2²·E₄²E₆/χ₁₀. These invariants satisfy a single algebraic relation, showing that A₂ is a rational threefold. The ring structure also controls the behaviour of Siegel modular forms under the action of the Hecke operators: the Hecke algebra acts by polynomial maps on the generators, and the eigenvalues of Hecke eigenforms can be read off from the action on the polynomial expressions. The Igusa ring is the starting point for the geometric study of the moduli space of abelian surfaces, for the computation of Siegel modular forms via theta series, and for the construction of the paramodular forms that appear in the paramodular conjecture.

Worked Example: Dimension of Weight 24 Siegel Modular Forms of Genus 2
Compute the dimension of the space M24(Sp(4,ℤ)) of Siegel modular forms of weight 24 and genus 2.
The graded ring M•(Sp(4,ℤ)) is generated by E₄ (weight 4), E₆ (weight 6), χ₁₀ (weight 10), χ₁₂ (weight 12). A basis of weight 24 consists of all monomials E₄a E₆b χ₁₀c χ₁₂d with 4a+6b+10c+12d = 24, a,b,c,d ≥ 0 integers. Enumerate solutions: (a,b,c,d) = (6,0,0,0), (3,2,0,0), (0,4,0,0), (4,0,1,0), (1,2,1,0), (2,0,0,1), (0,1,0,1) with c=1,d=0 gives weight 10, need 14 more: (a,b) = (1,? ) 4a+6b=14 → a=2,b=1 gives 8+6=14, so (2,1,1,0); (a,b) = (5,? ) 20+? no. Also c=0,d=1 gives weight 12, need 12 more: (a,b) = (3,0), (0,2). Also c=2 gives weight 20, need 4: (a,b)=(1,0). Also c=1,d=1 gives weight 22, need 2 impossible. Also d=2 gives weight 24: (0,0,0,2). Counting distinct monomials: (6,0,0,0), (3,2,0,0), (0,4,0,0), (4,0,1,0), (1,2,1,0), (2,1,1,0), (2,0,0,1), (0,2,0,1), (1,0,2,0), (0,0,0,2). That is 10 monomials. Hence dim M24 = 10.
Exam‑Day Strategy
Memorise the four Igusa generator weights (4, 6, 10, 12). Any dimension question for genus 2 reduces to counting non‑negative integer solutions of 4a+6b+10c+12d = k. Use generating functions or a quick manual enumeration for small k; for larger k the Hilbert series formula ⌊k/4⌋⌊k/6⌋⌊k/10⌋⌊k/12⌋ gives the answer directly.

The Pullback Formula for Siegel Eisenstein Series and Rankin–Selberg Integrals

The Siegel Eisenstein series of genus g and weight k is defined by Ek(g)(Z) = ∑γ∈P(ℤ)Sp(2g,ℤ) (det(CZ+D))−k where P is the Siegel parabolic. A fundamental tool is the pullback of Ek(g) to the diagonal embedding ℌg₁ × ℌg₂ → ℌg with g₁+g₂ = g. The restriction yields a product of lower‑genus Eisenstein series multiplied by a certain gamma factor. For example, the pullback of the genus 2 Eisenstein series Ek(2) to the diagonal Z = diag(τ₁, τ₂) gives Ek(2)(diag(τ₁,τ₂)) = ζ(2k−2) Ek(1)(τ₁) Ek(1)(τ₂) where ζ is the Riemann zeta function and Ek(1) is the classical elliptic Eisenstein series. More generally, the pullback to a block‑diagonal matrix with blocks of sizes g₁ and g₂ produces a product of Eisenstein series of genera g₁ and g₂ times a rational function in the gamma factors. This formula is the starting point for the Rankin–Selberg integral representation of the spinor L‑function. For a Hecke eigenform F ∈ Sk(Sp(4,ℤ)) one considers the integral ∫Sp(4,ℤ)\ℌ₂ F(Z) Es(2)(Z) (det Y)k dμ(Z) where Es(2) is the non‑holomorphic Eisenstein series of genus 2 and complex parameter s. Unfolding the integral against the Fourier expansion of F and using the pullback formula for the constant term of Es(2) yields the spinor L‑function L(s+½, F, spin) multiplied by a product of gamma functions. The analytic continuation and functional equation of the Eisenstein series then imply the corresponding properties for the spinor L‑function. This method, pioneered by Andrianov and later refined by Piatetski‑Shapiro and Rallis, works for any genus and provides the standard proof that the spinor L‑function of a Siegel cusp form satisfies a functional equation of the expected type. The same pullback technique also gives integral representations for the standard L‑function (degree 5) and for the twisted L‑functions by Dirichlet characters. Understanding the precise normalisation of the Eisenstein series and the measure dμ(Z) = (det Y)−g−1 dX dY is essential for correct gamma factors.

p‑adic Families of Siegel Modular Forms and Hida Theory for GSp(4)

Hida theory constructs p‑adic analytic families of ordinary Hecke eigenforms for GL₂ and, more generally, for reductive groups with a suitable ordinary projector. For GSp(4) the theory was developed by Hida (1994), Tilouine–Urban (1999), and later by Pilloni–Stroh (2016) using the geometry of the Siegel modular variety. An ordinary Siegel modular form of weight k and level N (prime to p) is one whose Up‑operator eigenvalue is a p‑adic unit. The ordinary projector e = limn→∞ Upn! isolates the ordinary part of the space of p‑adic Siegel modular forms. Hida proved that the ordinary Hecke algebra 𝔥ord is a finite flat algebra over the Iwasawa algebra Λ = ℤp[[ℤp×]] and that the universal ordinary Hecke eigenform gives a p‑adic family F(κ) = ∑ aκ(T) qT where the weight κ varies continuously in the weight space Homcont(ℤp×, ℂp×). The specialisation at a classical weight k yields a classical ordinary Siegel eigenform of weight k. The characteristic power series of Up on the ordinary part has coefficients in Λ and its Newton polygon controls the slopes of the Up eigenvalues. A key result is the control theorem: the specialisation map 𝔥ord → 𝔥kord is surjective for all sufficiently large k, and the kernel is generated by the difference between the weight character and the trivial character. This allows one to interpolate L‑values: the p‑adic L‑function Lp(s, F) attached to a Hida family interpolates the critical values of the complex spinor L‑function L(s, Fk, spin) as k varies. The existence of such p‑adic L‑functions for GSp(4) was proved by Böcherer–Schmidt and later by Liu–Zhang–Zhang using the pullback formula and the theory of p‑adic Eisenstein series. The geometry of the Siegel modular variety enters through the Hodge bundle ω = det(Ω1A/𝒜) on the universal abelian scheme 𝒜 → 𝒜g,N. The ordinary locus is the open subscheme where the Hasse invariant (the determinant of the Frobenius on the de Rham cohomology) is invertible. Over the ordinary locus the Hodge bundle splits into a direct sum of a unit‑root part and a nilpotent part, and the unit‑root part gives rise to the p‑adic modular forms. The Hida family can be viewed as a section of a certain p‑adic Banach sheaf on the ordinary locus, and the Hecke operators act via correspondences on the Siegel modular variety. This geometric perspective is essential for the proof of the Iwasawa main conjecture for GSp(4) and for the construction of Galois representations attached to Siegel modular forms via the p‑adic Langlands correspondence.

The Hodge Bundle, Kodaira–Spencer Map, and Geometric Siegel Modular Forms

On the Siegel modular variety 𝒜g,N = ℌg/Γg(N) (with Γg(N) a principal congruence subgroup) there is a universal principally polarised abelian scheme π: 𝒜 → 𝒜g,N. The relative cotangent bundle Ω1𝒜/𝒜g,N is a locally free sheaf of rank g. Its determinant ω = det Ω1𝒜/𝒜g,N is called the Hodge bundle. A Siegel modular form of weight k and level N can be identified with a global section of the line bundle ω⊗k on 𝒜g,N. The Kodaira–Spencer map is the natural morphism KS: T𝒜g,N → Sym²(π*Ω1𝒜/𝒜g,N) induced by the variation of Hodge structure. In terms of the Siegel upper half‑space, the tangent space at Z ∈ ℌg is Sym²(ℂg) and the Kodaira–Spencer map is the identity. The Hodge bundle extends to a vector bundle on any toroidal compactification 𝒜g,Ntor; its Chern classes compute the characteristic numbers of the moduli space of abelian varieties. For example, the first Chern class c₁(ω) is the class of the Hodge divisor, which is ample on 𝒜g,N for g ≥ 2. The Kodaira–Spencer map identifies the tangent bundle of 𝒜g,N with Sym²(𝔼) where 𝔼 = π*Ω1𝒜/𝒜g,N is the Hodge vector bundle. Consequently, the canonical bundle of 𝒜g,N is ω⊗(g+1). This implies that Siegel modular forms of weight g+1 are canonical forms on the modular variety. The geometry of the boundary divisors in a toroidal compactification is governed by the cone decomposition of the positive definite cone; the restriction of ω to a boundary divisor corresponding to a rank‑r boundary component is the Hodge bundle of the lower‑genus Siegel modular variety attached to that component. This recursive structure underlies the induction proofs of the Koecher principle and of the dimension formulas for spaces of Siegel modular forms. Moreover, the Hodge bundle carries a natural integrable connection (the Gauss–Manin connection) whose curvature is expressed in terms of the Kodaira–Spencer map. The Griffiths transversality property of the variation of Hodge structure translates into the fact that the Gauss–Manin connection maps ω into ω ⊗ Ω1𝒜g,N. This geometric framework is the foundation for the algebro‑geometric construction of Siegel modular forms via vector bundles on moduli spaces and for the study of their p‑adic properties via the theory of canonical subgroups.

  • Confusing the Hecke operators T(p) and T₁(p²) for genus 2; they have different weights in the Satake isomorphism.
  • Assuming the Igusa ring generators are algebraically independent over ℤ; they are independent over ℂ but the ring structure over ℤ has torsion in low weights.
  • Misapplying the pullback formula by forgetting the zeta factor ζ(2k−2) when restricting genus 2 Eisenstein series to the diagonal.
  • Thinking every ordinary Siegel eigenform belongs to a Hida family; the family exists only for forms whose Up eigenvalue is a p‑adic unit and whose weight is sufficiently regular.
  • Equating the Hodge bundle ω with the determinant of the Hodge vector bundle 𝔼; ω = det 𝔼, but 𝔼 itself is rank g and its Chern classes give more refined invariants.
  • Believing the Kodaira–Spencer map is an isomorphism on the compactified variety; it extends to a logarithmic map on the toroidal compactification with poles along the boundary divisors.
  • Overlooking that the canonical bundle of 𝒜g,N is ω⊗(g+1) only for the fine moduli scheme (N ≥ 3); for smaller level there are stacky corrections.

Fourier Coefficients of Siegel Modular Forms and Representation Numbers of Quadratic Forms

The Fourier expansion of a Siegel modular form of genus g is indexed by symmetric, semi‑positive definite half‑integral matrices T of size g. For a Hecke eigenform F in the space M_k(Γ_g) the coefficient a(T) carries deep arithmetic information. When g = 2, the coefficient a(T) with T = ( n r/2 ; r/2 m ) is essentially the representation number of the quadratic form Q(x,y) = n x² + r xy + m y² by the lattice attached to the abelian surface. The work of Eichler, Zagier, and later Böcherer shows that for Saito–Kurokawa lifts the Fourier coefficients factor as a product of a Fourier coefficient of the underlying elliptic modular form and a divisor sum, reflecting the fact that the lift comes from a CAP representation. For generic (non‑CAP) eigenforms the coefficients are expected to satisfy the multiplicative relations predicted by the spinor L‑function, and the Saito–Kurokawa conjecture (proved by Maass, Andrianov, and Zagier) identifies the precise set of T for which a(T) vanishes. In higher genus the Fourier coefficients are related to representation numbers of quadratic forms in 2g variables, and the Siegel–Weil formula expresses the average of these coefficients over a genus of lattices as an Eisenstein series coefficient. This connection underlies the proof of the rationality of special values of the spinor L‑function and the construction of p‑adic L‑functions via the pullback formula.

Explicit formulas for the Fourier coefficients of Eisenstein series are given by the Siegel–Eisenstein series E_k(Z) = ∑T≥0 a_E(T) e2πi tr(TZ) where a_E(T) = c_k det(T)k-g-1 ∏_p α_p(T) with α_p(T) the local density of representations of T by the standard lattice. For cusp forms the coefficients are more mysterious; the work of Duke–Imamoglu–Toth and later of A. Pitale–R. Schmidt gives bounds a(T) ≪ det(T)k/2 - 1/4 + ε which are sharp for Saito–Kurokawa lifts. The non‑vanishing of a(T) for fundamental discriminants is tied to the non‑vanishing of central L‑values via the Böcherer conjecture, now a theorem in many cases due to the work of Furusawa–Morimoto and others. In practice, computing a(T) for a given eigenform requires either the explicit Hecke action on Fourier expansions or the use of the pullback formula to restrict to the diagonal and match with elliptic modular forms.

The Fourier–Jacobi expansion provides a bridge between the full Fourier expansion and Jacobi forms. Writing Z = ( τ z ; z' τ' ) with τ, τ' in ℌ1 and z ∈ ℂ, the coefficient of e2πi m τ' is a Jacobi form of weight k and index m. The Fourier coefficients of this Jacobi form are precisely the a(T) with T having lower right entry m. This recursive structure is exploited in the proof of the Koecher principle and in the construction of the Maass lift, where the Jacobi form is a theta series attached to a binary quadratic form. The arithmetic of the coefficients a(T) thus controls both the analytic properties of the spinor L‑function and the geometry of the associated abelian variety.

Half‑Integral Weight Siegel Modular Forms and the Shimura Correspondence

Siegel modular forms of half‑integral weight arise naturally from theta series attached to even unimodular lattices of rank 2g. The metaplectic double cover Mp(2g, ℝ) of Sp(2g, ℝ) acts on the Siegel upper half‑space, and a modular form of weight k+1/2 transforms with the multiplier system given by the Weil representation. For genus one this is the classical theory of Shimura, who established a correspondence between half‑integral weight forms of weight k+1/2 on Γ0(4) and integral weight forms of weight 2k on SL2(ℤ). In higher genus the Shimura correspondence was extended by Shimura himself and later by Ikeda, who constructed a lift from half‑integral weight Siegel modular forms of genus g to integral weight forms of genus 2g. The lift is defined via an integral kernel involving the Siegel theta series of a suitable lattice, and it respects Hecke eigenvalues away from the bad primes. The image of the lift consists of Saito–Kurokawa type forms when g = 1, and of Ikeda lifts for higher g. This provides a geometric interpretation of the CAP representations in terms of theta lifts from orthogonal groups.

The Fourier coefficients of a half‑integral weight Siegel modular form are supported on matrices T with discriminant a square times a fundamental discriminant. The Shimura correspondence maps the coefficient a(T) to a product of a coefficient of the integral weight form and a local factor depending on the discriminant. This is the higher genus analogue of Waldspurger’s formula relating central L‑values to Fourier coefficients of half‑integral weight forms. The non‑vanishing of a(T) for a fundamental discriminant D is equivalent to the non‑vanishing of the central critical value L(1/2, π ⊗ χ_D) where π is the automorphic representation attached to the integral weight form. The work of Böcherer and Schulze‑Pillot gives precise formulas for the local factors and proves the non‑vanishing of the lift for a dense set of discriminants under the generalized Riemann hypothesis. In the p‑adic setting, Hida theory for half‑integral weight forms has been developed by U. Ray and others, allowing the construction of p‑adic L‑functions interpolating the central values L(1/2, π ⊗ χ_D) as D varies p‑adically.

The metaplectic group also plays a role in the theta correspondence between Mp(2g) and orthogonal groups O(V) where V is a quadratic space of dimension 2g+1. The Weil representation on the Schwartz space of V(ℝ) yields a theta kernel whose integral against a cusp form on O(V) produces a Siegel modular form on Mp(2g). This is the Rallis tower property, which controls the first occurrence of a representation in the tower. For g = 2 the correspondence with O(5) ≅ PGSp(4) recovers the Saito–Kurokawa lift, while for g = 3 the correspondence with O(7) gives the Ikeda lift of genus three. The half‑integral weight theory thus unifies the various CAP lifts and provides a framework for the endoscopic classification of representations of GSp(4) and its higher genus analogues.

Worked Example: Computing a Fourier Coefficient of a Genus‑2 Eisenstein Series
Let E4(Z) be the Siegel Eisenstein series of weight 4 and genus 2, normalized so that its constant term is 1. Compute the Fourier coefficient a_E(T) for T = ( 2 1 ; 1 2 ).
The Fourier coefficient of the genus‑2 Eisenstein series of even weight k ≥ 4 is given by a_E(T) = c_k det(T)k-3 ∏_p α_p(T) where c_k = 2 ζ(1-k) ζ(3-2k) / ζ(2-k) ζ(4-2k). For k = 4 we have c4 = 2 ζ(-3) ζ(-5) / ζ(-2) ζ(-4). Using ζ(-3) = 1/120, ζ(-5) = -1/252, ζ(-2) = 0, ζ(-4) = 0, the ratio is evaluated via the functional equation giving c4 = 1/240. The determinant det(T) = 2·2 - 1·1 = 3. The local density α_p(T) for a positive definite binary quadratic form is the p‑adic representation density of T by the standard lattice ℤ². For p ≠ 2,3 the form is unimodular and α_p(T) = 1. For p = 3, the discriminant is 3, so α3(T) = 1 + 1/3 = 4/3. For p = 2, the form has even diagonal entries, giving α2(T) = 1 + 1/2 = 3/2. Thus ∏_p α_p(T) = (4/3)(3/2) = 2. Finally a_E(T) = (1/240) · 34-3 · 2 = (1/240) · 3 · 2 = 6/240 = 1/40.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a Fourier coefficient of an Eisenstein series, first write down the general formula with the zeta‑factor and the product of local densities. Compute the determinant of T and the weight exponent. Then evaluate the local densities only at primes dividing the discriminant of T; at all other primes the density is 1. Use the known values of ζ at negative integers (or the functional equation) to simplify the constant factor. This systematic approach avoids missing the crucial ζ‑factor that appears in the pullback formula.

Vector‑Valued Siegel Modular Forms and Motives

Beyond scalar‑valued forms, one considers Siegel modular forms taking values in a finite‑dimensional representation ρ of GL_g(ℂ). Such vector‑valued forms arise naturally from the cohomology of local systems on the Siegel modular variety. For a dominant weight λ = (λ1 ≥ … ≥ λ_g) the associated irreducible representation V_λ of GL_g gives a local system 𝕍_λ on 𝒜_g,N. The space of vector‑valued Siegel modular forms of weight λ is H0(𝒜_g,N, ω^λ ⊗ 𝕍_λ) where ω^λ is the line bundle attached to the determinant character. The work of Weissauer, van der Geer, and others computes the dimensions of these spaces via the Hirzebruch–Mumford proportionality principle and the theory of invariant differential operators. The eigenvalues of Hecke operators on vector‑valued eigenforms are expected to correspond to the Frobenius traces on the ℓ‑adic realizations of motives attached to the automorphic representation. In particular, for g = 2 and λ = (k1, k2) with k1 > k2 ≥ 3 the associated motive has Hodge numbers (k1-3, k2-2) and (k2-2, k1-3), and its L‑function is the spinor L‑function of the Siegel eigenform. The existence of these motives is known for Saito–Kurokawa lifts (where the motive is a product of an elliptic curve and a Tate twist) and for generic forms under the endoscopic classification of Arthur. The vector‑valued setting also clarifies the role of the Kodaira–Spencer map: the Gauss–Manin connection on the Hodge bundle takes values in Sym²(𝔼) ⊗ Ω¹, and its iterates generate the higher weight vector‑valued forms.

The theory of nearly holomorphic Siegel modular forms, introduced by Shimura, provides a bridge between vector‑valued forms and the arithmetic of special L‑values. A nearly holomorphic form of weight λ is a polynomial in the entries of (Im Z)-1 with coefficients that are holomorphic vector‑valued forms. The differential operators ∂/∂Zij raise the weight by the standard representation, and the Maass–Shimura operators produce the Rankin–Cohen brackets. The algebra of nearly holomorphic forms is finitely generated over the ring of holomorphic forms, and its structure reflects the representation theory of the Lie algebra sp(2g). In the p‑adic setting, the theory of overconvergent vector‑valued forms developed by Pilloni and Stroh gives a geometric construction of p‑adic families of automorphic forms on GSp(2g) and relates them to the eigenvariety of Buzzard–Chenevier. This is a key ingredient in the proof of the local‑global compatibility of the Galois representations attached to Siegel modular forms.

Hilbert–Siegel Modular Forms over Totally Real Fields

Let F be a totally real field of degree d over ℚ. The Hilbert–Siegel upper half‑space is ℌ_g^d = ℌ_g × … × ℌ_g (d copies), and the Hilbert–Siegel modular group is Sp(2g, 𝒪_F) where 𝒪_F is the ring of integers. A Hilbert–Siegel modular form of parallel weight k is a holomorphic function on ℌ_g^d satisfying the transformation law under Sp(2g, 𝒪_F) and a suitable growth condition at the cusps. The Fourier expansion is indexed by totally positive semi‑definite half‑integral matrices T ∈ Sym_g(F) with tr(T) ≫ 0. The theory of Eisenstein series, Hecke operators, and the Koecher principle extends verbatim, with the zeta‑functions of F replacing the Riemann zeta function. The Satake isomorphism identifies the Hecke algebra at a prime 𝔭 of F with the representation ring of the dual group GSp(2g, ℂ), and the Satake parameters are d‑tuples of the usual genus‑g parameters. The spinor L‑function of a Hilbert–Siegel eigenform is a product over the d embeddings of the local spinor L‑functions, and it satisfies a functional equation relating s to 2g+1 - s.

The Saito–Kurokawa lift has a Hilbert analogue: starting from a Hilbert modular form f of parallel weight 2k-2 on GL2(F) one constructs a genus‑2 Hilbert–Siegel modular form of weight k whose spinor L‑function is ζ_F(s) ζ_F(s-k+1) L(s, f). The proof uses the theta correspondence between GSp(4) and the metaplectic cover of SL2 over F, developed by Shimura and later by Ikeda. The Ikeda lift also generalizes, producing Hilbert–Siegel forms of genus 2m from a Hilbert modular form of weight 2k-2. These lifts are crucial for the endoscopic classification of automorphic representations of GSp(4) over F, which was completed by Mok and later by Kaletha–Minguez–Shin–White. The paramodular conjecture for abelian surfaces over F predicts that the L‑function of an abelian surface with real multiplication by 𝒪_F matches the spinor L‑function of a paramodular Hilbert–Siegel eigenform. This has been verified in many cases by the work of Brumer–Kramer and others using the Faltings–Serre method.

In the p‑adic setting, Hida theory for Hilbert–Siegel modular forms was developed by Hida himself and later by Tilouine–Urban. The ordinary locus in the Hilbert–Siegel modular variety is defined by the non‑vanishing of the Hilbert–Hasse invariant, which is the product of the Hasse invariants at each embedding. Over the ordinary locus the Hodge bundle splits into a direct sum of unit‑root subbundles indexed by the embeddings, and the Hecke operators U_𝔭 act via correspondences. The resulting Hida families are parameterized by a rigid analytic space of dimension d+1, and the associated p‑adic L‑functions interpolate the critical values of the spinor L‑function twisted by finite order characters of the narrow class group of F. These p‑adic L‑functions are essential for the formulation of the Iwasawa main conjecture for GSp(4) over totally real fields, which is now a theorem in many cases due to the work of Liu–Zhang–Zhang and others.

Congruences, p‑adic L‑Functions, and the Bloch–Kato Conjecture for GSp(4)

Congruences between Siegel modular forms provide a powerful tool for relating special values of L‑functions to arithmetic invariants such as Tamagawa numbers and Selmer groups. If two Hecke eigenforms F and G of genus 2 are congruent modulo a prime ℓ, then their spinor L‑values satisfy a congruence modulo ℓ after dividing by appropriate periods. This is the higher genus analogue of the Ribet–Mazur–Wiles congruences for elliptic modular forms. The work of Böcherer–Dummigan and later of Katsurada–Kawamura gives explicit formulas for the congruence of critical L‑values in terms of the Fourier coefficients of the forms. The Bloch–Kato conjecture predicts that the order of the Selmer group of the motive attached to F is given by the algebraic part of the critical L‑value L(k, F) divided by the product of Tamagawa factors. For Saito–Kurokawa lifts the motive is a product of an elliptic curve E and a Tate twist, and the conjecture reduces to the Birch–Swinnerton‑Dyer conjecture for E, which is known in many cases. For generic forms the conjecture is open, but the existence of p‑adic L‑functions constructed via the pullback formula and the theory of p‑adic Eisenstein series provides a framework for proving one divisibility in the Iwasawa main conjecture.

The p‑adic L‑function L_p(F, s) for a genus‑2 ordinary eigenform F is a measure on the weight space ℤ_p^× × ℤ_p^× interpolating the algebraic parts of the critical values L(k, F) as the weight k varies in a Hida family. The construction uses the pullback of the genus‑2 p‑adic Eisenstein series to the diagonal, which yields a product of two genus‑1 p‑adic Eisenstein series. The resulting p‑adic L‑function satisfies a functional equation relating s to 2g+1 - s, and its zeros are conjectured to correspond to the trivial zeros of the complex L‑function. The work of Liu–Zhang–Zhang proves the non‑vanishing of L_p(F, s) at the central point for a dense set of forms, which implies the finiteness of the Selmer group under the Bloch–Kato conjecture. The congruence ideal of a Hida family measures the failure of the family to be a complete intersection, and its characteristic power series is expected to equal the p‑adic L‑function up to a unit. This is the main conjecture of Iwasawa theory for GSp(4), proved in the ordinary case by Skinner–Urban for GL2 and extended to GSp(4) by recent work of Wan and others using the Euler system of Beilinson–Flach elements on the Siegel modular variety.

The geometry of the boundary of the toroidal compactification plays a crucial role in the construction of the Euler system. The restriction of the Hodge bundle to a boundary divisor corresponding to a rank‑1 cusp is the Hodge bundle of the elliptic modular curve, and the Siegel Eisenstein series restricts to a product of elliptic Eisenstein series. The Beilinson–Flach classes are constructed from the pullback of the universal abelian scheme to the product of two copies of the Siegel threefold, and their norm compatibility follows from the compatibility of the pullback formula with the Hecke correspondences. The resulting Euler system bounds the Selmer group from above, while the p‑adic L‑function provides the lower bound via the explicit reciprocity law. The equality of the two bounds is the Iwasawa main conjecture, which is now a theorem for ordinary Siegel modular forms of genus 2 with regular weight. This completes the circle from the analytic theory of Siegel modular forms to the arithmetic of their associated motives.

  • Confusing the weight of a vector‑valued Siegel modular form with the highest weight of the representation; the weight is a dominant weight λ = (λ1,…,λ_g) and the scalar weight k corresponds to λ = (k,…,k).
  • Assuming the Shimura correspondence for half‑integral weight forms preserves the level exactly; the level typically multiplies by 4 and the character changes by the quadratic character of the discriminant.
  • Overlooking that the Hilbert–Siegel modular group is Sp(2g, 𝒪_F) not Sp(2g, ℤ) and that the Fourier expansion runs over totally positive matrices, not just positive definite ones over ℚ.
  • Misapplying the pullback formula for p‑adic L‑functions by forgetting the Euler factor at p that appears when restricting the genus‑2 Eisenstein series to the diagonal.
  • Equating the Bloch–Kato Selmer group with the classical Selmer group of an abelian variety; for higher genus motives the Selmer group is defined via Galois cohomology of the p‑adic realization of the motive, which may have dimension larger than 2g.
  • Believing that every congruence between Siegel eigenforms lifts to a congruence of the associated Galois representations; the congruence must be compatible with the local Langlands correspondence at all primes, which fails for non‑ordinary primes.

The Rallis Inner Product Formula and Theta Lifts for GSp(4)

The theta correspondence attaches to a pair of reductive groups forming a dual pair in a symplectic group a lifting of automorphic representations. For GSp(4) the relevant dual pairs are (GSp(4), O(V)) where V is a quadratic space of dimension m. The Rallis inner product formula, proved in full generality by Rallis and later refined by Kudla–Rallis and by Gan–Takeda, expresses the Petersson inner product of a theta lift θ(π) on GSp(4) in terms of the central value of the standard L‑function L(s, π, Std) multiplied by a product of local zeta integrals. Concretely, if π is a cuspidal automorphic representation of O(V)(𝔸) and θ(π) its (non‑zero) theta lift to GSp(4)(𝔸), then

⟨θ(φ), θ(φ)⟩ = C · L(½, π, Std) · ∏_v Z_v(φ_v)

where C is an explicit non‑zero constant, φ is a factorizable vector in π, and Z_v are the local doubling zeta integrals. The formula has two immediate consequences: it gives a criterion for the non‑vanishing of the theta lift (the central L‑value must be non‑zero) and it relates the norm of the lift to the special L‑value, which is the key input for the Böcherer conjecture on central critical values of spinor L‑functions for Siegel modular forms. In the case where V is a four‑dimensional quadratic space of discriminant 1, the theta lift lands in the space of Saito–Kurokawa lifts, and the Rallis formula recovers the classical formula of Maass for the Petersson norm of the lift. For higher dimensional V the lift produces generic Siegel modular forms, and the formula has been used by Ichino–Ikeda to prove cases of the Gan–Gross–Prasad conjecture for GSp(4). The local zeta integrals Z_v are computed by the doubling method of Piatetski‑Shapiro–Rallis; at unramified places they give the local standard L‑factor, while at ramified places they involve the local Langlands parameter of π_v. The global formula requires a careful choice of the Schwartz function to ensure convergence of the theta integral; this is achieved by the regularized theta lift of Kudla–Rallis. The Rallis inner product formula is also the starting point for the construction of p‑adic L‑functions for GSp(4) via the pullback of the Siegel Eisenstein series to the diagonal, because the Eisenstein series on the doubling group interpolates the local zeta integrals.

The Gan–Gross–Prasad Conjecture for GSp(4) and Period Integrals

The Gan–Gross–Prasad (GGP) conjecture predicts a precise relationship between the non‑vanishing of certain period integrals of automorphic forms on a group G and the central value of an associated L‑function for a representation of a subgroup H. For GSp(4) the relevant pair is (GSp(4), GSp(2) × GSp(2)) embedded via the diagonal block embedding, or equivalently (SO(5), SO(4)) after passing to the derived groups. The period integral in question is the Bessel period: for a cuspidal automorphic representation π of GSp(4)(𝔸) with a fixed character Λ of the torus T(𝔸) (the Bessel character), one integrates the automorphic form against Λ over T(ℚ)T(𝔸). The GGP conjecture asserts that this period is non‑zero for some choice of data if and only if the central value L(½, π, Std ⊗ χ) is non‑zero, where χ is the quadratic character attached to the Bessel model. The conjecture was formulated by Gan, Gross and Prasad in 2012 building on earlier work of Gross–Prasad for orthogonal groups. For GSp(4) the proof has been completed in a series of works: the case of globally generic representations was settled by Wei Zhang (2014) using the relative trace formula and the Rallis inner product formula for the dual pair (GSp(4), GO(4)). The non‑generic (Saito–Kurokawa) case reduces to the Waldspurger formula for GL(2) and was handled by Liu (2016). The local GGP conjecture, which describes the dimension of the space of H‑invariant linear forms on an irreducible representation π_v, was proved by Gan–Takeda (2016) using the local Langlands correspondence for GSp(4) and the theory of epsilon factors. The global conjecture then follows from the local–global compatibility and the non‑vanishing of the central L‑value. The Bessel period also appears in the pullback formula for Siegel Eisenstein series: the restriction of a genus‑2 Eisenstein series to the diagonal yields a product of two genus‑1 Eisenstein series, and the Fourier coefficients of this restriction are exactly the Bessel periods of the Siegel modular form. This connection underlies the construction of p‑adic L‑functions for GSp(4) via the Eisenstein measure of Hida and the work of Liu–Zhang–Zhang on non‑vanishing modulo p.

Worked Example: Bessel Period and Central L‑Value
Let F be a genus‑2 Siegel cuspidal Hecke eigenform of weight k, level 1, which is a Saito–Kurokawa lift of an elliptic cusp form f of weight 2k‑2. Let T be the standard split torus in GSp(4) and Λ the trivial character. Compute the Bessel period P_Λ(F) = ∫T(ℚ)T(𝔸) F(t) dt and relate it to the central value L(k, f).
For a Saito–Kurokawa lift, the Bessel period with trivial character factorises as a product of the Petersson norm of f and an explicit Euler factor. Using the pullback formula for the genus‑2 Eisenstein series E2(Z, s) restricted to the diagonal Z = diag(τ₁, τ₂), one obtains E2(diag(τ₁, τ₂), s) = E1(τ₁, s) E1(τ₂, s) × (Euler factor at ∞). The Fourier coefficient of E2 along the unipotent radical of the Siegel parabolic gives the Bessel period of F. By the Rankin–Selberg method, ∫SL₂(ℤ)\ℌ f(τ) E1(τ, s) dμ(τ) = L(s, f) / ζ(2s). Evaluating at s = k and using the functional equation of the Eisenstein series yields P_Λ(F) = C · ⟨f, f⟩ · L(k, f) / ζ(2k) where C is an explicit non‑zero constant depending only on k. Since f is a Hecke eigenform, L(k, f) is the central critical value (the functional equation relates s to 2k‑1‑s, so s = k is central). Thus the Bessel period is non‑zero exactly when L(k, f) ≠ 0, confirming the GGP prediction for this non‑generic case.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for the relation between a period integral and an L‑value, identify the dual pair or the subgroup involved. For GSp(4) the Bessel period corresponds to the GGP conjecture with H = GSp(2)×GSp(2). Remember that the Saito–Kurokawa lift reduces the problem to the Waldspurger formula on GL(2). Write down the pullback of the genus‑2 Eisenstein series to the diagonal; this is the standard technique to produce the period–L‑value formula.

The Schottky Problem and Siegel Modular Forms of Genus Three

The Schottky problem asks for a characterization of the Jacobian locus 𝒥_g inside the moduli space 𝒜_g of principally polarized abelian varieties of dimension g. For g = 3 the locus 𝒥3 is a proper subvariety of 𝒜3 of codimension 1, and its defining equation can be expressed in terms of Siegel modular forms. The classical approach uses the Schottky–Jung relations, which are identities among theta constants with characteristics. Igusa (1981) constructed a cusp form of weight 8 on the Siegel upper half‑space ℌ3, now called the Schottky form J3, whose zero divisor is exactly the closure of the Jacobian locus in the Satake compactification. The form J3 is given by a polynomial of degree 16 in the even theta constants with characteristic, and its Fourier expansion begins with a term supported on the rank‑1 semi‑abelian varieties corresponding to the boundary. Freitag (1983) proved that the ring of Siegel modular forms of genus 3 is generated by the 34 even theta constants of weight 1/2 (which are not modular forms on the full group but on a level subgroup) together with J3. The vanishing of J3 on a point Z ∈ ℌ3 is equivalent to the existence of a non‑trivial solution to the Schottky–Jung relations, which in turn is equivalent to the Riemann matrix Z being the period matrix of a curve of genus 3. The geometry of the boundary of the toroidal compactification plays a crucial role: the restriction of J3 to the boundary divisor corresponding to a rank‑1 cusp is the product of the genus‑2 Schottky form (which is identically zero because every principally polarized abelian surface is a Jacobian) and a certain Eisenstein series. This compatibility with the boundary stratification is what allows the extension of J3 to a section of the Hodge bundle on the toroidal compactification. The Schottky form also appears in the theory of the superstring measure: the genus‑3 superstring amplitude involves an integral over 𝒜3 of a certain combination of theta constants, and the vanishing of the cosmological constant is related to the fact that J3 is a cusp form. Recent work of Grushevsky–Salvati Manni (2015) gave a new proof of the Schottky–Jung relations using the geometry of the universal Kummer variety and the heat equations for theta functions. The problem for g ≥ 4 remains open; the Jacobian locus has higher codimension and is not a divisor, so it cannot be cut out by a single modular form. Instead one seeks a set of equations in the ring of Siegel modular forms, a programme pursued by Salvati Manni, O'Grady, and others using the geometry of the Andreotti–Mayer loci.

Nearly Holomorphic Siegel Modular Forms and Special Values of L‑Functions

Nearly holomorphic Siegel modular forms were introduced by Shimura (1982) as a tool to prove algebraicity results for critical values of L‑functions attached to Siegel modular forms. A function F on ℌ_g is called nearly holomorphic of weight ρ (a rational representation of GL_g(ℂ)) if it can be written as a polynomial in the entries of (Im Z)-1 with coefficients that are holomorphic modular forms of weight ρ ⊗ Sym^m(std). The space of nearly holomorphic forms is stable under the action of the Maass–Shimura differential operators Dρ, ρ', which raise the weight by a symmetric power of the standard representation. For scalar weight k, the operator D_k maps a holomorphic modular form of weight k to a nearly holomorphic form of weight k+2; iterating gives the full algebra of nearly holomorphic forms. The key property is that the Petersson inner product of a nearly holomorphic form with a holomorphic cusp form can be expressed as a special value of the Rankin–Selberg L‑function. Shimura used this to show that for a Hecke eigenform F of genus g, the critical values L(m, F) for integers m in the critical strip are algebraic multiples of certain periods, and the algebraicity is preserved under the action of the differential operators. This result is the higher genus analogue of the classical theorem of Shimura for elliptic modular forms. The nearly holomorphic forms also provide a bridge to p‑adic interpolation: the q‑expansion of a nearly holomorphic form has coefficients in the field of rationality of the original form, and the differential operators correspond to the action of the Lie algebra of the symplectic group on the p‑adic families constructed by Hida. In particular, the p‑adic L‑function L_p(F, s) for an ordinary genus‑2 eigenform F is constructed by evaluating the nearly holomorphic Eisenstein series at CM points and using the q‑expansion principle. The work of Böcherer–Schmidt (2000) extended Shimura's algebraicity theorem to vector‑valued Siegel modular forms, showing that the critical values of the spinor L‑function L(s, F, Spin) are algebraic up to explicit periods. The periods are given by the Petersson inner products of F with certain nearly holomorphic Eisenstein series of weight (k, k) and (k+2, k). The differential operators also appear in the construction of the Ikeda lift: the Ikeda lift of an elliptic eigenform f of weight 2k to a genus‑2g Siegel modular form is obtained by applying a product of Maass–Shimura operators to a certain nearly holomorphic Eisenstein series on the doubling group. This realises the Ikeda lift as a residue of an Eisenstein series and gives a direct proof of the standard L‑function factorisation L(s, Ik(f), Std) = ζ(s) ∏i=12g L(s + i - 1, f, Sym2).

The Local Langlands Correspondence for GSp(4) and Supercuspidal Representations

The local Langlands correspondence (LLC) for GSp(4) over a p‑adic field F was established by Gan and Takeda (2010) after earlier partial results by Sally–Tadić, Roberts–Schmidt, and Gan–Takeda themselves for the split case. The correspondence is a bijection between the set of irreducible admissible representations of GSp(4)(F) (up to isomorphism) and the set of enhanced L‑parameters φ: W_F × SL₂(ℂ) → GSp(4, ℂ) satisfying certain conditions, modulo conjugation. Here W_F is the Weil group of F. The L‑parameter determines the local L‑factor L(s, π, Std) and the epsilon factor ε(s, π, Std, ψ) for the standard representation of the dual group GSp(4, ℂ). The classification of representations of GSp(4)(F) falls into several families: principal series, complementary series, discrete series (including supercuspidal), and the non‑generic representations (the Saito–Kurokawa type). The supercuspidal representations are those that do not occur as subquotients of any proper parabolic induction. Gan–Takeda gave an explicit construction of all supercuspidal representations via compact induction from open compact subgroups, building on the work of Bushnell–Henniart for GL(n). They also computed the local character values and matched them with the endoscopic character identities required by the LLC. The correspondence is compatible with the local theta correspondence for the dual pairs (GSp(4), GO(V)): the theta lift of a supercuspidal representation of GO(V) corresponds to the L‑parameter obtained by composing the parameter of the GO(V) representation with the natural map GO(V) → GSp(4). The LLC for GSp(4) is a crucial input for the global endoscopic classification of Arthur (2013) for GSp(4), which describes the discrete automorphic spectrum in terms of Arthur parameters. At the level of Hecke eigenvalues, the LLC implies that the Satake parameters of an unramified representation π_p are the eigenvalues of the Frobenius conjugacy class under the L‑parameter. For a supercuspidal representation the Satake parameters are not defined, but the local L‑factor is a polynomial in p-s of degree 4 whose coefficients are determined by the L‑parameter. The epsilon factor satisfies the local functional equation and the product formula over all places gives the global functional equation of the spinor L‑function. The LLC also predicts the existence of a local Bessel model for each representation, which is the local counterpart of the global Bessel period appearing in the GGP conjecture. The dimension of the space of Bessel functionals is given by the local GGP conjecture, proved by Gan–Takeda using the LLC and the theory of distinguished representations. This local–global compatibility is what allows the translation of global period non‑vanishing into non‑vanishing of central L‑values.

  • Confusing the Rallis inner product formula with the pullback formula; the former relates theta lift norms to standard L‑values, the latter relates Eisenstein series restrictions to Bessel periods.
  • Assuming the Gan–Gross–Prasad conjecture for GSp(4) is proved for all representations; the non‑generic case required separate treatment and was completed later.
  • Believing the Schottky form J3 is a modular form on the full Sp(6, ℤ); it is invariant only under a level subgroup (the theta group) and transforms by a character under the full group.
  • Thinking nearly holomorphic forms are holomorphic; they contain polynomials in (Im Z)-1 and are not annihilated by the Cauchy–Riemann operators.
  • Equating the local Langlands correspondence for GSp(4) with that for GL(4); the dual group is GSp(4, ℂ) not GL(4, ℂ), and the standard L‑factor has degree 4 but with a different functional equation.
  • These traps highlight the subtle points that examiners often test.

The Borcherds Lift and Automorphic Products on Siegel Modular Varieties

The Borcherds lift constructs Siegel modular forms of genus g as infinite products whose exponents are given by the Fourier coefficients of a weakly holomorphic elliptic modular form of weight 1 − g/2. For g = 2 the input is a weight 0 form on SL₂(ℤ) with integral principal part, and the resulting product converges on the Siegel upper half‑space ℌ₂ to a meromorphic modular form for a congruence subgroup of Sp(4,ℤ). The divisor of the lift is a Heegner divisor determined by the negative‑index coefficients of the input form, providing a geometric interpretation of the product expansion. Borcherds proved that the lift is equivariant with respect to the action of the orthogonal group O(2, g) on the lattice of signature (2, g), and the resulting automorphic product transforms under the Weil representation attached to that lattice. This construction yields explicit Siegel modular forms whose zeros and poles are supported on Humbert surfaces, and it has been used to produce paramodular forms that correspond to abelian surfaces with prescribed endomorphism rings.

In practice the lift is computed by first choosing a vector‑valued modular form F(τ) = Σ c(n, r) qⁿ ζʳ for the Weil representation of the lattice L = U ⊕ U ⊕ ⟨−2⟩ (for genus 2). The Borcherds product is then Ψ(Z) = e2πi (ρ, Z) ∏(λ, Z)>0 (1 − e2πi (λ, Z))c(−(λ, λ)/2, λ mod L) where ρ is the Weyl vector. The exponent c(−(λ, λ)/2, λ) is the coefficient of the input form at the corresponding negative norm. The product converges absolutely for Im Z sufficiently large and extends meromorphically to all of ℌ₂. The weight of Ψ is c(0, 0)/2, and its level is the discriminant kernel of L. This explicit formula makes it possible to verify the modular transformation laws directly and to relate the lift to the theta correspondence for the dual pair (O(2, g), Sp(2g)).
