Applications include the construction of the Igusa cusp form χ₁₀ as a Borcherds product of weight 10, the proof of the paramodular conjecture for many abelian surfaces, and the generation of Siegel modular forms with prescribed vanishing orders along Humbert surfaces. The lift also provides a bridge between the theory of automorphic forms on orthogonal groups and the geometry of the moduli space of principally polarized abelian varieties, because the divisor of the product is a linear combination of Heegner divisors that are known to generate the Picard group of the Siegel modular variety after tensoring with ℚ.

The Fourier Coefficients of Siegel Eisenstein Series and Arithmetic Intersection Theory

Siegel Eisenstein series Eₖ(Z) of even weight k > g on the Siegel upper half‑space ℌ_g have Fourier expansions indexed by semi‑positive definite half‑integral matrices T of size g. The coefficient a(T) is given by a product of local densities: a(T) = ∏_p α_p(T) where α_p(T) is the p‑adic density of representations of T by the standard quadratic form over ℤ_p. For genus 2 this product can be expressed in terms of divisor sums and the Fourier coefficients of the corresponding elliptic Eisenstein series. The Siegel–Weil formula identifies the integral of a theta series over an orthogonal group with the value of an Eisenstein series, and the Fourier coefficients of the Eisenstein series therefore encode representation numbers of quadratic forms.

In arithmetic intersection theory, the Fourier coefficients of the derivative of a Siegel Eisenstein series at the central point s = k/2 appear as the arithmetic degrees of special cycles on the Siegel modular variety. Kudla and Rapoport showed that the generating series of these arithmetic intersection numbers is a Siegel modular form of weight g + 1, and its Fourier coefficients are precisely the derivatives of the local densities α_p(T). This result provides a geometric interpretation of the non‑singular Fourier coefficients of the Eisenstein series and connects them to the heights of Heegner cycles. For g = 2 the arithmetic Siegel–Weil formula relates the height pairing of Humbert surfaces to the derivative of the genus‑2 Eisenstein series, yielding explicit formulas for the Faltings heights of abelian surfaces with real multiplication.

The computation of the local densities α_p(T) involves the theory of quadratic forms over ℤ_p and the classification of their Jordan splittings. For a prime p ≠ 2 the density is a rational function in p⁻¹ determined by the invariants of T modulo p. At p = 2 the formula is more subtle and requires the 2‑adic Jordan decomposition. The product over all primes converges to a rational number that can be expressed in terms of the Fourier coefficients of the elliptic Eisenstein series of weight k − g + 1. This factorization is the analytic incarnation of the Siegel–Weil formula and underlies many applications to the arithmetic of Shimura varieties.

Worked Example: Computing a Fourier Coefficient of the Genus‑2 Eisenstein Series
Let k = 4 and T = [[2, 1],[1, 2]] (a positive definite half‑integral matrix). Compute the Fourier coefficient a(T) of the Siegel Eisenstein series E₄(Z) on ℌ₂.
The coefficient a(T) equals the product over all primes p of the local density α_p(T). For p ≠ 2, T has determinant 3, which is a unit modulo p, so the quadratic form represented by T is unimodular at p. The local density for a unimodular rank‑2 form over ℤ_p is α_p(T) = (1 − p⁻¹)(1 − p⁻²). For p = 2, the matrix T has even diagonal entries and odd off‑diagonal entry; its 2‑adic Jordan splitting is ⟨2⟩ ⊕ ⟨2⟩ with a non‑trivial linkage. The 2‑adic density is α₂(T) = 2⁻¹(1 − 2⁻¹)(1 − 2⁻²) = 3/32. Multiplying over all primes gives a(T) = α₂(T) ∏p≠2 (1 − p⁻¹)(1 − p⁻²) = (3/32) × ζ(2)⁻¹ ζ(3)⁻¹ = (3/32) × (6/π²) × (1/ζ(3)). Numerically a(T) ≈ 0.018. This coefficient appears in the Fourier expansion E₄(Z) = ΣT≥0 a(T) e2πi tr(TZ).
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a Fourier coefficient of a Siegel Eisenstein series, first identify the genus and weight, then write the coefficient as a product of local densities. Remember that for odd primes the density depends only on the determinant and the Hasse invariant of T, while the prime 2 requires the full Jordan splitting. Use the Siegel–Weil formula to replace the infinite product by a quotient of Riemann zeta values when the weight is large enough for absolute convergence. This shortcut saves time and avoids messy 2‑adic calculations.

The Non‑holomorphic Siegel Modular Forms and Maass–Shimura Differential Operators

Non‑holomorphic Siegel modular forms arise naturally when one applies Maass–Shimura differential operators to holomorphic Siegel modular forms. For genus g the Maass–Shimura operators are a family of invariant differential operators Dk, r that raise the weight by 2r and map holomorphic forms of weight k to nearly holomorphic forms of weight k + 2r. A nearly holomorphic form is a polynomial in (Im Z)⁻¹ with holomorphic coefficients; it is annihilated by a power of the Cauchy–Riemann operator but not by the operator itself. The operators are constructed from the Lie algebra of Sp(2g, ℝ) and satisfy a commutation relation with the slash action of the symplectic group. For g = 1 the classical Maass–Shimura operator is D_k = (1/2πi)(∂/∂τ + k/(τ − barτ)). For higher genus the operators are given by iterated applications of the basic raising operator on each diagonal entry of Z and appropriate symmetrization.

The image of a holomorphic Siegel eigenform under a Maass–Shimura operator is an eigenfunction of the hyperbolic Laplacian on ℌ_g with eigenvalue determined by the weight and the representation of the maximal compact subgroup. These nearly holomorphic forms play a crucial role in the construction of the Ikeda lift: the Ikeda lift of an elliptic eigenform f of weight 2k to a genus‑2g Siegel modular form is obtained by applying a product of Maass–Shimura operators to a certain nearly holomorphic Eisenstein series on the doubling group. The resulting form is holomorphic because the poles in (Im Z)⁻¹ cancel after taking a suitable linear combination. This cancellation is a manifestation of the fact that the Ikeda lift corresponds to a functorial transfer from GL₂ to GSp(2g) and its standard L‑function factorises as a product of shifted symmetric square L‑functions of f.

Another source of non‑holomorphic Siegel modular forms is the theory of real‑analytic Eisenstein series E(Z, s) which depend on a complex parameter s. Their Fourier expansions involve Bessel functions Ks‑k/2(2π√{det T}) and they satisfy a functional equation under s ↦ k − s. The residues at the poles of E(Z, s) give holomorphic or nearly holomorphic Siegel modular forms, while the values at the centre s = k/2 produce non‑holomorphic forms that are eigenfunctions of the Laplacian. These forms are used in the Rankin–Selberg method to represent the standard L‑function of a Siegel cusp form as an integral against an Eisenstein series, and they also appear in the Kudla–Millson theta lift which produces cohomology classes on the Siegel modular variety.

The Siegel Modular Forms of Genus Four and the Schottky Locus

The Schottky problem asks for a characterization of the Jacobian locus 𝒥_g inside the moduli space 𝒜_g of principally polarized abelian varieties of dimension g. For g = 4 the Jacobian locus has codimension 1 in 𝒜4, and its closure is a divisor known as the Schottky divisor. Igusa showed that the Schottky divisor can be defined by a single Siegel modular form of genus 4 and weight 8, the Schottky form J₄, which is a polynomial in the genus‑4 theta constants with rational coefficients. The form J₄ vanishes exactly on the locus of abelian fourfolds that are either Jacobians of genus‑4 curves or products of lower‑dimensional Jacobians. Its Fourier expansion begins with a term q1/2 corresponding to the unique even unimodular lattice of rank 8, and the coefficients encode the number of representations of half‑integral matrices by that lattice.

The construction of J₄ uses the fact that the ring of Siegel modular forms of genus 4 is generated by theta constants of the 10 even theta characteristics. The Schottky form is the unique (up to scalar) weight‑8 modular form that is invariant under the full symplectic group Sp(8, ℤ) and vanishes on the hyperelliptic locus. Its zero divisor is the sum of the hyperelliptic locus and the locus of products of Jacobians of genus 1 and 3, each with multiplicity 1. The form J₄ satisfies a heat equation with respect to the genus‑4 theta constants, which reflects its origin as a derivative of the genus‑4 theta nullwert. The Schottky form also appears in the factorisation of the genus‑4 Siegel Eisenstein series of weight 8: the pullback of the Eisenstein series to the boundary component corresponding to the product of two genus‑2 factors contains J₄ as a factor.

Recent work has extended the Schottky problem to higher genus by studying the ideal of the Jacobian locus in the graded ring of Siegel modular forms. For g = 5 the Jacobian locus has codimension 3, and the ideal is generated by the Schottky–Jung relations among theta constants together with certain higher‑degree forms constructed from the Borcherds lift. The geometry of the Schottky locus is closely related to the theory of Prym varieties and the Torelli map, and the equations defining it provide explicit modular forms whose Fourier coefficients have arithmetic significance, such as counting curves with prescribed automorphism groups.

  • Confusing the Borcherds lift with the Saito–Kurokawa lift; the former produces meromorphic forms with prescribed divisors, the latter produces holomorphic cusp forms from elliptic modular forms.
  • Assuming the Fourier coefficients of Siegel Eisenstein series are always given by divisor sums; they are products of local densities which at p = 2 require the full Jordan splitting.
  • Thinking nearly holomorphic forms are holomorphic; they contain polynomials in (Im Z)⁻¹ and are not annihilated by the Cauchy–Riemann operators.
  • Believing the Schottky form J₄ is a cusp form; it is a modular form of weight 8 that does not vanish at all cusps.
  • Equating the Maass–Shimura operators with the classical Ramanujan–Serre derivative; the former act on several complex variables and raise the weight by even integers, while the latter acts on one variable and raises weight by 2.
  • Overlooking the role of the Weil representation in the Borcherds product; the input form must be vector‑valued for the lattice L, not a scalar elliptic modular form.
  • Assuming the local Langlands correspondence for GSp(4) is identical to that for GL(4); the dual group is GSp(4, ℂ) and the standard L‑factor has a different functional equation.

The Siegel–Weil Formula and Theta Integrals for Dual Reductive Pairs

The Siegel–Weil formula expresses the integral of a theta kernel over an orthogonal group as a Siegel Eisenstein series on the symplectic side of a dual reductive pair. For a non‑degenerate quadratic space (V, Q) of dimension m over ℚ and a symplectic group Sp(2g), the theta kernel θ(g, h; φ) depends on a Schwartz function φ on V(𝔸)^g. Integrating θ over the orthogonal group O(V)(ℚ)O(V)(𝔸) yields a function on Sp(2g)(𝔸) that is invariant under Sp(2g)(ℚ). The Siegel–Weil formula asserts that this integral equals the value at a specific point of a Siegel Eisenstein series E(g, s; φ) attached to the same data. The point of evaluation is s = (m − g − 1)/2 when m > g + 1 (the convergent range), and the formula extends by meromorphic continuation to all m. When m ≤ g + 1 the integral diverges and must be regularised; the regularised integral equals the residue or the value of the Eisenstein series at the same point. This identity is the cornerstone of the theta correspondence: it shows that the theta lift of the trivial representation of O(V) is an Eisenstein series, and more generally that the theta lift of an automorphic representation of O(V) occurs in the space of automorphic forms on Sp(2g) generated by Eisenstein series. The formula also underlies the Rallis inner product formula, which expresses the Petersson norm of a theta lift in terms of the central value of a standard L‑function. In the case g = 2 and m = 3 the dual pair is (Sp(4), O(3)); the Siegel–Weil formula relates the integral of a genus‑2 theta series over a Shimura curve to a Siegel Eisenstein series of weight 3/2. This special case is used to prove non‑vanishing of central L‑values for elliptic modular forms and to construct p‑adic L‑functions via the Eisenstein measure. The proof of the Siegel–Weil formula uses the unfolding method, the Poisson summation formula on V(𝔸)^g, and the analytic properties of the Eisenstein series. The convergence range is determined by the condition that the theta integral is absolutely convergent, which holds exactly when m > g + 1. Outside this range one must use the regularised theta integral of Kudla and Rallis, which subtracts the contribution of the constant term of the Eisenstein series. The resulting identity has been extended to the case of unitary groups and to the metaplectic double cover of Sp(2g), where it plays a central role in the work of Ichino, Yamana, and others on the refined Gan–Gross–Prasad conjecture.

The Doubling Method and the Standard L‑Function of GSp(4)

The doubling method, introduced by Piatetski‑Shapiro and Rallis, constructs an integral representation for the standard L‑function of a cuspidal automorphic representation π of GSp(4, 𝔸). One embeds GSp(4) × GSp(4) diagonally into GSp(8) and considers the Siegel Eisenstein series E(g, s; Φ) on GSp(8) induced from a section Φ on the Siegel parabolic. For a cusp form f in the space of π, the integral ∫GSp(4)(ℚ)GSp(4)(𝔸) f(g) E(diag(g, g), s; Φ) dg unfolds to a product of local zeta integrals. At each place v the local integral computes the local standard L‑factor L_v(s, π_v, std) times a normalising factor. The global integral therefore equals L^S(s, π, std) times a product of local integrals at the ramified places. The Eisenstein series has a simple pole at s = 1 whose residue is the constant function; the integral at s = 1 gives the Petersson norm of f. The functional equation of the Eisenstein series under s ↦ 1 − s translates into the functional equation of the standard L‑function. The doubling method also works for the spinor L‑function when one uses a different parabolic subgroup of GSp(8). For GSp(4) the standard L‑function is of degree 4 and satisfies a functional equation relating s to 1 − s with gamma factors Γ_ℝ(s) Γ_ℝ(s + 1) Γ_ℂ(s + k − 3/2) Γ_ℂ(s + k − 1/2) for a holomorphic Siegel cusp form of weight k. The method has been extended to higher genus GSp(2g) by embedding GSp(2g) × GSp(2g) into GSp(4g). It provides a powerful tool for proving algebraicity of critical L‑values, for constructing p‑adic L‑functions via the Eisenstein measure, and for establishing the non‑vanishing of central L‑values in families. The local zeta integrals at unramified places are computed by the Casselman–Shalika formula, which expresses them in terms of the Satake parameters of π_v. At ramified places the local integrals can be normalised to give the correct local L‑factor and epsilon factor. The doubling method is also the starting point for the Rankin–Selberg integral for the product of two Siegel modular forms of genus 2, which represents the tensor product L‑function L(s, π × π').

Worked Example: Local Zeta Integral for an Unramified Representation
Let π_v be an unramified principal series representation of GSp(4, ℚ_p) with Satake parameters α1, α2, β1, β2 satisfying β_i = q_v1/2 α_i-1. Compute the local doubling zeta integral Z_v(s, W_v, Φ_v) for the spherical Whittaker function W_v and the standard section Φ_v, and show that it equals L_v(s, π_v, std) up to a simple factor.
The local integral is ∫NGSp(4) W_v(g) Φ_v(diag(g,g), s) dg where N is the unipotent radical of the Siegel parabolic. Using the Iwasawa decomposition and the fact that W_v and Φ_v are spherical, the integral reduces to an integral over the diagonal torus. The Casselman–Shalika formula gives W_v(diag(a1,a2,a2-1,a1-1)) = δ1/2(a) ∑w∈W c_w α1w1 α2w2 β1w3 β2w4 where W is the Weyl group of type C2. The section Φ_v on the Siegel parabolic of GSp(8) evaluated at diag(g,g) is a product of two copies of the spherical function on GSp(4). After changing variables and using the Macdonald formula for the spherical function, the integral factors as a product of four one‑dimensional integrals of the form ∫ℚ_p^× |x|_ps + ... dx. Each such integral yields a local factor (1 − q_v-(s+...))-1. Collecting the four factors gives exactly L_v(s, π_v, std) = ∏i=12 (1 − α_i q_v-s)-1 (1 − β_i q_v-s)-1 multiplied by the normalising factor (1 − q_v-2s)-1 which comes from the constant term of the Eisenstein series. Thus Z_v(s, W_v, Φ_v) = L_v(s, π_v, std) ⋅ (1 − q_v-2s)-1.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the integral representation of the standard L‑function of GSp(4), immediately recall the doubling embedding GSp(4)×GSp(4) GSp(8) and the Siegel Eisenstein series on the larger group. The key steps are: (1) write the global integral, (2) unfold using the cuspidality of the Siegel form, (3) factor into local integrals, (4) identify the local unramified integral with the Casselman–Shalika formula. Memorise the gamma factors for the functional equation: Γ_ℝ(s) Γ_ℝ(s+1) Γ_ℂ(s+k−3/2) Γ_ℂ(s+k−1/2). For p‑adic questions, know that the local zeta integral at an unramified place equals the local L‑factor times (1−p−2s)−1.

Siegel Modular Forms for Congruence Subgroups and Hecke Algebras at Higher Level

The classical theory of Siegel modular forms is usually developed for the full symplectic group Sp(2g, ℤ). For arithmetic applications one must consider congruence subgroups such as the principal congruence subgroup Γ_g(N) = {γ ∈ Sp(2g, ℤ) : γ ≡ 1 mod N} and the paramodular groups K_g(N) which are the stabilisers of a polarisation of type (1,…,1,N). A Siegel modular form of weight k and level N is a holomorphic function F : ℌ_g → ℂ satisfying F(γ⟨Z⟩) = det(CZ+D)^k F(Z) for all γ = (A B; C D) ∈ Γ_g(N) and the Koecher condition at all cusps. The space M_k(Γ_g(N)) is finite‑dimensional and carries an action of the Hecke algebra ℋ(Γ_g(N)Sp(2g, ℚ)/Γ_g(N)). For g = 1 this is the classical Hecke algebra generated by T_p and Tp2. For g ≥ 2 the Hecke algebra is generated by the double cosets Γ_g(N) diag(p^a1,…,p^a_g, pb1,…,pb_g) Γ_g(N) with a_i, b_i ∈ {0,1} and a_i + b_i = 1. The Satake isomorphism identifies the spherical Hecke algebra at an unramified prime p ∤ N with the ring of symmetric polynomials in the Satake parameters α1, α2, β1, β2 subject to β_i = pk−3/2 α_i-1. The Hecke operators act on Fourier coefficients by explicit formulas generalising the classical T_p action on elliptic modular forms. A Siegel eigenform is a simultaneous eigenfunction of all Hecke operators; its eigenvalues determine the local L‑factors. The theory of newforms for Sp(2g) was developed by Andrianov, Schmidt, and others: a newform of level N is an eigenform that is orthogonal to all forms coming from lower levels via the degeneracy maps. The multiplicity one theorem for GSp(4) (proved by Weissauer and others) asserts that the space of newforms of a given level and weight is multiplicity‑free under the Hecke algebra. This allows one to attach a cuspidal automorphic representation π of GSp(4, 𝔸) to each newform. The local components π_p at p ∤ N are unramified principal series; at p | N they are ramified representations whose type is determined by the level structure. The paramodular conjecture of Brumer and Kramer predicts a bijection between paramodular newforms of weight 2 and level N with rational eigenvalues and isogeny classes of abelian surfaces of conductor N. This conjecture has been verified for many levels using the paramodular Hecke algebra computed by Poor and Yuen. The Hecke algebra at higher level also controls the action of Atkin–Lehner involutions, which are essential for the functional equation of the twisted L‑functions and for the construction of p‑adic L‑functions via Hida theory.

Siegel Modular Forms with Nebentypus and the Theory of Characters

Just as elliptic modular forms can carry a Dirichlet character (nebentypus), Siegel modular forms of genus g can be twisted by a character of the symplectic group. A character χ : Γ_g(N) → ℂ^× that factors through the determinant of the lower‑right block D modulo N gives a multiplier system χ(γ) = χ(det D). The space M_k(Γ_g(N), χ) consists of holomorphic functions satisfying F(γ⟨Z⟩) = χ(γ) det(CZ+D)^k F(Z). When χ is a primitive Dirichlet character modulo N, the Fourier expansion of F involves Gauss sums and the Fourier coefficients satisfy twisted multiplicative relations. The Hecke operators at primes p ∤ N commute with the nebentypus and the eigenvalues are the same as for the untwisted case, but the local L‑factor at p | N is modified by the character. The standard L‑function of a twisted Siegel eigenform F is L(s, F, std, χ) = ∏_p L_p(s, π_p, std, χ_p) where χ_p is the local component of the character. The functional equation relates s to k − s with a root number involving the Gauss sum of χ. The theory of twisted Siegel modular forms is essential for the construction of p‑adic L‑functions in families: one varies the nebentypus through p‑power characters and interpolates the critical L‑values. The work of Hida, Tilouine, and Urban on p‑adic families of Siegel modular forms uses the ordinary projector on the Hecke algebra with nebentypus to construct a p‑adic measure on the weight space. The Fourier coefficients of a twisted Siegel Eisenstein series are given by products of local densities twisted by the character; at p = 2 the Jordan splitting must be taken into account. The Saito–Kurokawa lift can also be twisted: if f is an elliptic newform of weight 2k−2 with nebentypus χ, the lift produces a Siegel modular form of genus 2, weight k, and nebentypus χ (or χ2 depending on the normalisation). The Maass lift similarly extends to twisted Jacobi forms. The presence of nebentypus complicates the Koecher principle: one must check holomorphy at all cusps of the level structure, which are represented by the orbits of Γ_g(N) on the set of isotropic subspaces. The dimension formulas for M_k(Γ_g(N), χ) involve the Riemann–Roch theorem on the stack of principally polarised abelian varieties with level N structure and a character of the determinant line bundle.

The Global Langlands Correspondence for GSp(4) and Functorial Lifts to GL(4)

The global Langlands correspondence for GSp(4) predicts a bijection between cuspidal automorphic representations π of GSp(4, 𝔸) and certain 4‑dimensional symplectic representations ρ : Gal(ℚ̄/ℚ) → GSp(4, ℂ) of the absolute Galois group, up to equivalence. The endoscopic classification of Arthur (completed by Mok, Kaletha, and others) establishes this correspondence for representations that are globally generic (i.e., have a Whittaker model). The classification uses the stable trace formula and the theory of endoscopy for the quasi‑split group GSp(4). The endoscopic groups of GSp(4) are GL(2) × GL(2) (the split endoscopic group) and a non‑split inner form of GL(2) over a quadratic extension. A cuspidal representation π of GSp(4) is either of general type (not coming from an endoscopic lift) or an endoscopic lift from one of these groups. The Saito–Kurokawa lift corresponds to the non‑generic endoscopic lift from GL(2) via the non‑split endoscopic group; the Yoshida lift corresponds to the split endoscopic lift from GL(2) × GL(2). For a general type representation π, the functorial transfer to GL(4) is a cuspidal automorphic representation Π of GL(4, 𝔸) whose standard L‑function equals the spinor L‑function of π. This transfer is constructed by the Langlands–Shahidi method using the doubling integral for the spinor L‑function, and its existence is a theorem of Weissauer (for holomorphic Siegel modular forms) and of Gan–Takeda (for general generic representations). The transfer preserves local L‑ and epsilon factors at all places. The image of the transfer consists of those Π on GL(4) that are self‑dual with trivial central character and whose exterior square L‑function has a pole at s = 1. This characterisation is the global analogue of the local condition that the Langlands parameter lands in GSp(4, ℂ) ⊂ GL(4, ℂ). The correspondence also predicts the existence of Galois representations attached to Siegel eigenforms: for a holomorphic Siegel cusp form F of weight k ≥ 3 that is a Hecke eigenform, there should be a compatible system of ℓ‑adic Galois representations ρF,ℓ : Gal(ℚ̄/ℚ) → GSp(4, ℚ_ℓ) with the property that for almost all primes p the characteristic polynomial of ρF,ℓ(Frob_p) matches the Hecke polynomial of F at p. This has been proved by Laumon, Weissauer, and others for g = 2 and by Taylor, Yoshida, and others for higher genus under certain conditions. The functorial lift to GL(4) then gives a 4‑dimensional Galois representation via the known correspondence for GL(4). The global Langlands correspondence for GSp(4) is the foundation for the Bloch–Kato conjecture for the spinor motive of a Siegel modular form, for the construction of p‑adic L‑functions via the Euler system of Kato, and for the proof of cases of the paramodular conjecture via the Taylor–Wiles method.

  • Confusing the Siegel–Weil formula with the Rallis inner product formula; the former equates a theta integral to an Eisenstein series, the latter expresses the norm of a theta lift as an L‑value.
  • Assuming the doubling method works for the spinor L‑function without changing the parabolic; the spinor L‑function requires the Klingen parabolic in the doubling construction.
  • Thinking that Hecke operators at higher level are generated only by T_p and Tp2; for g ≥ 2 there are 2^g independent generators corresponding to the double cosets of diagonal matrices with entries in {1, p}.
  • Believing that a Siegel modular form with nebentypus has the same Fourier coefficient multiplicativity as the untwisted case; the twisted Hecke relations involve Gauss sums and the character values at the Fourier index.
  • Equating the endoscopic lift from GL(2) × GL(2) (Yoshida lift) with the Saito–Kurokawa lift; the former produces generic representations, the latter produces non‑generic CAP representations.
  • Assuming the functorial transfer to GL(4) exists for all cuspidal representations of GSp(4); it is known only for globally generic representations, while CAP representations (like Saito–Kurokawa) transfer to non‑cuspidal representations on GL(4).
  • Overlooking the role of the paramodular group in the paramodular conjecture; the conjecture specifically concerns the paramodular level K2(N), not the principal congruence subgroup Γ2(N).
The advanced theory of Siegel modular forms thus intertwines representation theory, algebraic geometry, and arithmetic in a way that continues to drive research in the Langlands program.

The Yoshida Lift and Endoscopic Lifts from GL(2) × GL(2)

The Yoshida lift is the split endoscopic lift from the product of two copies of GL(2) to GSp(4). Given two elliptic cuspidal newforms f and g of weights k₁ ≥ k₂ ≥ 2, levels N₁, N₂, and trivial nebentypus (or matching central characters), the lift produces a cuspidal automorphic representation π of GSp(4, 𝔸) whose archimedean component is a holomorphic discrete series with Harish‑Chandra parameter ((k₁−1)/2, (k₂−1)/2). When k₁ = k₂ = k this yields a scalar‑valued Siegel cusp form of genus two and weight k; when k₁ > k₂ the result is a vector‑valued Siegel modular form of weight (k₁, k₂). The construction proceeds via the theta correspondence for the dual pair (GL(2) × GL(2), GSp(4)) inside a larger symplectic group, or equivalently by the pullback of a Siegel Eisenstein series on GSp(4) to the endoscopic subgroup. The spinor L‑function of the lifted form factors as L(s, π, spin) = L(s, f) L(s, g), while the standard L‑function satisfies L(s, π, std) = ζ(s) L(s, f × g). The lift is generic (it admits a Whittaker model) and therefore lies in the discrete spectrum but not in the CAP spectrum. The local components at unramified places are the unramified principal series representations of GSp(4) whose Satake parameters are the pairwise products of the Satake parameters of f and g. The global existence of the lift was proved by Yoshida (1980s) using the Siegel–Weil formula and by Roberts (2005) via the Rallis inner product formula. A key condition for non‑vanishing is that the central characters of f and g coincide and that the root numbers ε(1/2, f) and ε(1/2, g) are both +1. The Yoshida lift provides a rich supply of non‑Saito–Kurokawa Siegel eigenforms and plays a central role in the endoscopic classification of Arthur for GSp(4), where it corresponds to the split endoscopic group GL(2) × GL(2).

The Endoscopic Classification for GSp(6) and Higher Rank Symplectic Groups

Arthur’s endoscopic classification for classical groups extends to the symplectic groups Sp(2g) and their similitude versions GSp(2g). For g = 3 the endoscopic groups of GSp(6) are products of lower‑rank symplectic and orthogonal groups: the split endoscopic group is GL(2) × GL(2) × GL(2) (or more precisely a Levi subgroup of a maximal parabolic), while non‑split endoscopic groups involve inner forms of GL(2) over quadratic extensions and the group GSp(2) × GSp(4). The classification parametrizes cuspidal automorphic representations of GSp(6, 𝔸) by global Arthur parameters — formal sums of irreducible representations of GL(n) satisfying certain self‑duality and parity conditions. A representation is of general type if its parameter is not induced from a proper endoscopic group; otherwise it is an endoscopic lift. The work of Gan and Takeda (2010s) established the local Langlands correspondence for GSp(6) and proved the multiplicity formula for generic representations. The global classification for GSp(6) is conditional on the stabilization of the trace formula for this group, which has been achieved by Mok and others for quasi‑split classical groups. For higher genus g ≥ 4 the endoscopic groups become increasingly numerous, including products of Sp(2a) × Sp(2b) with a+b = g and various non‑split inner forms. The paramodular conjecture for abelian threefolds predicts a bijection between paramodular Siegel eigenforms of genus three and abelian threefolds over ℚ with certain endomorphism algebras; this is the genus‑three analogue of the paramodular conjecture for abelian surfaces. Recent work of Bergström, Faber, and van der Geer on the cohomology of the moduli space of principally polarized abelian threefolds provides computational evidence for the conjecture. The endoscopic classification also governs the structure of the spinor L‑function: for a general type representation of GSp(6) the spinor L‑function is expected to be the degree‑8 L‑function associated to the spin representation of the dual group GSp(6, ℂ) → GL(8, ℂ), while for endoscopic lifts it factors into products of lower‑degree L‑functions.

Worked Example: Identifying the Endoscopic Type
A cuspidal Siegel eigenform F of genus three has spinor L‑function L(s, F, spin) = L(s, f₁) L(s, f₂) L(s, f₃) where each fᵢ is a weight‑2 newform of level Nᵢ with trivial character. Determine the endoscopic group from which F arises and describe the expected Arthur parameter.
The factorization of the spinor L‑function into a product of three GL(2) L‑functions indicates that F is an endoscopic lift from the split endoscopic group GL(2) × GL(2) × GL(2) of GSp(6). The Arthur parameter of F is the formal sum ψ = π₁ ⊠ 1 ⊕ π₂ ⊠ 1 ⊕ π₃ ⊠ 1, where πᵢ is the cuspidal automorphic representation of GL(2, 𝔸) attached to fᵢ, and 1 denotes the trivial representation of SL(2, ℂ). This parameter is elliptic for the endoscopic group but not for GSp(6) itself, confirming that F is not of general type. The central character condition forces the product of the central characters of the πᵢ to be trivial, which holds because each fᵢ has trivial nebentypus. The lift is generic because each πᵢ is generic, and the resulting representation of GSp(6) admits a Whittaker model.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks to classify a Siegel eigenform by its L‑function factorization, first identify the degree of each factor. A product of GL(2) L‑functions signals a split endoscopic lift; a product of a GL(2) L‑function with a symmetric square or adjoint L‑function signals a non‑split endoscopic lift (e.g., Saito–Kurokawa). Memorize the endoscopic groups for GSp(4) (GL(2)×GL(2) and non‑split GL(2)) and for GSp(6) (GL(2)³, GSp(2)×GSp(4), etc.). This pattern recognition saves time on multiple‑choice items.

p‑adic L‑functions and Hida Theory for Siegel Modular Forms of Genus ≥ 3

Hida’s theory of ordinary p‑adic families was originally developed for GL(2) and later extended to GSp(4) by Hida, Tilouine, and Urban. For genus g ≥ 3 the construction of p‑adic families of Siegel eigenforms faces new geometric and representation‑theoretic obstacles. The Siegel modular variety of genus g has dimension g(g+1)/2, and its minimal compactification has a highly singular boundary. Pilloni and Stroh (2016) developed a higher Hida theory for Siegel varieties using the theory of perfectoid spaces and the Hodge–Tate period map. They constructed the eigenvariety for GSp(2g) parametrizing finite‑slope overconvergent Siegel eigenforms, and proved a control theorem relating classical ordinary Siegel eigenforms to points on the eigenvariety. The ordinary locus in the moduli space of principally polarized abelian varieties with p‑power level structure is affine, which allows the use of the theory of (φ, Γ)‑modules to interpolate Hecke eigenvalues. For the spinor L‑function, the p‑adic L‑function is constructed by interpolating the critical values L(k/2, F, spin) as F varies in a Hida family. The work of Liu, Zhang, and Zhang (2020) established the p‑adic interpolation of the spinor L‑function for GSp(4) and initiated the genus‑three case using the doubling method in families. The main conjecture of Iwasawa theory for the spinor motive predicts that the characteristic ideal of the Selmer group coincides with the ideal generated by the p‑adic L‑function. This has been proved for GSp(4) in the ordinary case by Skinner and Urban (2014) and is a major open problem for higher genus. The existence of p‑adic families also enables the study of congruences between Siegel eigenforms of different genera, such as the congruence between a genus‑three Yoshida lift and a general type eigenform, which can be used to produce elements in Selmer groups via the Euler system of Kato.

The Rallis Tower Property and Theta Lifts for Dual Pairs (Sp(2g), O(V))

The theta correspondence for the dual pair (Sp(2g), O(V)) where V is a quadratic space over ℚ of dimension m provides a powerful tool for constructing automorphic representations of the symplectic group. The Rallis tower property describes how the theta lift Θ(π) of a fixed cuspidal automorphic representation π of O(V) behaves as the Witt index of V increases. One considers a tower of quadratic spaces V₀ ⊂ V₁ ⊂ V₂ ⊂ … obtained by adding hyperbolic planes. The first occurrence index r(π) is the smallest r such that the theta lift of π to Sp(2r) is non‑zero. The conservation relation, proved by Sun and Zhu (2015) for the metaplectic cover and by Gan and Ichino for the similitude groups, states that for a given π the sum of the first occurrence indices in the two towers (one for even dimensional V, one for odd) equals the dimension of the minimal representation of the dual group. This property governs the structure of CAP representations: for example, the Saito–Kurokawa lift corresponds to the first occurrence of the trivial representation of O(1) in the tower for Sp(4). The Rallis inner product formula expresses the Petersson norm of the theta lift in terms of the central value of the standard L‑function of π. For higher genus, the tower property is used to prove non‑vanishing of theta lifts for generic representations and to establish the local theta correspondence for unramified representations via the fundamental lemma. The Siegel–Weil formula identifies the theta integral of the Siegel Eisenstein series on O(V) with the Siegel Eisenstein series on Sp(2g), providing a bridge between the Rallis inner product formula and the doubling method for L‑functions. Recent work of Cai, Friedberg, and Kaplan extends the tower property to the setting of p‑adic families, allowing the interpolation of theta lifts across Hida families and the construction of p‑adic L‑functions for the standard L‑function of GSp(2g).

  • Confusing the Yoshida lift (split endoscopic, generic) with the Saito–Kurokawa lift (non‑split endoscopic, non‑generic CAP).
  • Assuming the endoscopic classification for GSp(6) is completely known; it is conditional on the stabilized trace formula and only established for generic representations.
  • Thinking that Hida theory for genus ≥ 3 works exactly as for GSp(4); the geometry of the boundary and the non‑affineness of the ordinary locus require perfectoid methods.
  • Believing the Rallis tower property gives a bijection between all representations of O(V) and Sp(2g); it only governs first occurrence and conservation, not a full correspondence.
  • Equating the spinor L‑function of a genus‑three general type form with a product of three GL(2) L‑functions; that factorization characterizes the split endoscopic lift, not the general type.
  • Overlooking the role of the central character matching condition in the Yoshida lift; without it the theta integral vanishes.
  • Assuming the p‑adic L‑function for the spinor L‑function of genus three has been fully constructed; only partial results exist for ordinary families.

The advanced theory of Siegel modular forms continues to intertwine representation theory, arithmetic geometry, and p‑adic analysis in ways that drive current research in the Langlands program.

The Moduli of Polarized Abelian Varieties with Non‑Principal Polarization and Siegel Modular Forms

The classical Siegel upper half‑space 𝔥g parametrizes principally polarized abelian varieties of dimension g. When the polarization type is a tuple d = (d₁,…,dg) with d₁ | d₂ | … | dg, the corresponding moduli problem is represented by a quotient of 𝔥g by a congruence subgroup of the symplectic similitude group GSp(2g,ℚ) that preserves the alternating form with elementary divisors d. The resulting Shimura variety 𝒜g,d is a finite cover of the principal level Siegel modular variety. Siegel modular forms for this group are holomorphic functions on 𝔥g satisfying a transformation law with respect to the paramodular group K(d) and a weight k factor of automorphy. Their Fourier expansions are indexed by semi‑positive definite half‑integral matrices T with the additional condition that d⁻¹ T d⁻¹ is integral. This condition reflects the fact that the polarization is not principal and leads to a richer structure of Fourier coefficients, which encode representation numbers of quadratic forms with level d. The geometry of 𝒜g,d has been studied by Hulek, Sankaran, and others: the boundary components in toroidal compactifications correspond to degenerations of polarized abelian varieties with the given type, and the Hodge bundle splits according to the polarization type. The ring of modular forms for K(d) is finitely generated; for genus two and small d explicit generators are known (e.g. the work of Poor and Yuen on paramodular forms of level 277). These forms play a central role in the paramodular conjecture, which predicts a bijection between paramodular newforms of weight two and abelian surfaces with the corresponding polarization. The theory also connects with the theory of Jacobi forms of higher index via the Fourier–Jacobi expansion, where the index is a positive definite matrix related to d.

Heegner Cycles on Siegel Modular Varieties and the Arithmetic of Special L‑Values

Heegner cycles are algebraic cycles on Siegel modular varieties constructed from CM points on Shimura curves embedded in the Siegel space. For genus g ≥ 2, one considers embeddings of a product of two Shimura curves (or a single Shimura curve via a Klingen parabolic) into the Siegel modular variety 𝒜g. The resulting cycles, often called Kudla–Millson cycles, are defined using the theta lift of a harmonic Siegel modular form of weight k = g + 1. Their cohomology classes lie in the middle degree Hodge structure of the Siegel variety and are expected to be related to the central critical values of the spinor L‑function of a Siegel eigenform F. The conjectural formula, due to Kudla, Rapoport, and Yang, expresses the height pairing of two such cycles as a derivative of the Rankin–Selberg L‑function L(s, F × χ) at s = 1/2, where χ is a Hecke character of the CM field. In genus two, this has been proved in many cases by Yuan, Zhang, and Zhang (2013) using the arithmetic Siegel–Weil formula. For higher genus, the work of Liu (2019) and of Howard and Yang (2021) extends the results to certain non‑split situations. The cycles also provide a geometric interpretation of the Fourier coefficients of the derivative of a Siegel Eisenstein series at the central point, linking the arithmetic of the cycles to the coefficients of the Siegel Eisenstein series. This circle of ideas underlies the Bloch–Beilinson conjectures for the motives attached to Siegel modular forms and gives a concrete method to produce elements in Selmer groups via the Euler system of Heegner cycles.

Worked Example: Computing a Heegner Cycle Height Pairing
Let F be a genus‑two Siegel eigenform of weight 3, level 1, with spinor L‑function L(s, F, spin). Let χ be a quadratic Hecke character of a real quadratic field K such that the root number of L(s, F × χ) is −1. Describe the steps to compute the Néron–Tate height of the Heegner cycle Δχ on the Siegel threefold 𝒜2 and relate it to L′(1/2, F × χ).
  1. Construct the Shimura curve X attached to an indefinite quaternion algebra over ℚ split at the infinite place and ramified at the primes dividing the conductor of χ. The CM points on X correspond to optimal embeddings of the order of K into the quaternion order.
  2. Embed X into 𝒜2 via the Klingen parabolic associated to the isotropic line defined by the CM point. This yields a divisor Zχ on 𝒜2.
  3. Define the Heegner cycle Δχ as the difference of two such divisors coming from conjugate CM points, ensuring it is homologically trivial.
  4. Compute the height pairing ⟨Δχ, Δχ⟩ using the arithmetic intersection theory on a regular model of 𝒜2 over ℤ. The local contributions at finite primes are given by the intersection numbers of the closures of the cycles; at the infinite place the contribution is the archimedean height given by the Green current of the Kudla–Millson form.
  5. Apply the arithmetic Siegel–Weil formula (Yuan–Zhang–Zhang) which identifies the generating series of these heights with the derivative at s = 1/2 of the incoherent Eisenstein series on the metaplectic double cover of Sp(4). The Fourier coefficient of this derivative at the matrix corresponding to χ equals L′(1/2, F × χ) up to an explicit non‑zero constant.
  6. Conclude that ⟨Δχ, Δχ⟩ = C · L′(1/2, F × χ) where C is a product of local factors (Tamagawa numbers, periods, and the Petersson norm of F).
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the relation between Heegner cycles and L‑values, recall the three pillars: (1) the geometric construction via Shimura curve embeddings, (2) the arithmetic Siegel–Weil formula linking heights to derivatives of Eisenstein series, and (3) the Rankin–Selberg unfolding that identifies the relevant Fourier coefficient with the central L‑value. Memorise the weight condition k = g + 1 for the Kudla–Millson form and the role of the root number −1 in forcing a zero at the centre.

p‑adic Uniformization of Siegel Modular Varieties and Overconvergent Siegel Modular Forms

The p‑adic uniformization of Siegel modular varieties, developed by Rapoport and Zink (1996) and refined by Andreatta, Iovita, and Pilloni (2015), provides a rigid‑analytic description of the supersingular locus of the Siegel modular variety 𝒜g at a prime p of good reduction. The uniformization identifies the formal completion of 𝒜g along its supersingular locus with a quotient of the Drinfeld p‑adic symmetric domain Ωg (a rigid‑analytic space of dimension g(g−1)/2) by a discrete group Γ arising from the endomorphisms of a supersingular abelian variety. This description allows one to define overconvergent Siegel modular forms as rigid‑analytic sections of the automorphic line bundle on a strict neighbourhood of the ordinary locus inside Ωg. The theory of overconvergent forms is essential for constructing p‑adic families of Siegel eigenforms beyond the ordinary locus. The work of Pilloni (2012) and of Andreatta–Iovita–Pilloni (2018) constructs the eigenvariety for GSp(4) using the geometry of the Hodge–Tate period map and the theory of (φ, Γ)‑modules. For higher genus, the eigenvariety is built using the perfectoid Siegel space of Scholze (2015), which provides a p‑adic uniformization at infinite level. Overconvergent forms admit a Up‑operator whose finite‑slope part controls the p‑adic variation of Hecke eigenvalues. The classicality theorem of Tian and Xiao (2019) states that an overconvergent eigenform of sufficiently small slope is classical. This machinery underlies the construction of p‑adic L‑functions for the spinor and standard L‑functions in families, and it is the setting for the proof of the main conjecture of Iwasawa theory for GSp(4) by Skinner and Urban (2014).

The Langlands Functoriality for GSp(2g) and the Transfer to GL(2g) via the Standard L‑Function

The Langlands dual group of GSp(2g) is GSpin(2g+1), whose standard representation is of dimension 2g+1. The standard L‑function L(s, π, std) of a cuspidal automorphic representation π of GSp(2g, 𝔸) is expected to arise from a functorial transfer to GL(2g+1). For g = 2 this is the transfer to GL(5) established by Arthur (2004) using the endoscopic classification and the stable trace formula. For general g, the transfer is conjectural but known in many cases: if π is globally generic, the work of Cogdell, Kim, Piatetski‑Shapiro, and Shahidi (2004) gives a functorial lift to GL(2g+1) via the Langlands–Shahidi method, proving the analytic properties of the standard L‑function. The lift is characterized by the equality of local L‑factors at almost all places. For non‑generic representations (CAP representations), the transfer factors through endoscopic groups: the Saito–Kurokawa lift corresponds to the endoscopic group GL(2) × GL(2) embedded in GSpin(5), while the Yoshida lift corresponds to the split endoscopic group GL(2) × GL(2) with a different embedding. The standard L‑function of a Saito–Kurokawa lift factorizes as ζ(s) ζ(s−k+1) L(s, f, sym²) where f is the underlying elliptic modular form. The functoriality also predicts a transfer from GSp(2g) to GL(2g) via the spin representation of the dual group; this is the spinor L‑function. The spinor lift for g = 2 is known by work of Weissauer (2005) and for higher genus by recent results of Arthur and Mok (2015) under local conditions. The compatibility of these transfers with the local Langlands correspondence for GSp(2g) (established by Gan and Takeda for g = 2 and by Atobe and Gan for higher rank) provides a complete picture of the L‑packet structure and the endoscopic classification.

  • Confusing the standard L‑function (degree 2g+1) with the spinor L‑function (degree 2g) and their respective functorial targets.
  • Assuming the endoscopic classification for GSp(2g) with g ≥ 3 is unconditional; it relies on the stabilized trace formula which is still open in full generality.
  • Thinking that every Siegel eigenform lifts to GL(2g+1) with the same central character; the central character must match the similitude character of the representation.
  • Overlooking the distinction between globally generic and CAP representations when applying the Langlands–Shahidi method.
  • Believing that the p‑adic uniformization of the supersingular locus works identically for all primes; the description requires p > 2g+1 for the Rapoport–Zink uniformization to be smooth.
  • Equating the Fourier coefficients of a paramodular form with those of a level‑one Siegel form; the polarization type imposes integrality conditions on the Fourier index.
  • Neglecting the role of the Kudla–Millson form’s weight (g+1) in the construction of Heegner cycles; using the wrong weight yields cycles that are not homologically trivial.

These advanced topics illustrate how the geometry of moduli spaces, the arithmetic of special cycles, p‑adic analytic methods, and the Langlands program intertwine in the modern theory of Siegel modular forms.

The Cohomology of Siegel Modular Varieties and the Langlands Program

The Siegel modular variety Ag = Sp(2g,ℤ)\𝔥g parametrizes principally polarized abelian varieties of dimension g. Its cohomology groups carry a natural action of the Hecke algebra and provide a geometric realization of automorphic representations of GSp(2g). By the Matsushima formula, the (ℓ‑adic) cohomology Hi(Ag, ℚℓ) decomposes as a direct sum over cuspidal automorphic representations π of GSp(2g, 𝔸) of the (𝔤, K)‑cohomology of π∞ tensored with the finite part πf. For g = 2, the middle degree i = 3 captures the cuspidal cohomology attached to holomorphic Siegel modular forms of weight (k1, k2) with k1 ≥ k2 ≥ 3. The work of Faltings and Chai on the toroidal compactifications shows that the boundary contributions are controlled by Eisenstein cohomology, which corresponds to non‑cuspidal automorphic representations induced from Levi subgroups. The Langlands program predicts that the Galois representations attached to π appear in the étale cohomology of the Shimura variety Ag. For g = 2, Taylor, Lafforgue, and others have constructed the compatible system of ℓ‑adic Galois representations for regular algebraic cuspidal π, confirming the local‑global compatibility at unramified places. The endoscopic classification of Arthur further organizes the cohomology into stable packets, separating generic representations from CAP representations such as the Saito–Kurokawa lifts. Recent advances by Caraiani and Scholze on the cohomology of Shimura varieties at infinite level provide a p‑adic analogue, linking the completed cohomology to the p‑adic Langlands correspondence for GSp(4). Understanding the precise Hecke eigenvalues on each cohomological degree remains a central problem, especially for higher genus where the endoscopic classification is still conditional on the stabilized trace formula.

The Garrett–Rankin–Selberg Integral for the Spinor L‑Function of GSp(4)

The spinor L‑function L(s, π, spin) of a cuspidal automorphic representation π of GSp(4, 𝔸) admits an integral representation due to Garrett, Piatetski‑Shapiro, and Rallis. One starts with a Siegel Eisenstein series E(s, g) on GSp(4) attached to the Siegel parabolic, and integrates it against a cusp form φ in the space of π over the quotient GSp(4, ℚ)GSp(4, 𝔸). The integral unfolds to a product of local zeta integrals Z(s, Wv, Φv) where Wv is a Whittaker function of πv and Φv is a Schwartz–Bruhat function. At almost all unramified places, the local integral computes the local spinor L‑factor L(s, πv, spin) = ∏i=14 (1 − αi,v qv−s)−1, where the αi,v are the Satake parameters of πv. The global integral converges for Re(s) large and provides a meromorphic continuation and functional equation for L(s, π, spin). The poles of the Eisenstein series at s = 1/2 and s = 1 correspond to the trivial representation and the Saito–Kurokawa lifts, respectively; their residues give the factorization L(s, π, spin) = ζ(s) ζ(s − k + 1) L(s, f, sym²) for a Saito–Kurokawa lift attached to an elliptic form f of weight 2k − 2. The integral also yields a precise formula for the central critical value L(1/2, π, spin) in terms of a Petersson inner product of φ with a theta lift from a smaller group, which is the starting point for the Böcherer conjecture. The method extends to higher genus, where the spinor L‑function of GSp(2g) is represented by an Eisenstein series on the Siegel parabolic of Sp(2g) and the unfolding produces a product of local integrals that match the degree 2g spinor L‑factor. The analytic properties of these integrals are essential for the proof of functoriality from GSp(2g) to GL(2g) via the spin representation of the dual group GSpin(2g+1).

Worked Example: Local Unramified Computation
Let πp be an unramified principal series representation of GSp(4, ℚp) with Satake parameters α1, α2, α3, α4 satisfying α1α4 = α2α3 = pk−3/2 for a Siegel modular form of weight (k, k). Compute the local spinor zeta integral Z(s, Wp, Φp) for the standard spherical vector and show it equals the local spinor L‑factor.
Choose the spherical Whittaker function Wp normalized so that Wp(1) = 1, and the spherical Schwartz function Φp = 1M2(ℤp). The local integral reduces to an integral over the unipotent radical N(ℚp) of the Siegel parabolic. Using the Iwasawa decomposition and the Casselman–Shalika formula for the spherical Whittaker function, the integral becomes a sum over the Weyl group of GSp(4) of rational functions in the Satake parameters. After simplification, one obtains Z(s, Wp, Φp) = ∏i=14 (1 − αi p−s)−1, which is exactly the local spinor L‑factor L(s, πp, spin). The condition α1α4 = α2α3 ensures the functional equation s 1−s for the completed L‑function.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the integral representation of a spinor L‑function, identify the Eisenstein series on the Siegel parabolic, the unfolding steps, and the role of the spherical vector. Remember that the local unramified computation always yields the product of (1 − αi q−s)−1. For CAP representations, note the extra poles at s = 1/2 and s = 1 and the resulting factorization of the global L‑function.

The Local Langlands Correspondence for GSp(2g) with g ≥ 3

The local Langlands correspondence (LLC) for GSp(2g) over a p‑adic field F matches irreducible admissible representations of GSp(2g, F) with Langlands parameters, i.e., homomorphisms from the Weil–Deligne group WF′ into the L‑group GSpin(2g+1, ℂ). For g = 2, the correspondence was established by Gan and Takeda (2010) using the theta correspondence with GO(5) and the classification of supercuspidal representations via Yu’s construction. For g ≥ 3, the LLC is known in many cases but not yet in full generality. Atobe and Gan (2017) proved the correspondence for generic representations of GSp(2g) by combining the Langlands–Shahidi method with the theory of twisted endoscopy. For non‑generic representations, the correspondence is expected to factor through endoscopic groups: the Siegel parabolic gives parameters that factor through GL(2) × GSpin(2g−3), the Klingen parabolic through GL(1) × GSpin(2g−1), and the Borel through a maximal torus. The classification of supercuspidal representations for higher rank symplectic groups relies on the work of Kim and Muić on the construction of supercuspidals via compact induction from open compact subgroups, together with the recent results of Kaletha on regular supercuspidals and the LLC for regular supercuspidals of connected reductive groups. The LLC for GSp(2g) is compatible with the local factors of the standard and spinor L‑functions: the standard L‑factor L(s, π, std) corresponds to the 2g+1 dimensional standard representation of GSpin(2g+1), while the spinor L‑factor L(s, π, spin) corresponds to the 2g dimensional spin representation. The local epsilon factors and gamma factors satisfy the expected functional equations. The correspondence also respects the local Langlands correspondence for the inner forms of GSp(2g), such as the groups of similitudes of quaternionic Hermitian forms, which is essential for the global endoscopic classification. Remaining open problems include the LLC for all non‑generic supercuspidals, the compatibility with the local Jacquet–Langlands correspondence for division algebras, and the explicit description of L‑packets for non‑tempered representations.

The Arithmetic of Special Values of L‑Functions via the Kudla Program on Siegel Modular Varieties

The Kudla program predicts a deep relationship between the arithmetic intersection theory of special cycles on Shimura varieties and the derivatives of Eisenstein series. For the Siegel modular variety Ag, the special cycles are defined by embedding smaller Siegel modular varieties Ag−r via the map that sends an abelian variety A to A × Er for a fixed elliptic curve E with complex multiplication. The arithmetic Chow group of these cycles carries a height pairing, and the generating series of their arithmetic degrees is conjectured to be a Siegel modular form of weight g+1, the so‑called Kudla–Millson form. The Fourier coefficients of this form are the arithmetic intersection numbers of the cycles. On the analytic side, the derivative at s = 0 of a certain incoherent Eisenstein series on the orthogonal group O(V) (where V is a quadratic space of signature (g, 2)) yields a Siegel modular form whose Fourier coefficients are the central derivatives of the Rankin–Selberg L‑functions L(s, π × χ) for automorphic representations π of GSp(2g). The work of Kudla, Rapoport, and Yang proved the conjecture for g = 1 (modular curves) and for certain cases of g = 2 (Hilbert modular surfaces). For higher genus, the program is wide open; recent progress by Liu, Zhang, and others uses the theory of mixed Shimura varieties and the arithmetic fundamental lemma to relate the intersection numbers to the derivatives of local orbital integrals. The arithmetic Siegel–Weil formula, which equates the arithmetic theta lift with the derivative of the Eisenstein series, provides a bridge between the geometry of special cycles and the analytic properties of L‑functions. This framework underlies the proof of the Gross–Zagier formula for higher dimensional Shimura varieties and the construction of Euler systems for the spinor and standard L‑functions of GSp(2g). The conjectural compatibility with the Bloch–Kato conjecture predicts that the order of vanishing of L(s, π, spin) at the central point equals the rank of the Selmer group associated to the Galois representation of π, and that the leading coefficient is expressed in terms of the height pairing of the Kudla cycles.

The p‑adic Hodge Theory of Siegel Modular Varieties and the Construction of p‑adic L‑Functions

The p‑adic Hodge theory of the Siegel modular variety Ag provides the necessary input to construct p‑adic L‑functions for the spinor and standard L‑functions of GSp(2g). The variety Ag is a Shimura variety of Hodge type, and its p‑adic étale cohomology carries a natural action of the Galois group Gal(ℚ̄p/ℚp) and of the Hecke algebra. By the work of Scholze (2015) on the perfectoid Siegel space, the inverse limit of the tower of Ag at infinite level p‑adically uniformizes to a perfectoid space, which allows one to compare the p‑adic étale cohomology with the de Rham cohomology via the Hodge–Tate period map. The Hodge–Tate period map πHT : Ag,∞ → 𝔽𝔩g (the flag variety of GSp(2g)) is GSp(2g, ℚp)‑equivariant and its fibers are related to the Rapoport–Zink space for the supersingular locus. The theory of (φ, Γ)‑modules and the Fontaine–Laffaille functor (for p > 2g+1) give a classification of the p‑adic Galois representations appearing in the cohomology. The ordinary part of the cohomology, cut out by the idempotent of the Up operator, is crystalline and its Hodge–Tate weights are determined by the weight of the Siegel modular form. Using the p‑adic interpolation of the critical values of the complex L‑functions, one constructs p‑adic L‑functions Lp(s, π, spin) and Lp(s, π, std) as measures on the weight space. The work of Hida, Panchishkin, and later of Urban, Skinner, and Loeffler–Zerbes establishes the interpolation formula for the spinor L‑function of GSp(4) and for the standard L‑function of GSp(2g) under genericity assumptions. The p‑adic L‑functions satisfy a functional equation relating s to 1−s, and their zeros encode the p‑adic valuations of the algebraic parts of the critical L‑values. The main conjecture of Iwasawa theory for GSp(4), proved by Skinner and Urban (2014), identifies the characteristic ideal of the Selmer group with the p‑adic L‑function. For higher genus, the construction of p‑adic L‑functions in families relies on the eigenvariety for GSp(2g) built by Andreatta, Iovita, and Pilloni (2018) using the geometry of the Hodge–Tate period map and the theory of overconvergent Siegel modular forms. The classicality theorem of Tian and Xiao (2019) ensures that overconvergent eigenforms of small slope are classical, which is crucial for the interpolation property. The p‑adic Hodge theory also provides the input for the p‑adic local Langlands correspondence for GSp(4), where the p‑adic Galois representations are matched with p‑adic Banach space representations of GSp(4, ℚp).

  • Confusing the spinor L‑function (degree 2g) with the standard L‑function (degree 2g+1) when applying the Garrett–Rankin–Selberg integral.
  • Assuming the local Langlands correspondence for GSp(2g) with g ≥ 3 is completely known; it is only established for generic and some non‑generic representations.
  • Overlooking the condition p > 2g+1 for the Fontaine–Laffaille classification in the p‑adic Hodge theory of Siegel varieties.
  • Neglecting the role of the Kudla–Millson form’s weight (g+1) in the arithmetic Siegel–Weil formula; using the wrong weight yields cycles that are not homologically trivial.
  • Equating the Fourier coefficients of a paramodular form with those of a level‑one Siegel form; the polarization type imposes integrality conditions on the Fourier index.
  • Believing that every Siegel eigenform lifts to GL(2g+1) with the same central character; the central character must match the similitude character of the representation.
  • Thinking that the endoscopic classification for GSp(2g) with g ≥ 3 is unconditional; it relies on the stabilized trace formula which is still open in full generality.

These advanced topics illustrate how the geometry of moduli spaces, the arithmetic of special cycles, p‑adic analytic methods, and the Langlands program intertwine in the modern theory of Siegel modular forms.

The Petersson Inner Product and Orthogonality for Siegel Modular Forms

The Petersson inner product is a fundamental tool for studying the structure of spaces of Siegel modular forms, analogous to its role in the theory of classical modular forms. It endows the space of cusp forms with a Hilbert space structure, allowing for decomposition theorems and the analysis of Hecke eigenforms. For two Siegel modular forms F and G of weight k and genus g with respect to a congruence subgroup Γ ⊂ Sp(2g, ℤ), at least one of which is a cusp form, the Petersson inner product is defined as:

⟨F, G⟩ = ∫Γ\ℍg F(Z) G(Z)conj det(Y)k dμ(Z)

where Z = X + iY is a point in the Siegel upper half-space ℍg, Y = Im(Z), det(Y)k is part of the weight factor, and dμ(Z) is the Sp(2g, ℝ)-invariant measure on ℍg. This measure is given by dμ(Z) = det(Y)-(g+1) dX dY, where dX and dY are Euclidean measures on the spaces of symmetric real matrices of size g × g. The integral is taken over a fundamental domain for the action of Γ on ℍg. The convergence of this integral is guaranteed if at least one of the forms is a cusp form, meaning its Fourier expansion has no constant term for any cusp.

A key property of the Petersson inner product is the orthogonality of Hecke eigenforms. If F and G are two Hecke eigenforms with respect to the same Hecke operators, but with different eigenvalues, then they are orthogonal with respect to the Petersson inner product. This property is crucial for the spectral theory of Hecke operators, which states that the space of cusp forms of a given weight and level can be decomposed into an orthogonal direct sum of subspaces, each spanned by a Hecke eigenform. This decomposition is essential for understanding the arithmetic properties encoded in the Fourier coefficients of these forms.

Furthermore, the Petersson inner product plays a vital role in the theory of Rankin-Selberg convolutions for Siegel modular forms. These convolutions are constructed by integrating products of modular forms over the fundamental domain, often involving the Petersson inner product. The analytic properties of these Rankin-Selberg integrals, such as their meromorphic continuation and functional equations, are directly linked to the L-functions associated with the modular forms. For instance, the normalization of the standard L-function for GSp(4) involves the Petersson norm of the corresponding Siegel modular form. The non-vanishing of this inner product for specific choices of F and G allows for the definition and study of various L-functions, providing a powerful link between automorphic forms and number theory.

The Moduli Problem for Polarized Abelian Varieties and the Geometric Meaning of Fourier Coefficients

Siegel modular forms are intimately connected to the geometry of polarized abelian varieties through the moduli problem. A g-dimensional abelian variety is a complex torus Cg / Λ, where Λ is a lattice of rank 2g. A polarization on an abelian variety is an ample line bundle, which geometrically corresponds to an embedding into projective space. A principal polarization is one where the associated divisor is of a specific type, ensuring that the abelian variety is "as generic as possible" in a certain sense. The moduli problem for principally polarized abelian varieties of dimension g asks for a space that parametrizes all such varieties up to isomorphism.

The Siegel upper half-space ℍg provides a concrete realization of this moduli space. Each point Z = X + iY in ℍg corresponds to a principally polarized abelian variety AZ. Specifically, Z determines a period matrix for the abelian variety, which encodes the complex structure and the polarization. The group Sp(2g, ℤ) acts on ℍg, and the quotient space Ag = Sp(2g, ℤ)\ℍg is the coarse moduli space of principally polarized abelian varieties of dimension g. Siegel modular forms of weight k are then holomorphic functions on ℍg that transform in a specific way under the action of Sp(2g, ℤ), and they can be interpreted as sections of certain line bundles on Ag.

The Fourier expansion of a Siegel modular form F(Z) = ΣT≥0 a(T) exp(2πi tr(TZ)) carries deep geometric and arithmetic information. The summation is over positive semi-definite integral symmetric matrices T, called Fourier indices. The Fourier coefficients a(T) often have a direct geometric interpretation, particularly when T is positive definite. For instance, in certain contexts, a(T) can be related to the number of ways a quadratic form represented by T can be embedded into a specific lattice, or more generally, to the number of points on certain cycles within the moduli space. For genus g=1, a classical modular form's Fourier coefficients a(n) relate to the number of ways n can be represented by a quadratic form, or to the number of elliptic curves with complex multiplication. For higher genus, the interpretation becomes more intricate, often involving representation numbers of quadratic forms, intersection numbers of cycles on the moduli space, or counts of specific geometric objects on the associated abelian varieties.

The level of the Siegel modular form, corresponding to a congruence subgroup Γ(N) ⊂ Sp(2g, ℤ), relates to the type of polarization or the presence of additional level structures on the abelian varieties. For example, a modular form for Γ(N) parametrizes principally polarized abelian varieties with a level N structure, which means a choice of basis for the N-torsion points satisfying certain conditions. This geometric connection allows for the translation of analytic properties of Siegel modular forms into geometric statements about abelian varieties, and vice-versa, forming a cornerstone of modern number theory and algebraic geometry.

Understanding the Petersson Inner Product
Consider two Siegel cusp forms F and G of weight k and genus g. The Petersson inner product ⟨F, G⟩ involves an integral over the fundamental domain Γ\ℍg. Explain why the term det(Y)k appears in the integrand and how it relates to the transformation property of Siegel modular forms.
The Petersson inner product for Siegel modular forms is defined as ⟨F, G⟩ = ∫Γ\ℍg F(Z) G(Z)conj det(Y)k dμ(Z). The term det(Y)k is crucial for ensuring the integrand is invariant under the action of the congruence subgroup Γ ⊂ Sp(2g, ℤ).

A Siegel modular form F(Z) of weight k transforms under an element M = (A B; C D) ∈ Sp(2g, ℤ) as F(M·Z) = det(CZ + D)k F(Z). The invariant measure dμ(Z) = det(Y)-(g+1) dX dY is invariant under the action of Sp(2g, ℝ).

Let's analyze the transformation of the product F(Z) G(Z)conj det(Y)k.
When Z is transformed to M·Z, we have:
1. F(M·Z) G(M·Z)conj = det(CZ + D)k F(Z) (det(CZ + D)k G(Z))conj = det(CZ + D)k det(CZ + D)conj, k F(Z) G(Z)conj = |det(CZ + D)|2k F(Z) G(Z)conj.
2. The imaginary part Y of Z transforms as YM·Z = (CZ + D)-1, conj Y (CZ + D)-1. Therefore, det(YM·Z) = |det(CZ + D)|-2 det(Y).

Substituting these into the integrand:
F(M·Z) G(M·Z)conj det(YM·Z)k = |det(CZ + D)|2k F(Z) G(Z)conj ( |det(CZ + D)|-2 det(Y) )k
= |det(CZ + D)|2k F(Z) G(Z)conj |det(CZ + D)|-2k det(Y)k
= F(Z) G(Z)conj det(Y)k.

Thus, the factor det(Y)k precisely cancels the transformation factor of the modular forms, making the entire integrand invariant under the action of Γ. This invariance is essential for the integral over the fundamental domain to be well-defined and independent of the choice of fundamental domain. Without this specific weight factor, the integral would not converge or would not be invariant, undermining the definition of the inner product.
Connecting Concepts for Deeper Understanding
When encountering advanced topics like the Petersson inner product or the moduli problem, always try to draw parallels with their simpler counterparts (e.g., for classical modular forms or elliptic curves). This helps build intuition. For instance, recognize that the Petersson inner product is a generalization of the L2 inner product on a compact Riemann surface, and the moduli space of abelian varieties generalizes the moduli space of elliptic curves. Understanding the 'why' behind specific terms (like det(Y)k) by checking transformation properties is a powerful technique to solidify your grasp, as these details often reveal the underlying mathematical consistency.

Explicit Construction of Siegel Cusp Forms via Theta Series Associated with Lattices

While Eisenstein series provide a direct way to construct Siegel modular forms, they are typically not cusp forms. A significant class of Siegel cusp forms can be constructed using theta series associated with positive definite even lattices. This method generalizes the construction of classical modular forms from theta series of quadratic forms.

Let L be a positive definite even lattice of rank m over ℤ, equipped with a symmetric bilinear form B(x, y) and a corresponding quadratic form Q(x) = B(x, x). An even lattice means Q(x) is an even integer for all x ∈ L. We can associate a theta series to this lattice. For a Siegel upper half-space variable Z ∈ ℍg, the Siegel theta series of genus g associated with L is defined as:

ΘL,g(Z) = ΣX ∈ Mm×g(ℤ) exp(πi tr(Z Xt Q X))

Here, Xt Q X is an m × m matrix where Q is the Gram matrix of the lattice L (i.e., Qij = B(ei, ej) for a basis {ei} of L), and X is an m × g matrix with integer entries. The term Xt Q X is actually a g × g matrix whose entries are quadratic forms in the columns of X. More precisely, if Q is the Gram matrix of L, then the quadratic form on Mm×g(ℤ) is given by TX = Xt Q X, which is a symmetric g × g matrix. The sum is over all m × g integer matrices X.

For ΘL,g(Z) to be a Siegel modular form, certain conditions must be met. If L is a positive definite even lattice of rank m, then ΘL,g(Z) is a Siegel modular form of weight m/2 for a certain congruence subgroup. Specifically, if m is even, say m = 2k, then ΘL,g(Z) is a Siegel modular form of integer weight k. The level of the modular form is related to the level of the lattice, which is the smallest integer N such that N times the inverse of the Gram matrix is an integral matrix. For example, if L is unimodular (det(Q) = 1), then ΘL,g(Z) is a modular form of weight m/2 for the full symplectic group Sp(2g, ℤ).

A famous example is the E8 lattice, which is an even unimodular lattice of rank 8. Its associated theta series ΘE8,g(Z) is a Siegel modular form of weight 4 for Sp(2g, ℤ). These theta series are often cusp forms, especially when the rank m is sufficiently large relative to the genus g. The cuspidality arises from the fact that the sum is over positive definite quadratic forms, which means the constant term in the Fourier expansion (corresponding to T=0) is typically zero or can be adjusted to be zero. The theory of the Weil representation (also known as the metaplectic representation) provides a more general framework for understanding the modular properties of these theta series, particularly for half-integral weights.

While theta series provide a rich source of Siegel modular forms, it is important to note that not all Siegel cusp forms can be realized as theta series. The problem of determining which modular forms are theta series is a deep question in the theory of automorphic forms, often related to the Gan-Gross-Prasad conjectures and the theory of theta lifts.

The Eichler-Shimura Isomorphism and Cohomology of Siegel Modular Varieties

The Eichler-Shimura isomorphism is a profound result connecting modular forms to the cohomology of certain algebraic varieties. For classical modular forms (genus g=1), it establishes an isomorphism between the space of modular forms of weight k and certain cohomology groups of modular curves. This connection is fundamental for understanding the arithmetic properties of modular forms, as cohomology groups are natural homes for Galois representations.

For Siegel modular forms, this isomorphism generalizes to connect spaces of Siegel modular forms of weight k to the cohomology of Siegel modular varieties Ag. Specifically, for a Siegel modular form F of weight k for a congruence subgroup Γ, and under certain conditions (e.g., F being a cusp form and k being sufficiently large), F can be associated with a class in the cohomology group Hg(g+1)/2(Ag, ℂ) or, more precisely, in the cohomology of the local system associated with the weight k. The precise statement involves the Hodge decomposition of the cohomology and the fact that Siegel modular forms correspond to certain holomorphic differential forms on the compactified Siegel modular variety.

The Siegel modular variety Ag is a complex algebraic variety (or an orbifold) that parametrizes principally polarized abelian varieties of dimension g. Its cohomology groups H*(Ag, ℂ) carry a rich structure, including actions of the absolute Galois group of ℚ and the Hecke algebra. The Eichler-Shimura isomorphism, in its generalized form, asserts that the space of Siegel cusp forms of a given weight k can be embedded into a direct sum of certain Hodge components of the cohomology of Ag. More specifically, it relates the space of vector-valued Siegel modular forms to the cohomology of the local system Vλ on Ag, where Vλ is a representation of Sp(2g, ℂ) corresponding to the weight λ.

This cohomological interpretation is crucial for several reasons. Firstly, it provides a geometric framework for studying Siegel modular forms. The properties of the modular forms, such as their Fourier coefficients, can be translated into properties of cohomology classes, which are often more amenable to algebraic and geometric techniques. Secondly, it establishes a direct link to the Langlands program. The Galois representations predicted by Langlands functoriality are expected to live in the cohomology of automorphic varieties, and the Eichler-Shimura isomorphism provides the mechanism to realize these representations from automorphic forms. Eigenforms for Hecke operators correspond to eigenclasses in cohomology, and these eigenclasses are conjectured to be associated with specific Galois representations.

The construction of these cohomology classes often involves differential forms. For example, a Siegel modular form F of weight k can be used to construct a (k-g-1)-form on the Siegel upper half-space, which then descends to a cohomology class on the modular variety. The action of Hecke operators on modular forms translates into an action on these cohomology classes, preserving the eigenstructure. This deep connection allows number theorists to use tools from algebraic geometry and topology to study automorphic forms and their associated L-functions, leading to profound results such as the construction of p-adic L-functions and the study of special values of L-functions.

The Role of Trace Formulas in the Theory of Siegel Modular Forms

The Selberg trace formula is a powerful analytical tool in the theory of automorphic forms, providing a link between the spectral side (eigenvalues of Hecke operators and Laplace operators) and the geometric side (orbital integrals over conjugacy classes). For Siegel modular forms, the trace formula is particularly complex but immensely important for understanding the structure of the space of forms and for the Langlands program.

The trace formula for GSp(2g) (or Sp(2g)) relates the trace of Hecke operators on a space of Siegel modular forms to a sum over conjugacy classes in the group. On the spectral side, it sums the contributions from automorphic representations, weighted by the trace of the Hecke operator on each representation. This side provides information about the multiplicity of different automorphic representations in the space of modular forms. On the geometric side, the sum is over conjugacy classes of elements in the group, and each term is an orbital integral, which involves integrating a test function over the conjugacy class. These orbital integrals are often difficult to compute but encode deep arithmetic information.

For Siegel modular forms, the trace formula has been developed by various mathematicians, including Selberg himself, and later by Arthur, Kottwitz, and others in the context of the endoscopic classification program. The "stabilized" trace formula, a more refined version, aims to separate contributions from different endoscopic groups, which are smaller groups related to the original group GSp(2g). This stabilization is crucial for the Langlands functoriality conjectures, which predict transfers of automorphic representations between different groups. For GSp(4), the trace formula has been extensively studied and used to establish cases of Langlands functoriality, such as lifts from GL(2) × GL(2) to GSp(4) (e.g., the Yoshida lift, which is already mentioned as an existing topic, but the *trace formula* is the underlying mechanism for proving such lifts).

The trace formula provides a method to count automorphic forms with specific properties. For instance, by choosing appropriate test functions, one can extract the dimension of spaces of modular forms, or the number of eigenforms with certain eigenvalues. It is also a key tool for proving the existence of certain automorphic representations and for studying their L-functions.
