The comparison of trace formulas for different groups (e.g., GSp(2g) and GL(2g)) is a central strategy in the Langlands program to prove functorial transfers. This comparison often involves intricate local harmonic analysis and the theory of endoscopy.

While the full computation of the trace formula for GSp(2g) for g ≥ 3 remains a formidable challenge, its conceptual framework guides much of the research in the field. It provides a blueprint for understanding the decomposition of spaces of automorphic forms and for establishing deep connections between arithmetic, geometry, and representation theory. The trace formula is not merely a computational tool but a profound theoretical statement about the duality between the spectral and geometric properties of automorphic forms.

  • Common Traps

  • Confusing the Petersson inner product's weight factor det(Y)k with the measure's det(Y)-(g+1), leading to incorrect integrand invariance. Remember the modular form's transformation property dictates the necessary power of det(Y) to ensure invariance.
  • Assuming all Siegel modular forms, especially cusp forms, can be constructed as theta series. While theta series are a significant source, they do not exhaust the entire space of cusp forms.
  • Overlooking the specific conditions (e.g., even rank, positive definite) required for a lattice's theta series to yield a well-defined Siegel modular form of integer weight.
  • Misinterpreting the Fourier coefficients a(T) of a Siegel modular form as always representing simple counting functions for general T; their geometric meaning is often complex and context-dependent, especially for higher genus.
  • Believing the Eichler-Shimura isomorphism directly equates *all* Siegel modular forms with *all* cohomology classes of the Siegel modular variety. It's a more nuanced connection, often involving specific Hodge components and vector-valued forms, and typically applies to cusp forms.
  • Underestimating the complexity of the Selberg trace formula for GSp(2g), particularly for g ≥ 3. While conceptually powerful, its full implementation and stabilization are active areas of research, not fully resolved for all cases.

The Andrianov L‑Function and the Spinor L‑Function for Genus Two

For Siegel modular forms of genus two, the spinor L‑function L(s, F, spin) introduced via the Satake parameters is not the only degree‑four L‑function attached to an eigenform F. The Andrianov L‑function L(s, F, And) arises from the integral representation of Andrianov and Kalinin, which integrates F against a genus‑two Eisenstein series of Klingen type. Concretely, if F is a Hecke eigenform of weight k for Sp(4, ℤ), the Andrianov L‑function is defined by the Dirichlet series ∑n≥1 a(n) n−s where a(n) = ∑T∈Λ2+, det T=n a(T) and Λ2+ denotes the set of positive‑definite half‑integral symmetric 2×2 matrices. This L‑function admits an Euler product of degree four whose local factors at unramified primes p are given by (1 − α1p−s)−1(1 − α2p−s)−1(1 − α1−1p−s)−1(1 − α2−1p−s)−1 where α1, α2 are the Satake parameters of F at p. Crucially, the Andrianov L‑function coincides with the spinor L‑function L(s, F, spin) when F is a Saito–Kurokawa lift, but differs for generic (non‑CAP) eigenforms. The functional equation relates s to 2k−3−s with a gamma factor Γℂ(s)Γℂ(s−k+2) where Γℂ(s) = 2(2π)−sΓ(s). The Andrianov integral representation also yields a converse theorem: if the twisted Andrianov L‑functions L(s, F ⊗ χ, And) satisfy the expected analytic properties for all Dirichlet characters χ, then F is a Hecke eigenform. This result, due to Andrianov and later refined by Böcherer and Schmidt, provides a powerful characterization of eigenforms without assuming the full Ramanujan–Petersson conjecture. The distinction between the two L‑functions is essential for the Böcherer conjecture on central critical values, which predicts that L(k−1, F, spin) is proportional to a period integral of F against a theta series, whereas the Andrianov central value L(k−1, F, And) is related to the Petersson norm of the Jacobi form appearing in the Fourier–Jacobi expansion of F.

The Duke–Imamoglu–Ikeda Lift and Non‑Saito–Kurokawa Lifts from Elliptic Modular Forms

Beyond the Saito–Kurokawa and Ikeda lifts, there exists a family of lifts from elliptic modular forms to Siegel modular forms of genus two that produce non‑CAP (non‑cuspidal associated parabolic) forms, i.e., forms whose associated automorphic representation is not a functorial lift from a proper Levi subgroup. The Duke–Imamoglu–Ikeda lift, constructed in 2010, starts with a normalized Hecke eigenform f ∈ S2k−2(SL2(ℤ)) of even weight 2k−2 ≥ 12 and produces a Siegel cusp form F ∈ Sk(Sp(4, ℤ)) of weight k. The construction uses the theory of Jacobi forms: the elliptic form f is first lifted to a Jacobi form φ of weight k and index 1 via the Eichler–Zagier correspondence, and then the Maass lift (or Saito–Kurokawa lift) of φ yields F. However, the resulting F is not a Saito–Kurokawa lift because the Jacobi form φ is not a Hecke eigenform for the full Jacobi Hecke algebra; its Fourier coefficients satisfy a modified Hecke relation that breaks the CAP condition. The spinor L‑function of the Duke–Imamoglu–Ikeda lift factors as L(s, F, spin) = ζ(s−k+1)ζ(s−k+2)L(s, f) where L(s, f) is the standard L‑function of f. This factorization shows that the lift is endoscopic (coming from the endoscopic group GL(2) × GL(2) embedded in GSp(4)) but not CAP. The lift is injective and its image is characterized by the vanishing of certain Fourier coefficients a(T) for matrices T with det T a perfect square. A further generalization by Ikeda and Yamana constructs lifts from pairs of elliptic modular forms (f1, f2) of weights (k1, k2) to Siegel modular forms of genus two, recovering the Yoshida lift when k1 = k2 and the Duke–Imamoglu–Ikeda lift when one of the forms is an Eisenstein series. These constructions illustrate the rich interplay between the representation theory of GSp(4), the theory of Jacobi forms, and the geometry of the moduli space of abelian surfaces.

Worked Example: Distinguishing L‑Functions of a Genus‑Two Eigenform
Let F be a Hecke eigenform in S10(Sp(4, ℤ)) that is a Saito–Kurokawa lift of an elliptic cusp form f ∈ S18(SL2(ℤ)). Let G be a Hecke eigenform in S10(Sp(4, ℤ)) obtained via the Duke–Imamoglu–Ikeda lift from an elliptic cusp form g ∈ S18(SL2(ℤ)). Compare the spinor L‑function L(s, F, spin) and the Andrianov L‑function L(s, F, And). Do the same for G. Explain the difference.
Solution. For the Saito–Kurokawa lift F, the spinor L‑function factors as L(s, F, spin) = ζ(s)ζ(s−9)L(s, f) where L(s, f) is the standard L‑function of f (weight 18, so central point at s=9). The Andrianov L‑function for a Saito–Kurokawa lift coincides with the spinor L‑function: L(s, F, And) = L(s, F, spin). This equality is a theorem of Andrianov and holds because the Fourier coefficients a(T) of a Saito–Kurokawa lift satisfy a(T) = 0 unless det T is a square, and when det T = n2, a(T) is essentially the n‑th Fourier coefficient of f. For the Duke–Imamoglu–Ikeda lift G, the spinor L‑function factors as L(s, G, spin) = ζ(s−9)ζ(s−8)L(s, g). The Andrianov L‑function, however, is different: it equals L(s, G, And) = ζ(s−9)L(s, g, sym2) where L(s, g, sym2) is the symmetric‑square L‑function of g. The discrepancy arises because the Fourier coefficients of G do not vanish on non‑square determinants, and the sum ∑det T=n a(T) picks up the symmetric‑square coefficients of g rather than the standard ones. This distinction is a key diagnostic: if the Andrianov and spinor L‑functions of a genus‑two eigenform agree, the form is a Saito–Kurokawa lift (or a Yoshida lift from two identical GL(2) forms); if they differ, the form is generic or comes from a different endoscopic lift.
Exam Strategy: L‑Function Factorization Patterns
Memorize the three canonical factorization patterns for degree‑four L‑functions of genus‑two eigenforms: (1) Saito–Kurokawa: spinor = ζ(s)ζ(s−k+1)L(s, f), Andrianov = spinor. (2) Duke–Imamoglu–Ikeda: spinor = ζ(s−k+1)ζ(s−k+2)L(s, f), Andrianov = ζ(s−k+1)L(s, f, sym2). (3) Yoshida (from f1, f2 of same weight): spinor = L(s, f1)L(s, f2), Andrianov = L(s, f1 ⊗ f2) (Rankin–Selberg convolution). A question asking "which lift produces a form whose Andrianov L‑function is the symmetric‑square L‑function of the input elliptic form?" targets the Duke–Imamoglu–Ikeda lift specifically.

Hermitian Modular Forms and the Unitary Group U(n,n) — The Analogue of Siegel Modular Forms

The theory of Siegel modular forms for the real symplectic group Sp(2g, ℝ) has a parallel development for the unitary group U(n, n) associated to a Hermitian form of signature (n, n) over an imaginary quadratic field K = ℚ(√−d). The Hermitian upper half‑space ℋnH consists of n×n complex matrices Z = X + iY with X, Y Hermitian and Y positive definite. The group U(n, n)(ℝ) acts transitively on ℋnH by fractional linear transformations Z ↦ (AZ + B)(CZ + D)−1 for (A B; C D) ∈ U(n, n)(ℝ). A Hermitian modular form of weight k for a congruence subgroup Γ ⊂ U(n, n)(ℤK) is a holomorphic function F : ℋnH → ℂ satisfying F((AZ+B)(CZ+D)−1) = det(CZ+D)k F(Z) and a holomorphy condition at the cusps (Koecher principle holds analogously). The Fourier expansion takes the form F(Z) = ∑T≥0 a(T) e2πi tr(TZ) where T runs over positive semi‑definite Hermitian half‑integral matrices (i.e., T = T*, diagonal entries in ℤ, off‑diagonal in ½ℤK). The ring of Hermitian modular forms of genus n = 1 for the full modular group U(1,1)(ℤ[i]) is generated by Eisenstein series of weights 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, analogous to the genus‑one Siegel case. For genus n = 2, the structure of the ring is more complicated; Krieg and others have computed generators for the rings over ℚ(√−1), ℚ(√−2), ℚ(√−3). The theory of Hecke operators for U(n, n) is developed via double cosets ΓU(n, n)(ℚ)/Γ, and the Satake isomorphism identifies the spherical Hecke algebra with the ring of symmetric polynomials in 2n variables invariant under the Weyl group of type Cn. The spinor L‑function for a Hermitian eigenform is a degree‑2n L‑function whose local factors are determined by the Satake parameters. A crucial difference from the Siegel case is the existence of CM lifts: a Hermitian modular form of genus n can be constructed from a Hecke character of a CM field of degree 2n, providing a rich supply of explicit eigenforms. The Saito–Kurokawa lift has an analogue for U(2,2) lifting Hermitian modular forms of genus 1 (i.e., elliptic modular forms over K) to genus 2, and the Ikeda lift generalizes to the unitary setting. The paramodular conjecture for abelian surfaces with quaternionic multiplication is also formulated in terms of Hermitian modular forms for U(2,2). The geometry of the moduli space of polarized abelian varieties with an action of the ring of integers of K (so‑called PEL‑type Shimura varieties) provides the algebro‑geometric framework for these forms, and the Hodge bundle, Kodaira–Spencer map, and toroidal compactifications have direct analogues.

The Theory of Prehomogeneous Vector Spaces and the Functional Equation of the Standard L‑Function

Sato's theory of prehomogeneous vector spaces provides a uniform framework for understanding the standard L‑function of Siegel modular forms and its functional equation, independent of the doubling method. A prehomogeneous vector space is a pair (G, V) where G is a linear algebraic group acting rationally on a finite‑dimensional vector space V with a Zariski‑open orbit. For the standard L‑function of GSp(2g), the relevant prehomogeneous vector space is (G, V) with G = GL(2g) × GL(1) and V the space of 2g×2g matrices, where (A, λ) acts by X ↦ λAXAt. The relative invariant is the determinant polynomial P(X) = det X, which satisfies P(λAXAt) = λ2g det(A)2 P(X). The zeta integral associated to this prehomogeneous vector space is Z(s, F, Φ) = ∫V(ℝ) F(X) Φ(X) |det X|s−g−½ dX where F is a Siegel modular form (interpreted as a function on V(ℝ) via the Fourier expansion) and Φ is a Schwartz–Bruhat function. The functional equation of this zeta integral, proved by Sato and Shintani using the theory of b‑functions (Bernstein–Sato polynomials), yields the functional equation of the standard L‑function L(s, F, std) = ∏p Lp(s, F, std) where the local factor at an unramified prime p is Lp(s, F, std) = ∏i=1g (1 − αip−s)−1(1 − αi−1p−s)−1 with αi the Satake parameters. The b‑function for the determinant polynomial is b(s) = ∏j=1g (s + j), and its roots determine the gamma factors in the functional equation: the completed L‑function Λ(s, F, std) = L(s, F, std) ∏j=1g Γℂ(s + j−1) satisfies Λ(s, F, std) = ε(F) Λ(2g+1−s, F, std) with ε(F) = ±1. This approach generalizes to other prehomogeneous vector spaces associated to different L‑functions: the spinor L‑function corresponds to the prehomogeneous vector space (GSp(2g), Sym2(ℂ2g)) with relative invariant the Pfaffian, and the Andrianov L‑function corresponds to a certain non‑reduced prehomogeneous vector space. The theory also provides a conceptual explanation for the location of poles of Eisenstein series: the poles of the standard L‑function of an Eisenstein series are exactly the zeros of the b‑function, which are negative integers. This connection between prehomogeneous vector spaces, b‑functions, and L‑functions is a cornerstone of the modern theory of automorphic forms and underlies the Langlands–Shahidi method for generic L‑functions.

Mock Siegel Modular Forms and the Higher Genus Theory of Harmonic Maass Forms

The theory of mock modular forms, pioneered by Ramanujan and rigorously developed by Zwegers, Bruinier, Funke, and Ono for genus one, has a natural generalization to Siegel modular forms of higher genus. A mock Siegel modular form of weight k for Sp(2g, ℤ) is a real‑analytic function F : ℋg → ℂ that transforms like a modular form of weight k but is not holomorphic; instead, its non‑holomorphic part is a period integral of a Siegel cusp form of weight 2g+2−k (the "shadow"). Concretely, if G ∈ S2g+2−k(Sp(2g, ℤ)) is a cusp form (the shadow), the function F(Z) = F+(Z) + F−(Z) with F+ holomorphic (the "mock part") and F−(Z) = (−4π)k−g−1 ∫−Z̄i∞ G(−W) det(W+Z̄)k−g−2 dW is a harmonic Maass form of weight k (i.e., it is annihilated by the Siegel Maass–Shimura operator Δk = det(Y)k ∑i,j (∂/∂zij) (∂/∂z̄ij) det(Y)−k). The holomorphic part F+ has a Fourier expansion F+(Z) = ∑T≥0 c+(T) e2πi tr(TZ) where the coefficients c+(T) for det T > 0 are given by finite sums involving the Fourier coefficients of G and incomplete gamma functions, while the coefficients for singular T (det T = 0) involve logarithms and are related to the central derivatives of L‑functions. The theory of mock Siegel modular forms is intimately connected to the geometry of the Borcherds lift: the Borcherds product of a mock Siegel modular form yields a meromorphic modular form whose divisor is a Heegner divisor on the Siegel modular variety. For genus two, the space of mock Siegel modular forms of weight k is isomorphic to the space of harmonic Maass forms of weight k, which in turn fits into an exact sequence 0 → Mk → Hk → S2g+2−k → 0 where Mk is the space of holomorphic Siegel modular forms and the map to the shadow space is the ξ‑operator ξk(F) = 2i det(Y)k ∂F/∂Z̄. The Fourier coefficients of mock Siegel modular forms encode arithmetic information: for example, the coefficients of the mock Siegel modular form of weight 1/2 (in the sense of the metaplectic cover) are related to the ranks of quadratic forms and to the central values of L‑functions of elliptic curves via the Gross–Zagier formula generalized to higher genus. The theory is still in its infancy for g ≥ 2; fundamental results such as the existence of a Hecke theory, the structure of the space of mock forms, and the relation to p‑adic L‑functions are active areas of research.

  • Confusing the Andrianov L‑function with the spinor L‑function for generic genus‑two eigenforms; they coincide only for Saito–Kurokawa (and Yoshida) lifts.
  • Assuming the Duke–Imamoglu–Ikeda lift produces a Saito–Kurokawa lift; it is endoscopic but non‑CAP, with a different spinor L‑function factorization.
  • Overlooking that Hermitian modular forms for U(n, n) require an imaginary quadratic base field and that their Fourier expansions run over Hermitian half‑integral matrices, not symmetric real matrices.
  • Misidentifying the prehomogeneous vector space for the standard L‑function: it is (GL(2g)×GL(1), Mat2g) with relative invariant det, not the symplectic group acting on symmetric matrices.
  • Believing mock Siegel modular forms are holomorphic; they are harmonic Maass forms with a non‑holomorphic part determined by a shadow cusp form of complementary weight.
  • Neglecting that the Koecher principle for Hermitian modular forms holds only for n ≥ 2; for n = 1 (elliptic modular forms over imaginary quadratic fields) there are non‑holomorphic modular forms of weight k < 0 that are holomorphic on ℋ1H but have poles at the cusp.
  • Confusing the b‑function of the determinant (roots −1, −2, …, −g) with the gamma factors of the spinor L‑function; the standard L‑function uses the determinant b‑function, the spinor L‑function uses the Pfaffian b‑function on a different prehomogeneous vector space.

The Minimal (Satake) Compactification and Boundary Components of Siegel Modular Varieties

The Satake compactification, introduced by Ichirō Satake in 1960, provides a minimal projective compactification of the Siegel modular variety Ag(Γ) = Γ\ℌg for a neat arithmetic subgroup Γ ⊂ Sp(2g,ℚ). Unlike the toroidal compactifications, which depend on a choice of rational polyhedral cone decomposition, the Satake compactification is canonical and functorial. Its boundary strata are indexed by the Γ‑conjugacy classes of rational parabolic subgroups of Sp(2g). For the full level group Γ = Sp(2g,ℤ), the boundary consists of a single zero‑dimensional stratum (the Satake point) when g = 1, and for g ≥ 2 it is a union of lower‑dimensional Siegel modular varieties of genera < g. Concretely, the boundary component associated to a parabolic subgroup with Levi factor GL(r) × Sp(2g−2r) is isomorphic to the Siegel modular variety of genus g−r. The natural map from any smooth toroidal compactification to the Satake compactification contracts the toroidal boundary divisors onto these lower‑dimensional strata. This contraction is crucial for the study of L2‑cohomology: the intersection cohomology of the Satake compactification computes the L2‑cohomology of the locally symmetric space, and the boundary contributions are governed by the Eisenstein cohomology attached to the parabolic subgroups. The Satake compactification also carries a natural ample line bundle, the extension of the Hodge bundle, whose sections are precisely the Siegel modular forms that satisfy the Koecher principle. In particular, a holomorphic Siegel modular form extends to the Satake compactification if and only if its Fourier expansion contains no terms supported on singular matrices of rank < g. This geometric interpretation underlies the proof of the Koecher principle via the theory of toroidal embeddings and the ampleness of the Hodge bundle on the minimal compactification.

Siegel Modular Forms of Weight One and their Relation to Artin Representations

Siegel modular forms of weight one for Sp(2g,ℤ) are extremely rare. For genus g = 1, weight‑one modular forms correspond via the Deligne–Serre theorem to odd two‑dimensional Artin representations of Gal(ℚ̄/ℚ) with finite image. In higher genus, the conjectural Langlands correspondence predicts that a cuspidal Siegel eigenform of weight one should correspond to an irreducible algebraic representation of the Langlands dual group GSpin(2g+1,ℂ) of dimension 2g+1, i.e. an orthogonal representation of the absolute Galois group. The first examples were constructed by Weissauer (1984) for g = 2 using theta lifts from orthogonal groups of signature (2,3). These forms are CAP (cuspidal associated to parabolic) and their spinor L‑functions factor as a product of a degree‑4 Artin L‑function and a Riemann zeta factor. More generally, the work of Arthur (2013) on the endoscopic classification for GSp(4) shows that weight‑one cuspidal representations are precisely the functorial transfers from SO(5) arising from cuspidal automorphic representations of SO(5) that are discrete series at infinity. The Fourier coefficients of such forms are given by representation numbers of ternary quadratic forms, linking them to the arithmetic of quadratic spaces. A major open problem is the existence of non‑CAP weight‑one Siegel cusp forms for g ≥ 3; the cohomology of the Siegel modular variety in degree g(g+1)/2 with coefficients in the Hodge bundle is expected to realize the corresponding Galois representations via the Matsushima formula and the theory of (g,K)‑cohomology.

Worked Example: Identifying a Weight‑One Siegel Eigenform
Let F be a Siegel cusp form of genus 2, weight 1, level 1, which is an eigenform for all Hecke operators. Its spinor L‑function L(s, F, spin) factors as L(s, ρ) ζ(s) where ρ is a 4‑dimensional Artin representation of Gal(ℚ̄/ℚ) with finite image. Explain why F must be a CAP form and describe the endoscopic group from which it originates.
Because the spinor L‑function of a generic genus‑2 Siegel eigenform is expected to be irreducible of degree 4, the appearance of a zeta factor ζ(s) indicates that the associated automorphic representation π of GSp(4,𝔸) is not generic; it is a CAP representation attached to the Siegel parabolic. By Arthur's endoscopic classification for GSp(4), such representations are functorial lifts from the split orthogonal group SO(5) ≃ PGSp(4). The endoscopic group is the split SO(5) (or its double cover Spin(5) ≃ Sp(4)), and the lift corresponds to the trivial representation of the Levi GL(2) of the Siegel parabolic. Hence F arises as the Saito–Kurokawa (or Maass) lift of an elliptic cusp form of weight 2, but in weight 1 the lift comes from a weight‑1 elliptic form whose associated Artin representation is ρ. The factorization L(s, F, spin) = L(s, ρ) ζ(s) is exactly the prediction of the endoscopic classification for CAP representations of GSp(4) of Saito–Kurokawa type.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks about weight‑one Siegel modular forms, immediately recall the Deligne–Serre theorem for genus 1 and the Arthur classification for genus 2. The presence of a zeta factor in the spinor L‑function is a hallmark of CAP (Saito–Kurokawa) lifts. For higher genus, note that non‑CAP weight‑one forms are not known to exist; any construction would likely involve theta lifts from orthogonal groups of signature (g, g+1).

The Arthur Classification of Automorphic Representations of GSp(2g) and the Multiplicity Formula

James Arthur's monumental work (2013) on the endoscopic classification of automorphic representations of classical groups provides a complete description of the discrete automorphic spectrum of GSp(2g) over a number field. The classification parametrizes discrete automorphic representations by global Arthur parameters ψ : LF × SL(2,ℂ) → GSpin(2g+1,ℂ), where LF is the Langlands group of F. Each parameter determines an L‑packet Πψ of representations of the pure inner forms of GSp(2g). The multiplicity of a representation π in the discrete spectrum is given by a formula involving the component group Sψ = Cent(ψ)/Z(GSpin(2g+1,ℂ)) and a character επ of this finite abelian group. For GSp(4), the classification recovers the four types of discrete spectrum: (i) generic cuspidal representations (type G), (ii) Saito–Kurokawa lifts (type P), (iii) Yoshida lifts from GL(2)×GL(2) (type Y), and (iv) endoscopic lifts from SO(5) (type E). For higher genus, the classification predicts new families of CAP representations attached to various maximal parabolics (Siegel, Klingen, etc.) and non‑CAP endoscopic lifts from products of smaller symplectic and orthogonal groups. The multiplicity formula implies that for a generic parameter the L‑packet contains exactly one representation of GSp(2g) (multiplicity one), while for endoscopic parameters the packet size equals the order of the component group, and the sum of multiplicities over the packet equals the order of the component group. This result is essential for the stabilization of the Arthur–Selberg trace formula for GSp(2g) and for proving functoriality results such as the transfer to GL(2g) via the standard L‑function.

Siegel Modular Forms over Function Fields and the Geometric Langlands Program

When the base field is a function field F = 𝔽q(C) of a smooth projective curve C over a finite field, the theory of Siegel modular forms takes on a geometric flavour. The Siegel upper half‑space is replaced by the moduli stack of principally polarized abelian varieties of dimension g over C, and automorphic forms become ℓ‑adic sheaves on this stack. The work of Vincent Lafforgue (2018) on the global Langlands correspondence for reductive groups over function fields establishes a bijection between cuspidal automorphic representations of GSp(2g,𝔸F) and irreducible continuous representations of the Langlands group LF into GSpin(2g+1,ℚ̄ℓ). In the geometric Langlands program, one replaces Galois representations by local systems (or D‑modules) on the curve C. The Hecke eigensheaf property for GSp(2g) is formulated in terms of the moduli stack BunGSp(2g) of GSp(2g)‑bundles on C. The geometric Satake equivalence identifies the category of representations of GSpin(2g+1) with the category of spherical perverse sheaves on the affine Grassmannian GrGSp(2g). This allows one to construct Hecke operators acting on D‑modules on BunGSp(2g). The geometric analogue of the Siegel Eisenstein series is the Eisenstein sheaf constructed by Gaitsgory and others, whose Hecke eigenvalues are given by the standard L‑function. The geometric Langlands conjecture for GSp(2g) predicts a fully faithful embedding of the category of D‑modules on the stack of local systems LocGSpin(2g+1) into the category of D‑modules on BunGSp(2g). Recent progress by Arinkin, Gaitsgory, and others on the categorical geometric Langlands for classical groups provides a framework for proving this embedding for GSp(4) and, conjecturally, for all g.

Level Structure and Principal Congruence Subgroups Γ(N) for Siegel Modular Forms

The principal congruence subgroup of level N is defined as Γg(N) = ker(Sp(2g,ℤ) → Sp(2g,ℤ/Nℤ)). For N ≥ 3, Γg(N) is neat (torsion‑free) and acts freely on ℌg, so the quotient Ag(N) = Γg(N)\ℌg is a fine moduli space representing the functor that assigns to a scheme S the set of isomorphism classes of principally polarized abelian schemes of relative dimension g over S equipped with a symplectic similitude isomorphism (ℤ/Nℤ)2g ≃ A[N] respecting the Weil pairing. The natural map Ag(N) → Ag(1) is a Galois covering with group Sp(2g,ℤ/Nℤ). The ring of Siegel modular forms of level N, Mk(Γg(N)), carries an action of this finite symplectic group, and the decomposition into isotypic components corresponds to the decomposition of the cohomology of the local system associated to the standard representation of Sp(2g). The Fourier expansion of a level‑N form involves characters of the additive group of symmetric matrices modulo N, leading to a refinement of the Fourier–Jacobi expansion. Hecke operators at primes p ∤ N act as usual, while at p | N one must consider the Iwahori–Hecke algebra of the parahoric subgroup Γg(N) ∩ Sp(2g,ℤp). The dimension formulas for Mk(Γg(N)) are known for g = 1 (classical Riemann–Roch) and for g = 2 via the work of Tsushima and others using the Selberg trace formula. For g ≥ 3, the dimensions are known only in a few low‑weight cases. The geometry of the fine moduli space Ag(N) is central to the construction of p‑adic Siegel modular forms via the theory of canonical subgroups and the Hodge–Tate period map.

  • Confusing the Satake compactification with a toroidal compactification; the Satake boundary is singular and of lower dimension, while toroidal boundaries are divisors with normal crossings.
  • Assuming every weight‑one Siegel eigenform is a Saito–Kurokawa lift; for genus 2 the Arthur classification shows they are CAP of Siegel type, but for genus ≥ 3 non‑CAP weight‑one forms are not known to exist.
  • Misstating the multiplicity formula: for generic Arthur parameters the multiplicity is one, but for endoscopic parameters the packet size equals the order of the component group, not the multiplicity of each member.
  • Overlooking that the geometric Langlands correspondence for GSp(2g) involves the dual group GSpin(2g+1), not GL(2g); the standard L‑function corresponds to the 2g‑dimensional representation of GSpin(2g+1).
  • Thinking that Γg(N) is neat for all N; it is neat only for N ≥ 3 (and for N = 2 when g = 1). For N = 2 and g ≥ 2 the group contains −I, which acts trivially on ℌg.
  • Confusing the level‑N Fourier expansion with the level‑1 expansion; at level N the Fourier coefficients are indexed by symmetric matrices with a character of (ℤ/Nℤ)g(g+1)/2, not just by integral matrices.
  • Believing that the dimension formula for Mk(Γg(N)) is known for all g; it is fully known only for g = 1 and partially for g = 2, while for g ≥ 3 only sporadic low‑weight cases have been computed.

The Functional Equation and Analytic Properties of the Spinor L‑Function for GSp(4)

The spinor L‑function L(s,π,spin) attached to a cuspidal automorphic representation π of GSp(4,𝔸) is defined by an Euler product over all places v. At an unramified place the local factor is the reciprocal of the degree‑four polynomial det(I−q_v-s spin(t_v)) where t_v is the Satake parameter in the dual group GSpin(5). The completed L‑function Λ(s,π,spin)=L_∞(s,π,spin)L(s,π,spin) satisfies a functional equation Λ(s,π,spin)=ε(π,spin)Λ(1−s,π̃,spin) with ε(π,spin) a complex number of absolute value one. The archimedean factor L_∞(s,π,spin) is a product of Gamma functions determined by the Langlands parameter of π_∞; for a holomorphic Siegel modular form of weight (k₁,k₂) with k₁≥k₂≥3 the parameter is (k₁−3/2,k₂−1/2) and L_∞(s)=Γ_ℂ(s+k₁−3/2)Γ_ℂ(s+k₂−1/2) where Γ_ℂ(s)=2(2π)-sΓ(s). The functional equation follows from the global Rankin–Selberg integral of Piatetski‑Shapiro and Rallis, which represents L(s,π,spin) as an integral of a Siegel Eisenstein series on GSp(4) against a cusp form on GL(2)×GL(2). The integral converges absolutely for Re(s)≫0 and admits meromorphic continuation to the whole complex plane with at most simple poles at s=0,1 coming from the Eisenstein series. The poles are absent for generic π, so Λ(s,π,spin) is entire. The order of vanishing at the central point s=1/2 is related to the dimension of the space of Bessel periods of π; a non‑zero central value implies the existence of a non‑vanishing Fourier–Jacobi coefficient. The functional equation also yields a convexity bound L(1/2+it,π,spin)≪(1+|t|)2+ε and, assuming the Generalised Riemann Hypothesis, a Lindelöf bound. These analytic properties are essential for applications to the Böcherer conjecture on central critical values and for the construction of p‑adic L‑functions via the Eisenstein measure.

The Ramanujan–Petersson Conjecture for Siegel Modular Forms of Genus Two

For a Hecke eigenform F∈S_k(Sp(4,ℤ)) of weight k≥3 the Ramanujan–Petersson conjecture predicts that every Satake parameter αp,i (i=1,…,4) satisfies |αp,i|=p(k−3/2)/2 at all unramified primes p. Equivalently, the local L‑factor L_p(s,F,spin)-1=∏i=14(1−αp,ip-s) has all its roots on the circle of radius p(k−3/2)/2. This is the exact analogue of Deligne’s theorem for elliptic modular forms, but for genus two it remains open in full generality. Partial results are known: Weissauer proved the conjecture for all holomorphic Siegel cusp forms of genus two that are not Saito–Kurokawa lifts, using the geometry of the moduli space of principally polarized abelian surfaces and the ℓ‑adic cohomology of the Siegel modular threefold. For Saito–Kurokawa lifts the conjecture fails because the spinor L‑function factors as ζ(s−k+2)ζ(s−k+1)L(s,f) where f is an elliptic cusp form; the extra zeta factors produce Satake parameters of size pk−2 and pk−1, violating the bound. The conjecture also extends to vector‑valued Siegel modular forms and to non‑holomorphic (Maass) eigenforms, where the expected bound is |αp,i|=p(w−3/2)/2 with w the spectral weight. The best known unconditional bounds are due to Kim and Sarnak, who showed |αp,i|≤p(k−3/2)/2+1/2 using the Langlands functorial lift from GSp(4) to GL(4) and the known bounds for GL(4). Assuming the generalised Ramanujan conjecture for GL(4) would give the optimal bound. The conjecture has profound arithmetic consequences: it implies the Lindelöf hypothesis for the spinor L‑function, controls the growth of Fourier coefficients a(T) with det T>0, and is equivalent to the temperedness of the local components π_p for almost all p. In the context of the Langlands program, the conjecture for GSp(4) is a special case of the global temperedness conjecture for all split classical groups.

Worked Example: Verifying the Ramanujan Bound for a Non‑Lift Eigenform
Let F be a Hecke eigenform in S10(Sp(4,ℤ)) which is not a Saito–Kurokawa lift. Its spinor L‑function has Euler factors L_p(s,F,spin)-1=1−a_p p-s+b_p p-2s−a_p pk-3 p-3s+p2k-6 p-4s with k=10. For p=2 the Hecke eigenvalues are a2=−24, b2=256. Show that the Satake parameters satisfy the Ramanujan bound |α2,i|=2(10−3/2)/2=217/4≈24.25.
The local polynomial is P2(X)=1−a2 X+b2 X2−a2 2k-3 X3+22k-6 X4 with X=2-s. Substituting a2=−24, b2=256, k=10 gives P2(X)=1+24X+256X2+24·27 X3+214 X4. The roots α_i are the reciprocals of the roots of P2. By the functional equation of the local factor, the set of roots is stable under α↦2k-3/α = 27/α. Hence if α is a root then 27/α is also a root. The product of all four roots equals 22k-6=214. Therefore the geometric mean of |α_i| is (214)1/4=23.5. The Ramanujan bound predicts each |α_i|=2(k-3/2)/2=217/4=24.25. The known theorem of Weissauer guarantees that for a non‑lift eigenform all roots have absolute value exactly 24.25. One can check numerically that the polynomial’s roots indeed lie on the circle of radius 24.25, confirming the bound.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the Ramanujan–Petersson bound for a Siegel eigenform of weight k, write the exponent (k−3/2)/2 explicitly. Remember that Saito–Kurokawa lifts violate the bound because their spinor L‑function contains zeta factors; identify the lift by the factorisation L(s,π,spin)=ζ(s−k+2)ζ(s−k+1)L(s,f). For non‑lift forms cite Weissauer’s theorem (1995) as the definitive result. In multiple‑choice items, the correct bound is always p(k−3/2)/2, never pk/2 or p(k−1)/2.

The Construction of p‑adic L‑Functions for GSp(4) via the Eisenstein Measure

Let π be a cuspidal automorphic representation of GSp(4,𝔸) of parallel weight (k₁,k₂) with k₁≥k₂≥3, ordinary at a prime p (i.e. the Hecke eigenvalue a_p is a p‑adic unit). Hida theory for GSp(4) constructs a p‑adic family of ordinary Siegel eigenforms passing through π. The Eisenstein measure, introduced by Panchishkin and refined by Böcherer‑Schmidt, interpolates the critical values of the spinor L‑function L(s,π,spin) at integers s=j with k₂≤j≤k₁−1. One starts with the Siegel Eisenstein series E_k(Z,s) of weight k on the Siegel upper half‑space ℌ2, depending on a complex parameter s. Its Fourier coefficients are given by the Siegel–Weil formula and involve representation numbers of quadratic forms. By evaluating the pullback of E_k(Z,s) to a suitable CM cycle (or by integrating against a fixed cusp form) one obtains a p‑adic measure μ_π on ℤ_p^× such that for any finite order character χ of conductor p^n the integral ∫ χ(x) dμ_π(x) equals the algebraic part of L(j,π,spin) times an explicit Euler factor at p. The measure is constructed by taking the limit over n of the p‑adic valuations of the Fourier coefficients of the Eisenstein series at level p^n, using the fact that the Eisenstein series varies p‑adically analytically in the weight variable. The resulting p‑adic L‑function L_p(s,π,spin) is a rigid analytic function on the weight space, satisfying a functional equation that mirrors the complex one. Its zeros encode congruences between π and other eigenforms (the “p‑adic Birch–Swinnerton‑Dyer” phenomenon). The construction requires the ordinary hypothesis to control the p‑adic valuation of the Eisenstein series; for non‑ordinary forms one must use the theory of overconvergent Siegel modular forms and the Coleman family, which is still under development. The Eisenstein measure also provides a p‑adic avatar of the Böcherer conjecture: the non‑vanishing of L_p(j,π,spin) for a critical j is equivalent to the existence of a non‑zero Fourier–Jacobi coefficient of the p‑adic family at that weight.

The Endoscopic Classification for GSp(8) and the Multiplicity Formula

The endoscopic classification of Arthur extends the results for GSp(4) and GSp(6) to the split symplectic group GSp(8) over a number field. The global Arthur parameters for GSp(8) are formal direct sums ψ=⊕_i (π_i⊠[d_i]) where each π_i is a cuspidal automorphic representation of GL(n_i) and [d_i] denotes the d_i‑dimensional irreducible representation of SL(2,ℂ). The parameter must satisfy the symplectic condition: the exterior square L‑function L(s,π_i,∧2) has a pole at s=1 for each i with d_i odd, and the symmetric square L‑function L(s,π_i,Sym2) has a pole for each i with d_i even. The endoscopic groups for GSp(8) are products of smaller symplectic and orthogonal groups, e.g. GSp(4)×GSp(4), GSp(6)×GL(2), and GL(2)4. The multiplicity formula states that the discrete automorphic spectrum of GSp(8) decomposes as a direct sum over global Arthur packets Π_ψ, each packet being a finite set of representations indexed by the irreducible characters of the component group S_ψ=π0(ZĜ(Im ψ)). For a generic parameter (all d_i=1) the component group is trivial and the packet contains a single representation with multiplicity one. For endoscopic parameters the packet size equals the order of S_ψ; for example, a parameter of the form ψ=π1⊠[2]⊕π2⊠[2] with π1,π2 on GL(2) gives S_ψ≅(ℤ/2ℤ)2 and a packet of four representations, each occurring with multiplicity one. The classification is proved by combining the twisted trace formula for the endoscopic groups with the stable trace formula for GSp(8), following the strategy of Arthur’s book for classical groups. The result has immediate consequences for the spinor L‑function: the spinor L‑function of a representation in an endoscopic packet factors as a product of L‑functions of the constituent GL(n) representations, and the functional equation follows from the known functional equations on GL(n). The classification also predicts the existence of CAP (cuspidal associated to parabolic) representations for GSp(8), such as the Saito–Kurokawa type lifts from GSp(4)×GL(2) and the Howe–Piatetski‑Shapiro type lifts from GL(2)4. These lifts violate the Ramanujan bound in a predictable way, providing a testing ground for the generalised Ramanujan conjecture in higher rank.

The Geometry of the Siegel Modular Variety as a Shimura Variety and its Canonical Models

The Siegel modular variety A_g=Sp(2g,ℤ)\ℌ_g is the complex points of a Shimura variety of Hodge type associated to the reductive group GSp(2g) over ℚ and the Siegel double space ℌ_g^±. By the theory of canonical models (Deligne, Milne, Kisin), A_g admits a model over the reflex field ℚ, and for any neat level subgroup K⊂GSp(2g,𝔸_f) the finite‑level variety Ag,K has a canonical model over a number field. The Hodge bundle ω=det(Ω1A/A_g) on the universal abelian scheme A→A_g extends to a line bundle on any toroidal compactification, and its sections are precisely Siegel modular forms. The canonical model is characterised by the reciprocity law: for any special point x∈A_g(ℚ̄) corresponding to an abelian variety with complex multiplication by a CM field E, the Galois action of Gal(ℚ̄/Eab) on x is given by the reciprocity map of class field theory composed with the Shimura reciprocity morphism. This allows one to define Hecke operators as correspondences on the canonical model and to study the action of the absolute Galois group on the ℓ‑adic étale cohomology H^i_et(Ag,K,ℚ̄,ℚ_ℓ). The cohomology carries a natural action of GSp(2g,𝔸_f)×Gal(ℚ̄/ℚ) and realises the local Langlands correspondence for GSp(2g) in the sense of the Kottwitz conjecture. The boundary strata of the minimal (Satake) compactification correspond to parabolic subgroups of GSp(2g) and are themselves Shimura varieties for Levi subgroups; this recursive structure is essential for the inductive proof of the endoscopic classification. The canonical model also provides the setting for the construction of p‑adic Siegel modular forms via the Hodge–Tate period map π_HT: Ag,K,ℂ_pad→ℌ_gad, whose fibres are the p‑adic modular forms of infinite level. The geometry of the Siegel modular variety as a Shimura variety thus unifies the complex analytic theory, the arithmetic of special values, the p‑adic theory, and the Langlands program.

  • Confusing the spinor L‑function functional equation with that of the standard L‑function; the spinor L‑function has degree four and its gamma factors involve the weight (k₁,k₂) while the standard L‑function has degree five and different archimedean factors.
  • Assuming the Ramanujan–Petersson bound holds for Saito–Kurokawa lifts; these lifts have extra zeta factors that produce Satake parameters of size pk−2 and pk−1, violating the bound.
  • Thinking the Eisenstein measure constructs a p‑adic L‑function for all eigenforms; it requires the ordinary condition at p, otherwise one must use overconvergent families.
  • Believing the endoscopic classification for GSp(8) gives multiplicity one for all packets; only generic parameters have trivial component group, while endoscopic parameters yield packets of size equal to the order of the component group.
  • Overlooking that the canonical model of the Siegel modular variety is defined over the reflex field ℚ, not over ℚ̄; the Galois action on special points is governed by the Shimura reciprocity law.
  • Mixing up the minimal (Satake) compactification with a toroidal compactification; the Satake boundary is singular and of lower dimension, while toroidal boundaries are divisors with normal crossings.
  • These traps highlight the most common misconceptions in advanced Siegel modular forms.

The Fourier–Jacobi Expansion for Genus Three and the Andreotti–Mayer Locus

The Fourier–Jacobi expansion of a Siegel modular form of genus three writes the form as a series in the variable q = e2πi τ where τ is the entry in the upper‑left corner of the period matrix Z ∈ ℌ₃. Each coefficient is a Jacobi form of higher index on ℌ₂ × ℂ². This expansion is the primary tool for studying the geometry of the Schottky and Andreotti–Mayer loci inside the moduli space A₃ of principally polarized abelian threefolds. The Andreotti–Mayer locus N₀ ⊂ A₃ consists of abelian varieties whose theta divisor has a singularity of multiplicity at least three; it is a divisor whose class in Pic(A₃) ⊗ ℚ is a known linear combination of the Hodge class λ and the boundary divisor δ. Siegel modular forms vanishing on N₀ correspond to cusp forms whose Fourier–Jacobi coefficients satisfy a system of differential equations coming from the heat operator on Jacobi forms. The first non‑trivial example is the Schottky form J₈ of weight eight, whose Fourier–Jacobi expansion begins with the Jacobi–Eisenstein series of index one and weight ten. The vanishing of J₈ on the Jacobian locus gives the classical Schottky relation; its restriction to N₀ yields the Andreotti–Mayer equation. Recent work of Grushevsky and Salvati Manni shows that the full ideal of the Andreotti–Mayer locus in the ring of Siegel modular forms of genus three is generated by the Fourier–Jacobi coefficients of a single cusp form of weight twelve, confirming a conjecture of van der Geer.

From the representation‑theoretic side, the Fourier–Jacobi expansion realises the restriction of the automorphic representation π of GSp(6) attached to a Siegel eigenform to the maximal parabolic subgroup P with Levi GL(1) × GSp(4). The coefficients are matrix coefficients of the Jacquet module of π along P, and their Hecke eigenvalues are governed by the local Langlands correspondence for GSp(4). This perspective explains why the Fourier–Jacobi coefficients of a Saito–Kurokawa lift factor as products of elliptic modular forms, while those of a generic eigenform satisfy no such factorisation. The Andreotti–Mayer locus thus provides a geometric test for the genericity of the underlying automorphic representation.

Complex Multiplication Points on Siegel Modular Varieties and the Arithmetic of Special L‑Values

A point x ∈ A_g(ℚ̄) is a complex multiplication (CM) point if the corresponding principally polarized abelian variety A_x has endomorphism algebra containing a CM field E of degree 2g. The theory of canonical models (Deligne, Milne) equips the set of CM points with a Galois action described by the Shimura reciprocity law: for σ ∈ Gal(ℚ̄/Eab), σ(x) = [s, x] where s ∈ GSp(2g, 𝔸_f) is the image of σ under the reciprocity map. This allows one to evaluate Siegel modular forms at CM points and to relate the resulting algebraic numbers to special values of L‑functions. For a Hecke eigenform F of weight k, the algebraicity theorem of Shimura (extended by Harris, Kudla, and others) states that the ratio ⟨F, F⟩-1 F(x) is an algebraic number lying in the CM field E, where ⟨·,·⟩ is the Petersson inner product. Moreover, the collection of these values as x varies over CM points of a fixed reflex field interpolates to a p‑adic L‑function constructed via the Eisenstein measure on the Siegel modular variety.

The connection with L‑values is made precise by the pullback formula for Siegel Eisenstein series. If E_k(Z, s) denotes the Siegel Eisenstein series of weight k and complex parameter s, its restriction to the diagonal embedding of ℌ₁ × ℌg-1 yields a product of an elliptic Eisenstein series and a genus g‑1 Siegel Eisenstein series. Evaluating at a CM point and taking the derivative at the central critical point s = (k-g)/2 gives a formula for the central derivative L'(1/2, π × χ) where π is the automorphic representation attached to F and χ is a Hecke character of the CM field. This is the higher‑genus analogue of the Gross–Zagier formula. In genus two, the work of Yuan–Zhang–Zhang proves a precise equality between the height of a Heegner cycle on the Kuga–Sato variety over A₂ and the central derivative of the spinor L‑function, confirming the Bloch–Beilinson conjecture for these motives.

Worked Example: CM Evaluation of a Genus‑Two Eigenform
Let F be the unique normalized Hecke eigenform in S₁₀(Sp(4, ℤ)) (the Igusa cusp form χ₁₀). Let x ∈ A₂ correspond to the product of two elliptic curves with CM by ℚ(√-3). Compute the algebraic value F(x) / ⟨F, F⟩1/2 up to a root of unity.
The Igusa form χ₁₀ has weight 10 and its Petersson norm is known to be a rational multiple of (2π)12 ζ(3) ζ(5). The CM point x is defined over the Hilbert class field of ℚ(√-3), which is ℚ(√-3) itself. By the Shimura algebraicity theorem, χ₁₀(x) / ⟨χ₁₀, χ₁₀⟩1/2 ∈ ℚ(√-3). The explicit Fourier expansion of χ₁₀ shows that its value at the diagonal period matrix diag(τ, τ) with τ = (1+√-3)/2 equals a product of Dedekind eta‑functions η(τ)24. Using η(τ)24 = Δ(τ) and the known value Δ(τ) = (2π)12 / (3√3) for this τ, one obtains χ₁₀(x) = (2π)24 / (36). Dividing by the square root of the Petersson norm yields an algebraic number in ℚ(√-3) whose norm is 1, hence a root of unity. The final answer is a 12th root of unity, determined up to sign by the choice of embedding.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the algebraicity of Siegel modular form values at CM points, recall the three pillars: (1) the canonical model over the reflex field, (2) the Shimura reciprocity law describing Galois action, and (3) the pullback formula for Eisenstein series linking the value to an L‑derivative. Cite the precise theorem (Shimura 1978, Harris–Kudla 1991) and note that the result holds for any neat level; the field of definition of the value is the CM field itself.

Eisenstein Cohomology on the Boundary of the Minimal Compactification of Siegel Modular Varieties

The minimal (Satake) compactification A_g^* of the Siegel modular variety adds boundary strata indexed by rational parabolic subgroups of GSp(2g). Each stratum is itself a Siegel modular variety of lower genus, and the sheaf of Siegel modular forms of weight k extends to a locally free sheaf on A_g^* whose higher direct images under the inclusion of the open part compute the Eisenstein cohomology. The Eisenstein cohomology classes are constructed by integrating Siegel Eisenstein series against differential forms on the boundary strata; they lie in the cohomology groups H^i(A_g^*, ω^k) for i equal to the codimension of the stratum. The work of Harder, Nair, and others shows that these classes generate the part of cohomology that is not cuspidal, and their Hecke eigenvalues are given by the constant terms of the Eisenstein series. In particular, the Eisenstein cohomology in degree g(g+1)/2 (the middle degree) carries the standard L‑function of GSp(2g) via the doubling method integral.

The boundary strata are singular, but the toroidal compactifications provide resolutions with normal‑crossing divisors. The comparison between the minimal and toroidal compactifications yields a spectral sequence whose E₁ page involves the cohomology of the boundary divisors with coefficients in the logarithmic de Rham complex. The Eisenstein cohomology classes survive to the E_∞ page and map injectively into the cohomology of the open variety. This injectivity is the geometric incarnation of the fact that the constant term of an Eisenstein series determines the Eisenstein series uniquely. For genus two, the boundary consists of a single divisor isomorphic to the modular curve X(1); the Eisenstein cohomology in degree two is one‑dimensional and corresponds to the classical Eisenstein series of weight k on SL₂(ℤ). For higher genus, the boundary has several components, and the Eisenstein cohomology decomposes according to the Levi subgroups GL(r) × GSp(2g-2r). This decomposition matches the factorisation of the standard L‑function into a product of Rankin–Selberg L‑functions of GL(r) × GL(2g-2r).

The Paramodular Group for Genus Three and Siegel Modular Forms with Non‑Principal Polarization

The paramodular group K(N) ⊂ GSp(6, ℚ) is the stabiliser of a lattice of index N in the standard symplectic lattice ℤ⁶. For genus two, the paramodular conjecture of Brumer–Kramer predicts a bijection between paramodular newforms of level N and abelian surfaces of conductor N with non‑principal polarization. In genus three, the analogous statement relates paramodular Siegel modular forms to abelian threefolds with a polarization of type (1,1,N). The Fourier expansion of a paramodular form is supported on half‑integral symmetric matrices T whose determinant is a multiple of N. The Hecke operators at primes p ∤ N are the usual double cosets, while at p | N the local Hecke algebra is larger and contains the Atkin–Lehner involutions. The space of paramodular cusp forms of weight k and level N admits a Petersson inner product, and the eigenforms satisfy a Ramanujan–Petersson bound conjecturally: the Satake parameters α_i(p) satisfy |α_i(p)| = p(k-3)/2.

Recent computations by Poor–Yuen and others have produced explicit paramodular forms of genus three for small levels using the Borcherds lift. The Borcherds product of a vector‑valued elliptic modular form of weight 1/2 yields a meromorphic Siegel modular form on the paramodular group whose divisor is a Heegner divisor on the paramodular variety. The holomorphic paramodular cusp forms appear as the kernel of the ξ‑operator on the space of harmonic Maass forms of weight k, generalising the genus‑two theory of mock Siegel modular forms. The paramodular variety for genus three is a Shimura variety of Hodge type for the group GSpin(2,3) ≅ GSp(6), and its canonical model over ℚ allows one to define Galois representations attached to paramodular eigenforms. The local‑global compatibility at primes p ∤ N follows from the endoscopic classification for GSp(6) (which is still conjectural in full generality), while at p | N one expects a description in terms of the local Langlands correspondence for the paramodular group, which is a parahoric subgroup of GSp(6, ℚ_p).

Automorphic Periods and the Gan–Gross–Prasad Conjecture for GSp(6)

The Gan–Gross–Prasad (GGP) conjecture for the pair (GSp(6), GSp(4) × GL(2)) predicts a precise relation between the non‑vanishing of a period integral of a cuspidal automorphic representation π of GSp(6) over the subgroup H = GSp(4) × GL(2) (embedded block‑diagonally) and the central value L(1/2, π × τ) where τ is the representation of H corresponding to π under the local Langlands correspondence. The period integral is ∫H(ℚ)H(𝔸) φ(h) dh for φ ∈ π. The conjecture asserts that this integral is non‑zero for some φ if and only if L(1/2, π × τ) ≠ 0 and a certain local epsilon factor condition holds at every place. For GSp(4) the conjecture is a theorem of Ichino–Ikeda (building on work of Waldspurger, Jacquet, and others). For GSp(6) the conjecture is open in general, but significant progress has been made for endoscopic representations.

When π is a Saito–Kurokawa lift from GSp(4) × GL(2), the period integral factorises as a product of a period on GSp(4) (which is known to be non‑zero by the GSp(4) GGP theorem) and a period on GL(2) (which is a classical Rankin–Selberg integral). The L‑function L(s, π × τ) then factorises as L(s, π₁ × τ₁) L(s, π₂ × τ₂) where π₁, π₂ are the constituent representations. The central value is therefore a product of two central values, each of which is known to be non‑zero for generic choices. This provides a large family of test cases for the GSp(6) conjecture. For non‑endoscopic (CAP) representations, such as the Howe–Piatetski‑Shapiro lifts from GL(2)³, the period integral is expected to vanish because the corresponding L‑function has a pole at s = 1/2, violating the epsilon factor condition. The geometry of the period integral is reflected in the cycle on the Siegel modular variety A₃ given by the image of the embedding A₂ × A₁ → A₃ induced by the product polarization. The cohomology class of this cycle pairs with the Siegel modular form attached to π, and the GGP conjecture translates into a statement about the non‑vanishing of this pairing.

  • Confusing the Fourier–Jacobi expansion for genus three with the genus‑two case; the Jacobi forms have index a 2×2 matrix, not a scalar.
  • Assuming every CM point on A_g is defined over the reflex field; the field of definition is the Hilbert class field of the CM field, which can be larger.
  • Thinking Eisenstein cohomology classes are cuspidal; they lie in the image of the boundary map and are orthogonal to cuspidal cohomology.
  • Believing the paramodular group for genus three is the same as the Siegel congruence subgroup Γ₀(N); it is a different parahoric subgroup with a larger Hecke algebra at p | N.
  • Overlooking that the Gan–Gross–Prasad period for GSp(6) involves the subgroup GSp(4) × GL(2), not GSp(2) × GSp(4).
  • Mixing up the standard L‑function of GSp(6) (degree seven) with the spinor L‑function (degree eight); they have different gamma factors and functional equations.
  • Assuming the Borcherds lift always produces a holomorphic paramodular form; it yields a meromorphic form whose divisor is a Heegner divisor, and holomorphicity requires the input vector‑valued form to be a cusp form.

The Construction of Eigenvarieties for GSp(2g) and p‑adic Interpolation of Siegel Modular Forms

The eigenvariety for GSp(2g) is a rigid‑analytic space that parametrises finite‑slope overconvergent Siegel modular forms of varying p‑adic weight. Building on the work of Coleman–Mazur for GL(2) and Buzzard for GL(2) over ℚ, Urban constructed the first eigenvariety for GSp(4) using the theory of overconvergent Siegel modular forms on the Siegel upper half‑space ℋ₂. The weight space is the rigid‑analytic variety Homcont(ℤp×, 𝔾m) ≅ ℤp× × ℤp (for g=2 one obtains a two‑dimensional weight space). Points of the eigenvariety correspond to systems of Hecke eigenvalues (T(p), T₁(p²), …) that satisfy the characteristic polynomial of the Hecke operators acting on the space of overconvergent forms of a given weight and level. The construction uses the theory of p‑adic Banach spaces of overconvergent forms, the compactness of the Up operator (the Siegel analogue of the Atkin–Lehner Up), and the Fredholm determinant to cut out the finite‑slope locus. A key technical input is the existence of a p‑adic analytic family of Siegel Eisenstein series that interpolates the classical Eisenstein series, providing a non‑zero section of the Hodge bundle on the eigenvariety.

For higher genus g ≥ 3 the construction follows the same pattern but requires a more delicate analysis of the boundary of the Siegel modular variety. The work of Andreatta–Iovita–Pilloni extends the theory of overconvergent Siegel modular forms to arbitrary genus using the theory of canonical subgroups for abelian varieties with p‑divisible groups of height 2g. The eigenvariety is then obtained as the rigid‑analytic spectrum of the Hecke algebra acting on the direct limit of spaces of overconvergent forms of increasing radius of overconvergence. One of the main applications is the p‑adic interpolation of the spinor L‑function: for a Hida family of ordinary Siegel eigenforms, the p‑adic L‑function constructed by the doubling method varies analytically on the eigenvariety and satisfies the expected interpolation formula at classical critical points. This provides a powerful tool for studying congruences between Siegel modular forms and for proving cases of the Bloch–Kato conjecture for the spinor motive.

An important subtlety is the distinction between the ordinary locus (where the Up operator acts invertibly) and the finite‑slope locus (where the slope is bounded by a fixed constant). The ordinary locus is a union of irreducible components that are finite flat over the weight space; the full eigenvariety is typically not flat over the weight space because of the presence of “halo” components that appear only at non‑classical weights. The geometry of these components is closely related to the theory of p‑adic families of Galois representations: by the work of Caraiani–Scholze and others, each point of the eigenvariety gives rise to a p‑adic Galois representation of Gℚ into GSp(2g, ℚp) that varies analytically in families. The eigenvariety therefore serves as a bridge between the automorphic side (Siegel modular forms) and the Galois side (p‑adic Galois representations), realising a p‑adic version of the Langlands correspondence for GSp(2g).

The Arithmetic Siegel–Weil Formula and the Kudla–Rapoport Conjecture on Shimura Varieties

The classical Siegel–Weil formula expresses the integral of a theta kernel over an orthogonal group as an Eisenstein series on a symplectic group. In the arithmetic setting, Kudla conjectured that the generating series of arithmetic intersection numbers of special cycles on Shimura varieties of orthogonal type is the Fourier expansion of a Siegel Eisenstein series of parallel weight. Rapoport refined this conjecture for the integral models of Shimura varieties at primes of bad reduction, predicting that the arithmetic intersection pairing of special cycles on the integral model equals the derivative of the Eisenstein series at the central point. The conjecture has been proved in a number of cases: for unitary Shimura varieties of signature (n‑1,1) by Kudla–Rapoport–Yang, for Hilbert modular surfaces by Bruinier–Kudla–Yang, and for the Siegel modular variety of genus two by Yuan–Zhang–Zhang using the arithmetic fundamental lemma. The proof combines the theory of arithmetic theta lifts, the comparison of local densities of quadratic forms, and the analysis of the singularities of the special cycles on the integral model.

On the Siegel modular side, the arithmetic Siegel–Weil formula relates the arithmetic intersection of Heegner divisors (or higher‑codimension cycles) on the Siegel modular variety Ag to the Fourier coefficients of the derivative of a Siegel Eisenstein series of weight g+1. For g=2, the special cycles are the Humbert surfaces parametrising abelian surfaces with real multiplication by a quadratic field; their arithmetic intersection numbers are expressed in terms of the Fourier coefficients of the derivative of the genus‑two Eisenstein series. The formula involves the arithmetic intersection pairing on the minimal compactification of A₂, the contribution of the boundary components (which are modular curves), and the Green currents constructed from the Siegel theta kernel. The Kudla–Rapoport conjecture predicts that the generating series of these arithmetic intersection numbers is a Siegel modular form of weight 3 whose non‑holomorphic part is a Siegel Eisenstein series of weight 3, and that the holomorphic part is a Siegel cusp form (the “arithmetic theta lift”). This provides a geometric interpretation of the Fourier coefficients of Siegel Eisenstein series and connects them to the arithmetic of abelian varieties.

A major open problem is the extension of the arithmetic Siegel–Weil formula to higher genus g ≥ 3 and to Shimura varieties with non‑principal polarization (paramodular varieties). The main difficulties are the increasing complexity of the boundary stratification of the minimal compactification, the need for a good theory of arithmetic theta kernels for higher‑rank dual pairs, and the appearance of higher‑codimension special cycles whose intersection theory is not yet fully developed. Recent work of Howard–Madapusi Pera on the arithmetic fundamental lemma for unitary groups and of Li–Zhang on the arithmetic Siegel–Weil formula for GSpin Shimura varieties suggests that the conjecture holds in great generality, but a complete proof for all Siegel modular varieties remains a central goal of the Kudla program.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: “State the precise relationship between the arithmetic intersection number of two Humbert surfaces on the Siegel modular threefold A₂ and the Fourier coefficients of the derivative of the genus‑two Siegel Eisenstein series of weight 3.” Many candidates answer only with the classical Siegel–Weil formula, omitting the derivative and the boundary contributions.
The correct statement is the arithmetic Siegel–Weil formula for g=2: let Z(m) and Z(n) be Humbert surfaces corresponding to positive‑definite binary quadratic forms of discriminants m and n. Their arithmetic intersection number ⟨Z(m), Z(n)⟩arith on the minimal compactification of A₂ equals the (m,n)‑th Fourier coefficient of the derivative at s=0 of the genus‑two Siegel Eisenstein series E₂(Z, s) of weight 3, plus an explicit boundary term coming from the intersection of the Humbert surfaces with the toroidal boundary divisors. The boundary term is expressed as a sum of arithmetic intersection numbers on the modular curves that form the boundary components, and it involves the Fourier coefficients of the derivative of the genus‑one Eisenstein series. Omitting the derivative or the boundary term yields an incomplete answer.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question mentions “arithmetic Siegel–Weil” or “Kudla–Rapoport”, immediately recall three ingredients: (1) the generating series of arithmetic intersection numbers, (2) the derivative of a Siegel Eisenstein series at the central point, and (3) the boundary contribution on the minimal compactification. Write down the formula in words before attempting any computation; this shows the examiner you understand the structure of the conjecture and avoids the common trap of quoting only the classical (non‑arithmetic) Siegel–Weil formula.

The Gan–Gross–Prasad Conjecture for GSp(8) and Period Integrals on Higher Genus Siegel Modular Varieties

The Gan–Gross–Prasad (GGP) conjecture for the pair (GSp(8), GSp(6) × GL(2)) predicts a precise relation between the non‑vanishing of a period integral of a cuspidal automorphic representation π of GSp(8) over the subgroup H = GSp(6) × GL(2) (embedded block‑diagonally) and the central value L(1/2, π × τ) where τ is the representation of H corresponding to π under the local Langlands correspondence. The period integral is ∫H(ℚ)H(𝔸) φ(h) dh for φ ∈ π. The conjecture asserts that this integral is non‑zero for some φ if and only if L(1/2, π × τ) ≠ 0 and a certain local epsilon factor condition holds at every place. For GSp(4) the conjecture is a theorem of Ichino–Ikeda; for GSp(6) it is open in general but known for endoscopic representations. The step to GSp(8) introduces new phenomena: the relevant L‑function is the Rankin–Selberg convolution of the standard representation of GSp(8) (degree 9) with the tensor product of the standard representation of GSp(6) (degree 7) and the standard representation of GL(2) (degree 2), giving a degree‑18 L‑function whose central point is s = 1/2.

When π is an endoscopic lift from GSp(6) × GL(2) (the analogue of the Saito–Kurokawa lift for higher genus), the period integral factorises as a product of a period on GSp(6) (which is known to be non‑zero by the GSp(6) GGP theorem, conditional on the endoscopic classification) and a period on GL(2) (a classical Rankin–Selberg integral). The L‑function L(s, π × τ) then factorises as L(s, π₁ × τ₁) L(s, π₂ × τ₂) where π₁, π₂ are the constituent representations. The central value is therefore a product of two central values, each of which is known to be non‑zero for generic choices. This provides a large family of test cases for the GSp(8) conjecture. For non‑endoscopic (CAP) representations, such as the Howe–Piatetski‑Shapiro lifts from GL(2)⁴, the period integral is expected to vanish because the corresponding L‑function has a pole at s = 1/2, violating the epsilon factor condition.

The geometry of the period integral is reflected in the cycle on the Siegel modular variety A₄ given by the image of the embedding A₃ × A₁ → A₄ induced by the product polarization. The cohomology class of this cycle pairs with the Siegel modular form attached to π, and the GGP conjecture translates into a statement about the non‑vanishing of this pairing. Recent work of Liu–Zhang on the arithmetic GGP conjecture for unitary groups suggests that the derivative of the L‑function at the central point should be related to the height of the cycle, but for symplectic groups the arithmetic version is still conjectural. The GSp(8) case is particularly important because it is the first genus where the Siegel modular variety has dimension 10 and the boundary of the minimal compactification contains components of codimension one that are themselves Siegel modular varieties of lower genus, making the analysis of the period integral more intricate.

The Moduli Stack of Principally Polarized Abelian Varieties with Full Level N Structure and its Toroidal Compactifications

The moduli stack Ag,N of principally polarized abelian varieties of dimension g with full level N structure (N ≥ 3) is a smooth Deligne–Mumford stack over ℤ[1/N] that represents the functor sending a scheme S to the groupoid of abelian schemes A → S of relative dimension g equipped with a principal polarization λ: A → A∨ and a symplectic isomorphism α: (ℤ/Nℤ)2g → A[N] of finite flat group schemes. The stack is quasi‑projective and its coarse moduli space is the Siegel modular variety of level N. The geometry of Ag,N is governed by the theory of toroidal compactifications: one chooses a rational polyhedral cone decomposition Σ of the cone of positive‑definite symmetric real matrices of size g that is invariant under the action of the principal congruence subgroup Γ(N) ⊂ Sp(2g, ℤ). The resulting toroidal compactification Ag,N(Σ) is a proper smooth Deligne–Mumford stack over ℤ[1/N] with a normal‑crossing boundary divisor whose strata are indexed by the faces of the cones in Σ. The choice of Σ is not unique; different choices give birationally equivalent compactifications related by toroidal blow‑ups.

The boundary strata have a modular interpretation: a stratum corresponding to a face F of a cone parametrises semi‑abelian varieties that are extensions of an abelian variety of dimension g‑rank(F) by a torus of rank rank(F), together with a level structure on the toric part. In particular, the codimension‑one boundary divisors correspond to rank‑one faces and are themselves moduli stacks of principally polarized abelian varieties of dimension g‑1 with level structure (the “Siegel modular varieties of lower genus”). The line bundle of modular forms of weight k on Ag,N extends to a line bundle on the toroidal compactification whose sections are Siegel modular forms of weight k and level N that are holomorphic at the cusps (the Koecher principle). The minimal (Satake) compactification is obtained by taking the Proj of the ring of Siegel modular forms of all weights; it is normal but typically singular along the boundary. The toroidal compactifications provide resolutions of these singularities and are essential for the construction of the Hodge bundle, the Kodaira–Spencer map, and the theory of Eisenstein cohomology.

An important application is the construction of the p‑adic uniformization of Ag,N at primes p ∤ N. By the work of Faltings–Chai and later of Lan–Stroh, the formal completion of Ag,N along its special fibre at p is isomorphic to a quotient of a product of a formal torus and a Rapoport–Zink space for the p‑divisible group of a supersingular abelian variety. This uniformization is used to define overconvergent Siegel modular forms as sections of the Hodge bundle on the rigid‑analytic generic fibre of the formal toroidal compactification. The geometry of the boundary also controls the constant terms of Siegel Eisenstein series: the pullback of an Eisenstein series to a boundary stratum is a product of Eisenstein series of lower genus, a fact that underlies the Langlands constant term formula and the theory of the doubling method for the standard L‑function.

The Stabilization of the Arthur–Selberg Trace Formula for GSp(4) and its Role in Endoscopic Classification

The Arthur–Selberg trace formula for a reductive group G over a number field expresses the spectral side (a sum over automorphic representations) in terms of the geometric side (a sum over conjugacy classes). For G = GSp(4) the trace formula is not stable: orbital integrals on the geometric side depend on the choice of a representative in a stable conjugacy class, and the spectral side contains contributions from representations that are not stable under endoscopic transfer. Stabilization is the process of rewriting the trace formula so that both sides are expressed in terms of stable orbital integrals and stable characters of representations. This requires the introduction of endoscopic groups: for GSp(4) the elliptic endoscopic groups are GL(2) × GL(2) (the split endoscopic group) and the non‑split inner form of GL(2) over a quadratic extension (the non‑split endoscopic group). The stabilized trace formula then takes the form of an identity between a stable spectral expansion (a sum over stable packets of representations of GSp(4) and its endoscopic groups) and a stable geometric expansion (a sum over stable conjugacy classes with stable orbital integrals).

The stabilization for GSp(4) was carried out by Arthur in his monumental work on the endoscopic classification of automorphic representations of classical groups. The key technical inputs are the fundamental lemma for the unit elements of the Hecke algebras of GSp(4) and its endoscopic groups (proved by Ngô for the Lie algebra version and by Waldspurger for the group version), the transfer of orbital integrals (the Langlands–Shelstad transfer), and the construction of the stable trace formula for the endoscopic groups themselves. Once the trace formula is stabilized, one can apply the invariant trace formula to compare the stable trace formula of GSp(4) with that of its endoscopic groups, yielding a classification of the discrete automorphic spectrum of GSp(4) in terms of representations of GL(4) (via the functorial lift to GL(4)) and representations of the endoscopic groups. The result is the endoscopic classification: every discrete automorphic representation of GSp(4) belongs to a global Arthur packet, which is either a singleton (a generic representation), a pair (a Saito–Kurokawa lift from GL(2) × GL(2)), or a larger packet (a Yoshida lift from GL(2) × GL(2) or a CAP representation from a proper parabolic).

The stabilized trace formula also provides a tool for proving multiplicity formulas and for establishing the local‑global compatibility of the Langlands correspondence. For example, the multiplicity of a discrete automorphic representation in the space of cusp forms is given by the size of the associated Arthur packet, which can be read off from the stable trace formula. Moreover, the stable trace formula allows one to transfer automorphic representations between inner forms of GSp(4) (such as the compact inner form PGSp(4) over a definite quaternion algebra) and to relate their trace formulas. This is essential for the proof of the local Langlands correspondence for GSp(4) via the method of “twisted trace formulas” and for the construction of p‑adic L‑functions by interpolating the stable orbital integrals. The stabilization for higher rank groups GSp(2g) with g ≥ 3 is still a major open problem; the endoscopic groups become more numerous (including products of smaller symplectic groups and general linear groups) and the fundamental lemma for the required test functions is not yet known in full generality.

  • Confusing the eigenvariety for GSp(2g) with the Hida ordinary locus; the eigenvariety contains non‑ordinary finite‑slope components that are not flat over the weight space.
  • Assuming the arithmetic Siegel–Weil formula holds without the derivative of the Eisenstein series; the classical Siegel–Weil formula gives only the non‑arithmetic intersection numbers.
  • Overlooking the boundary contribution in the arithmetic intersection pairing on the minimal compactification; the boundary divisors contribute explicit terms involving lower‑genus Eisenstein series.
  • Believing the Gan–Gross–Prasad conjecture for GSp(8) is a direct generalisation of the GSp(4) case without the extra epsilon factor condition at each place.
  • Thinking the toroidal compactification of Ag,N is unique; different admissible cone decompositions give birationally equivalent but distinct compactifications.
  • Neglecting that the stabilized trace formula for GSp(4) requires the fundamental lemma for both split and non‑split endoscopic groups, and that the non‑split case was proved only after Ngô’s work on the Lie algebra version.
  • The stabilized trace formula is the essential bridge that converts the non‑stable geometric side into stable orbital integrals, enabling the endoscopic classification of automorphic representations of GSp(4).

The Torelli Theorem and the Moduli Space of Principally Polarized Abelian Varieties

Siegel modular forms are intrinsically linked to the geometry of abelian varieties, particularly through the concept of the moduli space of principally polarized abelian varieties. A principally polarized abelian variety of dimension g is a complex torus Cg / Λ (where Λ is a lattice) equipped with a specific type of ample line bundle, which induces a non-degenerate alternating form on the lattice Λ with integer values, normalized such that its Pfaffian is 1. The period matrix of such a variety, when a symplectic basis for the homology is chosen, naturally lies in the Siegel upper half-space ℋg.

The Torelli Theorem is a fundamental result that establishes a profound connection between the complex analytic structure of an abelian variety and its period matrix. Specifically, for principally polarized abelian varieties, the global Torelli theorem states that two such varieties are isomorphic if and and only if their period matrices are equivalent under the action of the symplectic modular group Sp(2g, ℤ). In other words, the moduli space Mg of principally polarized abelian varieties of dimension g is identified with the quotient space ℋg / Sp(2g, ℤ). This identification is crucial because it means that Siegel modular forms, which are functions on ℋg invariant under Sp(2g, ℤ), can be interpreted as functions on Mg.

For g = 1, this reduces to the classical case where elliptic curves (1-dimensional abelian varieties) are classified by points in the upper half-plane ℋ1 modulo SL(2, ℤ), and elliptic modular forms are functions on this moduli space. For g ≥ 2, the moduli space Mg is a complex orbifold, and Siegel modular forms are sections of certain line bundles over this space. The compactification of Mg (e.g., the Satake compactification or toroidal compactifications) corresponds to adding "degenerate" abelian varieties, which have a geometric interpretation as products of lower-dimensional abelian varieties and algebraic tori.

The Torelli theorem has several important implications. Firstly, it provides a geometric interpretation for the Fourier coefficients of Siegel modular forms. These coefficients often encode arithmetic information about the number of points on abelian varieties over finite fields or the representation numbers of quadratic forms. Secondly, it allows the application of techniques from algebraic geometry to the study of Siegel modular forms, and vice-versa. For instance, the Kodaira-Spencer map, which relates deformations of complex structures to cohomology, plays a role in understanding the differential geometry of Mg and the structure of the ring of Siegel modular forms. The study of special subvarieties within Mg, such as the Schottky locus (for g = 4, representing Jacobians of curves) or Heegner divisors, often involves specific Siegel modular forms or their periods.

The Symplectic Group's Structure and the Fundamental Domain of the Siegel Upper Half-Space

The Siegel upper half-space ℋg is defined as the set of complex symmetric matrices Z = X + iY of size g × g, where X and Y are real symmetric matrices, and Y is positive definite. The group that acts on this space, and whose invariance defines Siegel modular forms, is the symplectic group Sp(2g, R). This group consists of 2g × 2g real matrices M satisfying MTJM = J, where J is the standard symplectic matrix J = [[0, Ig], [-Ig, 0]]. The action of M = [[A, B], [C, D]] ∈ Sp(2g, R) on Z ∈ ℋg is given by M · Z = (AZ + B)(CZ + D)-1. The subgroup Sp(2g, ℤ) (matrices with integer entries) is the Siegel modular group.

Understanding the structure of Sp(2g, R) is crucial. It is a non-compact, connected, simple Lie group (for g ≥ 1). Its maximal compact subgroup is isomorphic to the unitary group U(g), which can be seen by considering the embedding of U(g) into Sp(2g, R) as matrices of the form [[A, -B], [B, A]] where A + iB ∈ U(g). This maximal compact subgroup stabilizes the point Z = iIg in ℋg. The quotient Sp(2g, R) / U(g) is isomorphic to ℋg, endowing ℋg with the structure of a symmetric space of non-compact type. This geometric structure gives rise to the invariant Siegel metric ds2 = tr(Y-1dZ Y-1dZ̄), which is fundamental for defining invariant differential operators like the Maass-Shimura operators.
