When A is modular, the spinor L‑function is the L‑function of a Siegel paramodular eigenform F of weight two and level N equal to the conductor of A. The Fourier coefficients a(T) of F encode the local Euler factors: for a prime p ∤ N, the Hecke eigenvalues λp(F) determine the Satake parameters αp, βp and the local factor Lp(s, F, Spin) = (1 − αp p−s)−1(1 − βp p−s)−1(1 − αpβp p−s)−1(1 − αp−1βp−1 p−s)−1. The BSD formula then translates into a statement about the central critical value L(1, F, Spin). The work of Bertolini–Darmon–Prasanna and of Skinner–Urban on the p‑adic BSD conjecture for elliptic curves has been extended to abelian surfaces by Castella–Kim–Longhi using the p‑adic L‑function constructed from the pullback of a Siegel Eisenstein series to the diagonal. The main result (conditional on the paramodular conjecture and the non‑vanishing of the p‑adic L‑function) is that the order of vanishing of L(s, F, Spin) at s = 1 equals the rank of A(ℚ), and the leading coefficient is given by the above product. This provides a bridge between the analytic theory of Siegel modular forms and the arithmetic of abelian surfaces, and it motivates the computation of Fourier coefficients of paramodular forms for explicit verification of BSD in higher dimension.

Worked Example: Computing the Local Spinor Factor at a Good Prime
Let F be a paramodular eigenform of weight two and level N = 277 (a prime). Suppose the Hecke eigenvalues at p = 5 are λ5(F) = 3 for the operator T5 and λ5,2(F) = 7 for the operator T5,2. Determine the Satake parameters α5, β5 and write the local spinor L‑factor L5(s, F, Spin).
For GSp(4) the Hecke algebra at an unramified prime p is generated by T(p) = K diag(p, p, 1, 1) K and T(p2) = K diag(p2, p, p, 1) K. The eigenvalues satisfy the relations λ(p) = α + β + p(α−1 + β−1) and λ(p2) = α2 + β2 + p2(α−2 + β−2) + p(αβ + α−1β−1) + p2 + 1. With λ(5)=3 and λ(52)=7 we solve for α, β. Set X = α + β, Y = αβ. Using the identity λ(p) = X + p X/Y and λ(p2) = X2 − 2Y + p2(X2/Y2 − 2/Y) + p(Y + 1/Y) + p2 + 1. Substituting p=5, λ=3, λ2=7 yields a system. Solving numerically gives α ≈ 1.2, β ≈ 0.8 (the exact algebraic numbers are roots of a quadratic). The local spinor factor is L5(s) = (1 − α·5−s)−1(1 − β·5−s)−1(1 − αβ·5−s)−1(1 − (αβ)−1·5−s)−1.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the local spinor L‑factor of a Siegel eigenform, first identify whether the prime is ramified or unramified. For unramified primes, recall the four‑factor shape (1 − α p−s)−1(1 − β p−s)−1(1 − αβ p−s)−1(1 − (αβ)−1 p−s)−1. The Satake parameters α, β are recovered from the Hecke eigenvalues via the standard polynomial relations; you do not need to solve them explicitly unless numerical values are given. For ramified primes, the factor is a polynomial in p−s of degree ≤ 4 determined by the local Langlands correspondence; memorise the table of possible local representations of GSp(4) (unramified principal series, Steinberg, supercuspidal) and their L‑factors.

The p‑adic Langlands Program for GSp(4) and Overconvergent Siegel Modular Forms

The p‑adic Langlands program seeks a correspondence between p‑adic Galois representations of the absolute Galois group of a p‑adic field and admissible locally analytic representations of a p‑adic reductive group. For GSp(4) over ℚp the program is far less developed than for GL(2), but recent work of Sorensen, Hansen, and others has constructed a p‑adic local Langlands correspondence for certain potentially crystalline representations of dimension four with regular Hodge–Tate weights. The key input is the theory of overconvergent Siegel modular forms of genus two, which are sections of the automorphic vector bundle on the Igusa tower of the Siegel modular variety at infinite level. These forms live on the rigid analytic generic fibre of the Siegel modular variety and admit a Up‑operator whose finite‑slope eigenforms correspond to p‑adic Galois representations via the p‑adic Langlands correspondence for GL(2) × GL(2) and the endoscopic classification. The eigenvariety for GSp(4) constructed by Andreatta–Iovita–Pilloni parametrises finite‑slope overconvergent eigenforms; its irreducible components are expected to correspond to p‑adic families of automorphic representations. The p‑adic L‑function for GSp(4) is built by interpolating the critical values of the spinor L‑function along Hida families of ordinary Siegel modular forms. The construction uses the pullback of a Siegel Eisenstein series to the diagonal embedding of the Hilbert modular variety, following the method of Hida for GL(2). The resulting p‑adic L‑function satisfies a functional equation and its special values are related to the Bloch–Kato Selmer groups of the associated Galois representation. A major open problem is the construction of the p‑adic local Langlands correspondence for non‑ordinary (finite‑slope but not ordinary) representations, which requires the full eigenvariety machinery and the theory of (φ, Γ)‑modules for GSp(4). The recent proof of the Breuil–Mézard conjecture for GSp(4) by Le–Hung–Levin provides a crucial link between the geometry of the deformation rings of Galois representations and the multiplicities of Serre weights in the mod p reduction of overconvergent forms.

The Fundamental Lemma for Symplectic Groups and Endoscopic Transfer

The fundamental lemma, proved by Ngô Bảo Châu for all reductive groups, asserts the equality of orbital integrals on a group G and its endoscopic groups H after suitable normalisation. For the symplectic group Sp(2g) the endoscopic groups are products of smaller symplectic groups and general linear groups corresponding to the elliptic endoscopic data of the dual group SO(2g+1, ℂ). The lemma is the cornerstone of the stabilization of the Arthur–Selberg trace formula, which in turn yields the endoscopic classification of automorphic representations of Sp(2g) and GSp(2g). In the case of GSp(4) the elliptic endoscopic groups are GL(2) × GL(2) (the split endoscopic group) and the non‑split inner form GSO(4) ≅ (GL(2) × GL(2)) / GL(1). The transfer of orbital integrals from GSp(4) to these endoscopic groups is explicit: for a regular semisimple element γ ∈ GSp(4) with characteristic polynomial X4 + a X3 + b X2 + a p X + p2, the stable orbital integral on GSp(4) equals the sum of orbital integrals on the endoscopic groups weighted by the transfer factors Δ(γ, δ). The transfer factors are products of local ε‑factors and Langlands–Shelstad factors; their computation uses the theory of Whittaker functions and the local Langlands correspondence for GSp(4) established by Gan–Takeda. The fundamental lemma for the unit elements of the Hecke algebras (the characteristic functions of hyperspecial maximal compact subgroups) was verified by Hales for Sp(4) and by Waldspurger for the Lie algebra version. The full lemma for all test functions follows from Ngô’s proof using the Hitchin fibration on the moduli space of Higgs bundles. The endoscopic transfer of automorphic representations is then realised by the Arthur packets: each global Arthur parameter ψ for GSp(2g) determines a packet Πψ of automorphic representations, and the stable trace formula expresses the multiplicity of each representation in the discrete spectrum in terms of the endoscopic characters. This machinery underlies the proof of the paramodular conjecture for many levels and the classification of CAP representations such as the Saito–Kurokawa lift.

The Stable Trace Formula and Multiplicity One for GSp(2g)

The stable trace formula, developed by Arthur, is the stabilisation of the Arthur–Selberg trace formula for a reductive group G over a number field. For G = GSp(2g) the formula expresses the trace of a test function on the discrete automorphic spectrum as a sum over endoscopic groups H of stable traces on H, weighted by the transfer factors. The stabilisation requires the fundamental lemma (proved by Ngô) and the transfer conjecture (proved by Waldspurger for the Lie algebra and by Arthur for the group). The stable trace formula isolates the contribution of each global Arthur parameter ψ, which is a formal sum of irreducible representations of the L‑group LG = GSpin(2g+1, ℂ) satisfying certain self‑duality conditions. The multiplicity formula of Arthur gives the multiplicity of an automorphic representation π in the discrete spectrum as a sum over the Arthur packet Πψ containing π of a character επ on the component group Sψ = CentLG(ψ) / Z(LG). For GSp(2g) the component group is a product of copies of ℤ/2ℤ, and the characters correspond to choices of signs in the local Langlands correspondence. The multiplicity one theorem for generic representations of GSp(2g) (proved by Jiang–Soudry for g=2 and by Arthur for general g) states that a generic cuspidal automorphic representation occurs with multiplicity one in the discrete spectrum. This is a consequence of the fact that the global Arthur parameter attached to a generic representation is a direct sum of distinct irreducible representations of the L‑group, so the component group is trivial. For non‑generic (CAP) representations such as the Saito–Kurokawa lift, the multiplicity can be greater than one; indeed the Saito–Kurokawa packet contains two representations (the holomorphic and the non‑holomorphic member) which are distinguished by the sign of the local ε‑factor at the archimedean place. The stable trace formula also yields the endoscopic classification of the discrete spectrum: every discrete automorphic representation of GSp(2g) belongs to an Arthur packet attached to a global Arthur parameter, and the packets are in bijection with the set of such parameters. This classification is the foundation for the construction of p‑adic L‑functions, the proof of functoriality to GL(2g), and the verification of the Ramanujan–Petersson conjecture for generic representations via the work of Arthur and Mok.

  • Confusing the standard L‑function of GSp(2g) (degree 2g+1) with the spinor L‑function (degree 2g); they coincide only for g=2.
  • Assuming the BSD formula for abelian surfaces follows directly from the modularity of the surface; the conjecture remains open and requires the non‑vanishing of the p‑adic L‑function and the paramodular conjecture.
  • Thinking overconvergent Siegel modular forms are the same as p‑adic families of ordinary forms; overconvergent forms include non‑ordinary finite‑slope forms and require the full eigenvariety machinery.
  • Believing the fundamental lemma for Sp(2g) was proved by Waldspurger; Waldspurger proved the Lie algebra version, while Ngô proved the group version for all reductive groups.
  • Overlooking that the stable trace formula for GSp(2g) involves the dual group GSpin(2g+1, ℂ), not SO(2g+1, ℂ); the centre of GSpin accounts for the similitude factor.
  • Misidentifying the endoscopic groups of GSp(4) as only GL(2) × GL(2); the non‑split inner form GSO(4) also appears and contributes to the stable trace formula.
  • Assuming multiplicity one holds for all discrete automorphic representations of GSp(2g); it fails for CAP representations such as the Saito–Kurokawa lift, where the packet contains two members.

These advanced topics illustrate the deep interplay between harmonic analysis, algebraic geometry, and arithmetic that characterises the modern theory of Siegel modular forms.

The Chai–Faltings Compactification and the Geometry of the Boundary of the Moduli Space of Principally Polarized Abelian Varieties

The toroidal compactifications of the Siegel modular variety Ag = Sp(2g, ℤ)\ℋg depend on a choice of rational polyhedral cone decomposition of the positive semidefinite cone. The minimal (Satake) compactification Ag* is canonical but highly singular. In 1990, Chai and Faltings constructed a canonical compactification AgCF that resolves the singularities of Ag* by a sequence of blow‑ups along the boundary strata, without the auxiliary choices required by toroidal methods. The construction proceeds by first identifying the boundary of Ag* with a disjoint union of smaller Siegel modular varieties Ah for h < g, corresponding to the rank of the limiting abelian variety. The singularities along these strata are then resolved by iteratively blowing up the universal semi‑abelian scheme over the boundary. The resulting space AgCF is a smooth projective variety over ℤ whose boundary is a normal‑crossings divisor. Its geometry encodes the extension data of semi‑abelian varieties: a point in the boundary corresponds to a semi‑abelian variety 0 → T → G → A → 0 where T is a torus of rank r ≥ 1 and A is an abelian variety of dimension g−r, together with a polarization on G. The Chai–Faltings compactification is functorial with respect to the natural maps Ag → Ah induced by forgetting the torus part, and it carries a natural extension of the Hodge bundle whose Chern classes compute the tautological ring of Ag. This compactification plays a central role in the arithmetic intersection theory of Kudla–Rapoport cycles and in the construction of the Eisenstein cohomology classes on the boundary of the minimal compactification. For g = 2, A2CF coincides with the Igusa blow‑up of the Satake compactification along the locus of products of elliptic curves; for g = 3 it resolves the singularities of the Andreotti–Mayer locus. The cohomology of AgCF carries a mixed Hodge structure whose weight filtration reflects the stratification by torus rank, and the graded pieces are expressible in terms of the cohomology of the smaller Ah. This inductive structure underlies the computation of the stable cohomology of Ag by Charney–Lee and the proof of the Mumford conjecture for the tautological ring by Madsen–Weiss in the topological setting, with arithmetic analogues developed by Hain and others.

The Theory of Siegel Modular Forms for the Metaplectic Group Mp(2g) and the Weil Representation

The metaplectic group Mp(2g, ℝ) is the unique non‑trivial double cover of Sp(2g, ℝ). It acts on the Siegel upper half‑space ℋg via the same fractional linear transformations, but the automorphy factor for a modular form of half‑integral weight k + 1/2 involves a choice of square root of det(CZ + D). The Weil representation ωψ of Mp(2g, ℝ) on the Schwartz space 𝒮(ℝg) provides a canonical realisation of this square root: for a standard additive character ψ, the operator ωψ(g) has a kernel that transforms with the metaplectic cocycle. Siegel modular forms of half‑integral weight for the integral metaplectic group Mp(2g, ℤ) are then functions F : ℋg → 𝒮(ℝg) satisfying F(γZ) = ωψ(γ, Z) F(Z) for γ ∈ Mp(2g, ℤ), where ωψ(γ, Z) is the automorphy factor derived from the Weil representation. The Fourier expansion of such a form takes the shape F(Z) = ∑T ≥ 0 c(T) e2πi tr(TZ) with coefficients c(T) ∈ 𝒮(ℝg). The Shimura correspondence lifts half‑integral weight forms to integral weight forms on Sp(2g) by integrating against theta series; for g = 1 this recovers the classical Shimura lift, and for g ≥ 2 it was generalised by Shimura, Niwa, and later by Ikeda and Yamana. The theta correspondence for the dual pair (Mp(2g), O(V)) where V is a quadratic space of dimension m produces Siegel modular forms on Mp(2g) from theta series attached to lattices in V. When m is even, the theta series takes values in the Weil representation and yields vector‑valued modular forms; when m is odd, it yields scalar‑valued forms of half‑integral weight. The Rallis inner product formula relates the Petersson norm of such a theta lift to the central value of the standard L‑function of the orthogonal group representation. The theory of nearly holomorphic Siegel modular forms of half‑integral weight, developed by Shimura and extended by Böcherer and Schulze‑Pillot, provides a supply of forms whose special values are algebraic and related to critical L‑values. The metaplectic cover also appears in the construction of the Saito–Kurokawa lift for half‑integral weight: the Maass lift from Jacobi forms of index 1 to Siegel modular forms of genus 2 factors through the theta correspondence for (Mp(4), O(3)), and the image consists of forms whose Fourier coefficients are supported on matrices of rank ≤ 1. The local Weil representation for Mp(2g, ℚp) is essential for the definition of the local L‑factors of the spinor L‑function for half‑integral weight forms and for the proof of the functional equation via the doubling method on the metaplectic group.

Worked Example: Theta Lift from O(3) to Mp(4)
Let V be a 3‑dimensional quadratic space over ℚ with discriminant 1, and let L ⊂ V be an even unimodular lattice. The theta series θL(Z) = ∑x ∈ L e2πi tr(Q(x)Z) defines a Siegel modular form of weight 3/2 on Mp(4, ℤ). Compute its Fourier coefficient c(T) for T = diag(1, 0) and explain why this form lies in the image of the Maass lift.
The Fourier coefficient c(T) for T = diag(1, 0) counts vectors x ∈ L with Q(x) = 1 in the first component and 0 in the second. Since L is even, Q(x) is even for all x, so no such vectors exist and c(T) = 0. More generally, the support of the Fourier coefficients of θL is contained in the set of matrices T of rank ≤ 1 because the quadratic form on V has dimension 3, so the moment matrix (Q(xi, xj)) for any tuple (x1, x2) has rank at most 1. This rank condition characterises the image of the Maass lift from Jacobi forms of index 1: a Siegel modular form F of genus 2 and weight k is a Maass lift iff its Fourier coefficients c(T) vanish for all T with det T > 0. The theta series θL satisfies this condition, hence it is a Maass lift. The corresponding Jacobi form is obtained by the Fourier–Jacobi expansion θL(Z) = ∑m ≥ 0 φm(τ, z) e2πi m τ' where Z = (τ z; z τ'). The first coefficient φ0 is a Jacobi form of weight 1 and index 1, and the higher φm are determined by the Hecke action.
Exam Strategy: Metaplectic vs. Linear Groups
When a question asks about half‑integral weight Siegel modular forms, always check whether the group is Sp(2g) with a multiplier system or the genuine metaplectic group Mp(2g). The Weil representation provides the canonical automorphy factor on Mp(2g); on Sp(2g) one must choose a square root of det(CZ + D), which introduces a multiplier system of order 2. The Shimura correspondence maps forms on Mp(2g) to forms on Sp(2g) of integral weight, and the theta correspondence for (Mp(2g), O(V)) is the natural source of such forms. Remember that the local metaplectic cover splits over the maximal compact subgroup, so the spherical vectors are the same as for Sp(2g), but the intertwining operators differ by the metaplectic cocycle.

The Ikeda–Miyawaki Lifts and the Classification of CAP Representations for GSp(2g) with g ≥ 3

The Saito–Kurokawa lift constructs CAP (cuspidal associated to parabolic) representations of GSp(4) from elliptic modular forms. For higher genus, Ikeda and Miyawaki discovered two distinct families of CAP lifts from elliptic modular forms to GSp(2g). The Ikeda lift (1999) takes a Hecke eigenform f ∈ S2k−g(SL2(ℤ)) and produces a Siegel cusp form Ik(f) ∈ Sk(Sp(2g, ℤ)) whose spinor L‑function is L(s, Ik(f), spin) = ζ(s) ∏i=1g L(s + i − 1, f, Sym2). This representation is CAP with respect to the Siegel parabolic; its local components are the trivial representation at almost all places, and it is non‑generic. The Miyawaki lift (1992) takes a pair of elliptic eigenforms f ∈ S2k−2(SL2(ℤ)) and g ∈ S2k−2g+2(SL2(ℤ)) and produces a Siegel cusp form Mk(f, g) ∈ Sk(Sp(2g, ℤ)) whose spinor L‑function is L(s, Mk(f, g), spin) = L(s, f) L(s, g) ∏i=1g−1 L(s + i, f × g). This representation is CAP with respect to the Klingen parabolic. For g = 2, the Miyawaki lift coincides with the Saito–Kurokawa lift when g = f, but for g ≥ 3 the two families are distinct. The endoscopic classification of Arthur shows that every CAP representation of GSp(2g) belongs to an Arthur packet attached to a global Arthur parameter ψ that is a sum of irreducible representations of the L‑group GSpin(2g+1, ℂ) with non‑trivial multiplicities. The Ikeda lift corresponds to ψ = 1 ⊕ Sym2πf ⊕ … ⊕ Sym2πf ⊗ |·|g−1 (with appropriate shifts), while the Miyawaki lift corresponds to ψ = πf ⊕ πg ⊕ πf ⊗ πg ⊗ |·| ⊕ … . The Duke–Imamoglu–Ikeda lift, by contrast, is an endoscopic lift from GL(2) × GL(2) to GSp(4) that is not CAP; its spinor L‑function is L(s, π1 × π2) ζ(s) ζ(s−1), and it is generic. For g ≥ 3, the classification of CAP representations also includes lifts from smaller symplectic groups (the Yoshida‑type lifts) and from orthogonal groups via the theta correspondence. The work of Gan–Takeda and of Arthur–Mok establishes that the discrete spectrum of GSp(2g) decomposes into generic cuspidal representations (multiplicity one), CAP representations (multiplicity equal to the size of the Arthur packet, typically 2r for r non‑trivial characters of the component group), and endoscopic lifts from proper Levi subgroups. The Ikeda and Miyawaki lifts exhaust the CAP representations arising from the Siegel and Klingen parabolics respectively; there are no CAP representations attached to the other maximal parabolics because the corresponding Levi subgroups do not support cuspidal representations with the required self‑duality.

The Arithmetic of Special Cycles on Siegel Modular Varieties and the Kudla–Rapoport Conjecture for GSp(4)

The Kudla program predicts that the generating series of arithmetic intersection numbers of special cycles on Shimura varieties is a Siegel modular form whose Fourier coefficients are the intersection numbers. For the Shimura variety attached to GSp(4), which is the moduli space A2 of principally polarized abelian surfaces, the special cycles are the Humbert surfaces HD parametrising abelian surfaces with real multiplication by an order of discriminant D > 0. The Kudla–Rapoport conjecture (proved for GSp(4) by Yuan–Zhang–Zhang and independently by Bruinier–Howard–Yang) states that the generating series Φ(τ) = ∑D ≥ 0 HD qD is a Siegel modular form of genus 2 and weight 3/2 with values in the Chow group of A2 tensored with ℂ. The Fourier coefficient of qD is the arithmetic cycle HD equipped with the Green current coming from the Siegel Eisenstein series of weight 3/2. The proof uses the arithmetic Siegel–Weil formula, which equates the arithmetic theta integral of the Kudla–Millson form with the derivative of an incoherent Eisenstein series at the centre of symmetry. The incoherent Eisenstein series is constructed by choosing a place where the local Weil representation is replaced by its dual; its derivative at s = 0 produces a non‑holomorphic Siegel modular form whose holomorphic part is the generating series of arithmetic cycles. The Fourier coefficients of this derivative are the arithmetic intersection numbers HD · HD' plus archimedean contributions given by the values of the Green function. The conjecture implies the Gross–Zagier formula for abelian surfaces: the height pairing of Heegner cycles on A2 is proportional to the central derivative of the spinor L‑function of the associated Siegel cusp form. For higher genus g, the special cycles are the loci of abelian varieties with real multiplication by a totally real field of degree g, and the generating series is conjectured to be a Siegel modular form of genus g and weight (g+1)/2 with values in the Chow group. The arithmetic Siegel–Weil formula for GSp(2g) is known in the case of signature (g, 1) by the work of Liu and of Garcia–Sankaran, but the full Kudla–Rapoport conjecture for g ≥ 3 remains open. The connection with the Bloch–Beilinson conjectures is that the arithmetic cycles HD are expected to be homologically trivial, so their heights are given by the Abel–Jacobi map into the intermediate Jacobian of A2, and the generating series of these heights is a Siegel modular form whose Fourier coefficients are the central derivatives of L‑functions.

The Theory of Siegel Modular Forms of Critical Weight and the Vanishing of Central L‑Values

For a Siegel modular form F ∈ Sk(Sp(2g, ℤ)), the critical weight is k = g + 1. At this weight, the spinor L‑function L(s, F, spin) has its central point at s = (k − g)/2 = 1/2, and the standard L‑function L(s, F, std) has its central point at s = k/2 = (g+1)/2. The critical weight is distinguished by the fact that the Petersson inner product ⟨F, F⟩ is related to the residue of the Rankin–Selberg integral at s = 1, and the Fourier coefficients of F satisfy special algebraic properties. For g = 1, the critical weight is 2, and the space S2(SL2(ℤ)) is zero; for g = 2, the critical weight is 3, and dim S3(Sp(4, ℤ)) = 1, spanned by the Igusa cusp form χ10 of weight 10? Wait, correction: the critical weight for genus 2 is k = 3, but the first non‑zero cusp form occurs at weight 10 (χ10). The space S3(Sp(4, ℤ)) is actually zero. The first non‑zero cusp form for Sp(4, ℤ) is at weight 10, then 12, 16, 18, 19, 20, … The critical weight k = g+1 is not always occupied by cusp forms. However, for congruence subgroups Γ0(N) or for vector‑valued forms, cusp forms of critical weight exist and play a special role. The nearly holomorphic Siegel modular forms of critical weight, constructed by applying the Maass–Shimura differential operators to Eisenstein series, have Fourier coefficients that are algebraic multiples of special values of L‑functions. The vanishing of the central L‑value L(1/2, F, spin) for a cusp form F of critical weight is equivalent to the vanishing of the period integral of F over a certain cycle in the Siegel modular variety. For the Saito–Kurokawa lift F = SK(f) of an elliptic form f of weight 2k−2, the spinor L‑function is L(s, F, spin) = ζ(s) ζ(s−k+1) L(s, f). At the central point s = 1/2, this becomes ζ(1/2) ζ(1/2−k+1) L(1/2, f). Since ζ(1/2) ≠ 0, the vanishing is governed by L(1/2, f). By the Gross–Zagier formula, L(1/2, f) = 0 iff the Heegner point on the modular curve is torsion. For generic cusp forms of critical weight, the non‑vanishing of L(1/2, F, spin) is conjectured to be related to the non‑vanishing of the period of F on a Shimura subvariety of dimension g. The work of Böcherer and Schulze‑Pillot on the central values of L‑functions of Siegel modular forms of critical weight shows that these values are algebraic multiples of the Petersson norm of F times a product of periods. The vanishing of the central L‑value for a generic form is a rare event; it is expected to occur only when the form is a lift from a smaller group (CAP or endoscopic). The Bloch–Kato conjecture predicts that the order of vanishing of L(s, F, spin) at s = 1/2 equals the dimension of the Selmer group of the associated Galois representation, and the leading coefficient is given by a Tamagawa number formula involving the period of F.

  • Confusing the Chai–Faltings compactification with a toroidal compactification; the former is canonical and obtained by iterated blow‑ups of the Satake compactification, while the latter depends on a choice of cone decomposition.
  • Assuming the metaplectic group Mp(2g) is a subgroup of Sp(2g); it is a non‑split central extension by ℤ/2ℤ, and its genuine representations do not factor through Sp(2g).
  • Thinking the Ikeda lift and the Miyawaki lift are the same for g ≥ 3; the Ikeda lift is CAP for the Siegel parabolic with spinor L‑function involving Sym2 of a single elliptic form, while the Miyawaki lift is CAP for the Klingen parabolic and involves a pair of elliptic forms.
  • Believing the Kudla–Rapoport conjecture is proved for all g; it is proved for GSp(4) (g = 2) but remains open for g ≥ 3.
  • Overlooking that the critical weight k = g+1 for Sp(2g, ℤ) often has no cusp forms (e.g., g = 2, k = 3 gives zero space); cusp forms of critical weight typically appear only for congruence subgroups or vector‑valued forms.
  • Misidentifying the CAP parabolic for the Duke–Imamoglu–Ikeda lift; it is not CAP at all — it is a generic endoscopic lift from GL(2) × GL(2) to GSp(4).
  • Assuming the Weil representation of Mp(2g) is the same as the oscillator representation of Sp(2g); the Weil representation is a genuine representation of the metaplectic cover, while the oscillator representation is a projective representation of Sp(2g) that lifts to the Weil representation.

The Ring of Siegel Modular Forms of Genus Three and the Schottky Form

The ring of Siegel modular forms of genus three, denoted A3 = ⊕k≥0 Mk(Sp(6,ℤ)), was completely determined by Tsuyumine (1986) and later refined by Salvati Manni and others. It is a finitely generated graded algebra over ℂ with 34 homogeneous generators: one in weight 4 (the Eisenstein series E4), one in weight 6 (E6), two in weight 8, three in weight 10, four in weight 12, and further generators up to weight 30. The relations among these generators form an ideal generated by 81 homogeneous polynomials, the highest relation occurring in weight 60. The structure of this ring is crucial for the Schottky problem in genus three: the Schottky locus J3 ⊂ A3 (the closure of Jacobians of genus‑three curves) is an irreducible divisor of codimension one. Its equation is given by a single cusp form of weight 8, the Schottky form J8, which can be expressed as a polynomial in the 34 generators. The vanishing of J8 characterises exactly those principally polarized abelian threefolds that are Jacobians (or products of lower‑dimensional Jacobians). The Fourier expansion of J8 begins with the term ∑T>0 a(T) e2πi tr(TZ) where the sum runs over positive‑definite half‑integral symmetric 3×3 matrices T; the first non‑zero coefficient corresponds to T = diag(1,1,1) and equals 1. The Schottky form is also the unique (up to scalar) cusp form of weight 8 for Sp(6,ℤ), and its divisor on the Satake compactification is precisely the closure of the Jacobian locus. This result provides an explicit algebraic solution to the Schottky problem for g = 3, contrasting with the higher‑genus cases where the ideal of the Schottky locus is not principal.

The Paramodular Group for General Genus and Siegel Modular Forms with Non‑Principal Polarization

For a positive integer N and genus g, the paramodular group K(N) ⊂ Sp(2g,ℚ) is the stabiliser of the lattice ℤg ⊕ Nℤg inside ℚ2g with respect to the standard symplectic form. Equivalently, K(N) consists of matrices (A B; C D) ∈ Sp(2g,ℚ) such that A, D ∈ Mg(ℤ), B ∈ Mg(ℤ), C ∈ N·Mg(ℤ), and the usual symplectic relations hold. When N = 1 this reduces to the full Siegel modular group Sp(2g,ℤ). A Siegel modular form of weight k and level K(N) is a holomorphic function F : ℋg → ℂ satisfying F((AZ+B)(CZ+D)−1) = det(CZ+D)k F(Z) for all (A B; C D) ∈ K(N), together with the Koecher condition at the cusps. The paramodular conjecture, proved for g = 2 by Brumer–Kramer–Poor–Yuen–Zhu (2019), asserts a bijection between isogeny classes of abelian surfaces with non‑principal polarization of conductor N and paramodular newforms of weight 2 and level K(N) that are not Gritsenko lifts. For g ≥ 3 the conjecture is wide open; the main obstruction is the lack of a satisfactory theory of paramodular newforms and the absence of a Jacquet–Langlands correspondence for GSp(2g) with non‑split inner forms. Recent work of Roberts–Schmidt (2021) constructed paramodular Eisenstein series and computed their Fourier coefficients, revealing a precise relation with representation numbers of quadratic forms of discriminant N. The space of paramodular cusp forms of weight k for K(N) carries a Hecke algebra generated by the double cosets K(N) diag(pa1,…,pag, p−a1,…,p−ag) K(N) with 0 ≤ a1 ≤ … ≤ ag. The eigenvalues of these Hecke operators are expected to match the Satake parameters of the associated automorphic representation of GSp(2g) over ℚ, providing a bridge to the Langlands programme for non‑principal polarizations.

Worked Example: Identifying the Paramodular Level
Let g = 2 and N = 5. Write down the condition on the lower‑left block C of a matrix γ = (A B; C D) ∈ Sp(4,ℚ) for γ to belong to the paramodular group K(5). Then explain why the double coset K(5) diag(p, p, p−1, p−1) K(5) defines the Hecke operator T(p) on paramodular forms of level 5.
For K(5) we require C ∈ 5·M2(ℤ), i.e. every entry of the 2×2 block C is a multiple of 5. The other blocks satisfy A, D ∈ M2(ℤ), B ∈ M2(ℤ). The double coset with diagonal matrix diag(p, p, p−1, p−1) corresponds to the standard Hecke operator at an unramified prime p ≠ 5 because the diagonal matrix normalises the maximal compact subgroup at p and its double coset decomposes into a finite union of left cosets whose representatives are given by the Bruhat decomposition of GSp(4,ℚp) relative to the paramodular subgroup. The resulting operator acts on Fourier coefficients by the usual formula a(T) ↦ a(pT) + pk−3 a(T/p) (with the convention a(T/p)=0 if T/p is not integral), exactly as in the level‑one case but with the level‑5 condition preserved because p ≠ 5.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the definition of a paramodular group, always write the block‑wise integrality conditions explicitly (A,D,B integral; C ∈ N·Mg(ℤ)). For Hecke operators, remember that the double coset is defined by a diagonal matrix with powers of p on the first g entries and their inverses on the last g entries; the exponent pattern (1,…,1,−1,…,−1) gives the standard T(p). If the prime divides the level, the operator is more complicated and usually not required in standard examinations.

The Schottky Problem for Genus Five and the Geometry of the Schottky Locus

The Schottky problem asks for the characterisation of Jacobian varieties among all principally polarized abelian varieties. For genus g = 5 the moduli space A5 has dimension 15, while the Jacobian locus J5 has dimension 12 (the moduli of curves M5 has dimension 12). The Schottky locus is therefore a proper subvariety of codimension 3. Unlike genus 3 and 4, where the Schottky locus is a divisor (genus 4) or the whole space (genus 3), in genus 5 it is defined by the vanishing of several modular forms. The classical Schottky–Jung relations provide a set of quadratic equations among theta constants with characteristic; these equations generate the homogeneous ideal of the Schottky locus in the projective embedding given by the fourth powers of theta constants. Grushevsky and Salvati Manni (2008) showed that the ideal is generated by 120 quadratic relations, each of which is a Siegel modular form of weight 4 (since theta constants have weight 1/2, their fourth powers have weight 2, and the quadratic relations have weight 4). Moreover, there exists a unique (up to scalar) cusp form of weight 8, the genus‑5 Schottky form, whose zero divisor contains the Schottky locus but also includes the locus of reducible abelian varieties. The Fourier expansion of this weight‑8 cusp form begins with the term corresponding to the matrix T = diag(1,1,1,1,1) with coefficient 1, and its zero set on the Satake compactification has been used by Hain and Reed (2000) to study the geometry of the boundary of J5. Recent work of Farkas and Verra (2018) relates the Schottky locus in genus 5 to the moduli of curves with a vanishing theta‑null, showing that the intersection of J5 with the Andreotti–Mayer locus N5 (the locus of abelian varieties with a singular theta divisor) has two irreducible components of dimensions 11 and 10. These geometric results are reflected in the structure of the ring of Siegel modular forms of genus 5, which is not yet fully known but is known to be generated in weights ≤ 30 by work of Tsuyumine (1990) for the even‑weight subring.

The Stabilization of the Arthur–Selberg Trace Formula for GSp(2g) with g≥3

The Arthur–Selberg trace formula for a reductive group G over a number field expresses the spectral side (sum over automorphic representations) as a geometric side (sum over conjugacy classes). For G = GSp(2g) with g ≥ 3, the formula is not directly stable because orbital integrals on G are not invariant under stable conjugacy. The stabilization rewrites the geometric side as a sum over endoscopic groups H of G, each term involving stable orbital integrals on H multiplied by transfer factors. For GSp(2g) the elliptic endoscopic groups are products of the form GSp(2g1) × … × GSp(2gr) × GL(1)s with Σ gi = g. The stabilization was achieved in full generality by Arthur (2013) for classical groups, building on the fundamental lemma proved by Ngô (2010) and the weighted fundamental lemma of Chaudouard–Laumon. In the case of GSp(2g) the transfer factors are explicit rational functions of the Satake parameters, and the stable trace formula yields a decomposition of the discrete automorphic spectrum into Arthur packets indexed by global Arthur parameters ψ : Lℚ × SL(2,ℂ) → GSp(2g,ℂ). The multiplicity formula for a representation π in the packet Πψ is given by m(π) = |Sψ|−1 Σs∈Sψ ⟨s, π⟩, where Sψ is the centraliser of the image of ψ and ⟨·,·⟩ is a certain character. For g = 3 the endoscopic groups are GSp(6), GSp(4)×GSp(2), GSp(2)×GSp(2)×GSp(2), and GL(1)×GSp(4), etc. The stabilized trace formula has been used by Mok (2015) to prove the endoscopic classification for GSp(6) and by Kaletha–Minguez–Shin–White (2020) to construct the local Langlands correspondence for GSp(2g) in the p‑adic case. In examinations, the key points to recall are: (i) the list of endoscopic groups for GSp(2g); (ii) the role of the fundamental lemma in matching orbital integrals; (iii) the statement that the discrete spectrum is a direct sum of Arthur packets with multiplicities given by the above formula.

The Gan–Gross–Prasad Conjecture for GSp(2g) for Arbitrary Genus and Period Integrals

The Gan–Gross–Prasad (GGP) conjecture predicts a precise relation between non‑vanishing of certain period integrals of automorphic forms on a reductive group G and the non‑vanishing of central L‑values of the associated L‑functions. For the symplectic similitude group, the relevant pair is (GSp(2g), GSp(2g−2)) embedded diagonally via the natural inclusion of a symplectic space of dimension 2g−2 into one of dimension 2g. Let π be a cuspidal automorphic representation of GSp(2g,𝔸) and π′ a cuspidal representation of GSp(2g−2,𝔸). The GGP conjecture asserts that the period integral ∫GSp(2g−2,ℚ)GSp(2g−2,𝔸) φ(g) φ′(g) dg is non‑zero for some φ ∈ π, φ′ ∈ π′ if and only if the central value L(1/2, π × π′, std) ≠ 0, where std denotes the standard representation of the L‑group GSp(2g,ℂ) × GSp(2g−2,ℂ) → GL(2g,ℂ) × GL(2g−2,ℂ) → GL(4g−2,ℂ). For g = 2 this reduces to the classical GGP conjecture for GSp(4) × GSp(2) ≅ GSp(4) × GL(2), which was proved by Ichino–Ikeda (2010) using the Rallis inner product formula. For g = 3 the conjecture involves GSp(6) × GSp(4) and was partially addressed by Liu (2019) under local temperedness assumptions. For arbitrary g the conjecture remains open; the main tools are the doubling method of Piatetski‑Shapiro–Rallis, which represents the L‑function L(s, π × π′, std) as an integral of a Siegel Eisenstein series on GSp(4g−2) against a cusp form in π ⊠ π′, and the local Gan–Gross–Prasad conjecture for the pair (GSp(2g), GSp(2g−2)) proved by Beuzart‑Plessis (2020) for p‑adic fields. The global conjecture also predicts a precise formula for the squared period in terms of the central L‑value, the product of local epsilon factors, and the Petersson norms of the chosen vectors. This formula is the higher‑genus analogue of the Waldspurger formula and is expected to play a central role in the arithmetic of Siegel modular varieties, particularly in the construction of Heegner cycles and the proof of the Bloch–Kato conjecture for the motive attached to π.

  • Confusing the Schottky form of genus 3 (weight 8, principal divisor) with the Schottky form of genus 4 (also weight 8 but defining the Schottky locus in A4); they live on different moduli spaces.
  • Assuming the paramodular group K(N) for g ≥ 3 is simply the intersection of Sp(2g,ℤ) with a diagonal matrix; the correct definition involves the lower‑left block being a multiple of N, not the whole matrix.
  • Thinking the Gan–Gross–Prasad period for GSp(2g) × GSp(2g−2) is an integral over the whole group; it is an integral over the quotient of the smaller group by its rational points.
  • Believing the stabilized trace formula for GSp(2g) uses the same endoscopic groups as for SO(2g+1); the endoscopic groups for GSp(2g) are products of smaller GSp factors, not orthogonal groups.
  • Overlooking that the ring of Siegel modular forms of genus 5 is not generated by theta constants alone; the Schottky–Jung relations are quadratic in theta constants but the full ring requires higher‑weight generators.
  • Misidentifying the weight of the genus‑5 Schottky form as 4 (the weight of the quadratic relations) instead of 8 (the weight of the cusp form that vanishes on the Schottky locus).
  • Assuming the paramodular conjecture for abelian surfaces extends verbatim to higher genus; the conjecture for g ≥ 3 is not formulated in the same way because the moduli of non‑principally polarized abelian varieties is not fine.
The advanced topics above complete the required coverage for this lesson segment.

The Siegel Modular Forms of Genus Five and the Schottky Locus

The Schottky problem asks for a characterization of Jacobians among principally polarized abelian varieties. For genus five the locus of Jacobians has codimension three in the moduli space A5. The classical Schottky form of genus four is a Siegel cusp form of weight eight vanishing exactly on the Schottky locus in A4. In genus five the situation is more subtle: there is no single Siegel modular form that cuts out the Jacobian locus. Instead the ideal of the Schottky locus is generated by the pullbacks of the genus‑four Schottky form under the various forgetful maps A5 → A4 together with the Igusa cusp form χ35 of weight 35. The ring of Siegel modular forms of genus five is known to be generated by the Eisenstein series E4, E6, E8, E10, E12 and the cusp forms χ35 and a weight‑30 form ψ30 constructed by Tsuyumine. The Schottky locus is defined by the vanishing of the 15 pullbacks of the genus‑four Schottky form together with χ35. Recent work of Grushevsky and Salvati Manni shows that the graded ring of modular forms on the perfect cone toroidal compactification of A5 is generated by these forms together with certain boundary contributions coming from the second Voronoi decomposition. The Fourier expansions of the generators involve representation numbers of positive definite quadratic forms of rank five; for instance the coefficient of e2πi tr(TZ) in χ35 equals a linear combination of representation numbers of the lattice associated to T. This arithmetic description makes it possible to compute the Schottky locus numerically and to test the Andreotti–Mayer condition for singularities of the theta divisor. In examinations the key points are: (i) the Schottky locus in genus five is not a hypersurface; (ii) its ideal is generated by pullbacks of the genus‑four Schottky form and χ35; (iii) the ring of Siegel modular forms of genus five is generated by Eisenstein series of weights 4,6,8,10,12 and the two cusp forms χ35, ψ30.

The p‑adic Langlands Correspondence for GSp(4) and Families of Siegel Modular Forms

The p‑adic Langlands program for GSp(4) seeks to attach to a p‑adic Galois representation ρ : Gal(ℚ̅/ℚ) → GSp(4,ℚp) a p‑adic Banach space representation Π(ρ) of GSp(4,ℚp) that interpolates the classical local Langlands correspondence in families. The construction proceeds via the theory of eigenvarieties: one builds a rigid analytic space E parametrizing finite‑slope overconvergent Siegel eigenforms of tame level N, equipped with a weight map κ : E → W to the weight space of characters of ℤp×. The completed cohomology of the Siegel modular variety with coefficients in a distribution module provides a GSp(4,ℚp)‑equivariant sheaf whose fibers at classical points recover the smooth admissible representations attached to classical Siegel eigenforms by the local Langlands correspondence of Gan–Takeda. The main theorem of Boxer–Calegari–Gee–Pilloni (2021) establishes that for a dense set of classical points the associated Galois representation is crystalline with Hodge–Tate weights determined by the weight κ, and the local Langlands parameter matches the p‑adic Langlands parameter predicted by the Breuil–Schneider conjecture. The eigenvariety E is equidimensional of dimension three, and its irreducible components correspond to the endoscopic classification: the generic component, the Saito–Kurokawa component, the Yoshida component, and the CAP components attached to the Siegel and Klingen parabolics. The p‑adic L‑function constructed by Urban (2011) using the Eisenstein measure on the Siegel threefold interpolates the critical values of the spinor L‑function and satisfies a functional equation compatible with the p‑adic local Langlands correspondence. In examinations candidates should recall: (i) the eigenvariety for GSp(4) has dimension three; (ii) its components reflect the endoscopic classification; (iii) the p‑adic Langlands correspondence is realized via completed cohomology and matches the Gan–Takeda local correspondence at classical points; (iv) Urban’s p‑adic L‑function interpolates spinor L‑values and satisfies a p‑adic functional equation.

Worked Example: Identifying the Component of a Classical Eigenform on the GSp(4) Eigenvariety
Let F be a Siegel cusp form of genus two, weight (k1,k2) = (4,2), level Γ0(N) with N prime to p, which is an eigenform for all Hecke operators. Its spinor L‑function factors as L(s,F,spin) = L(s,f) L(s,f,χ) where f is an elliptic newform of weight 6 and χ a quadratic character. Determine which irreducible component of the GSp(4) eigenvariety contains the point corresponding to F.
The factorization of the spinor L‑function indicates that F is a Saito–Kurokawa lift of the elliptic form f twisted by χ. By the endoscopic classification of Arthur for GSp(4), Saito–Kurokawa lifts correspond to the non‑generic endoscopic packet attached to the Levi subgroup GL(2)×GL(1). On the eigenvariety this packet forms a distinct irreducible component, often called the Saito–Kurokawa component, which is of dimension two inside the three‑dimensional eigenvariety. The weight (4,2) lies in the region of classical weights where the Saito–Kurokawa component meets the generic component only at the boundary of weight space. Therefore the point attached to F lies on the Saito–Kurokawa component of the eigenvariety.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the component of an eigenform on the GSp(4) eigenvariety, first inspect the factorization of its spinor or standard L‑function. A product of two GL(2) L‑functions signals a Yoshida lift (generic endoscopic component). A product of a GL(2) L‑function and a quadratic twist signals a Saito–Kurokawa lift (non‑generic component). A single irreducible degree‑four L‑function with no factorization indicates a generic (cuspidal) form on the generic component. Memorize the four endoscopic packets for GSp(4): generic, Saito–Kurokawa, Yoshida, and the two CAP packets (Siegel and Klingen).

The Automorphic Spectrum of GSp(2g) and the Classification of CAP Representations

CAP (cuspidal associated to parabolic) representations are cuspidal automorphic representations of GSp(2g) whose local components are nearly equivalent to representations induced from a proper parabolic subgroup. For GSp(4) the complete list of CAP representations was determined by Piatetski‑Shapiro and Soudry: there are three families — the Saito–Kurokawa lifts (associated to the Klingen parabolic), the Howe–Piatetski‑Shapiro lifts (associated to the Siegel parabolic), and the generic CAP representations attached to the Borel subgroup which do not occur for GSp(4) but appear for higher rank. For general genus g, Arthur’s endoscopic classification organizes the discrete spectrum into Arthur packets indexed by global Arthur parameters ψ : Lℚ × SL(2,ℂ) → GSp(2g,ℂ). A representation is CAP precisely when the SL(2,ℂ) factor of ψ is non‑trivial. The Siegel parabolic CAP representations correspond to ψ factoring through the Levi GL(g)×GL(1) with the SL(2) mapping into the GL(g) factor; the Klingen parabolic CAP representations correspond to ψ factoring through GL(2)×GSp(2g−2) with the SL(2) mapping into the GL(2) factor. The multiplicity formula m(π) = |Sψ|−1 Σs∈Sψ ⟨s,π⟩ shows that CAP representations occur with multiplicity one in the discrete spectrum. For g = 3 the CAP spectrum includes lifts from GL(3) (Siegel type) and from GL(2)×GSp(4) (Klingen type). The work of Gan–Takeda on the local Langlands correspondence for GSp(2g) provides a precise description of the local components of CAP representations in terms of Langlands parameters with a non‑trivial SL(2) factor. In examinations the essential facts are: (i) CAP representations are exactly those with non‑trivial SL(2) in the Arthur parameter; (ii) for GSp(4) there are two CAP families (Saito–Kurokawa and Howe–Piatetski‑Shapiro); (iii) for higher genus the Siegel and Klingen parabolics give the main CAP families; (iv) CAP representations have multiplicity one and are endoscopic.

The Construction of p‑adic L‑functions for GSp(2g) via Eisenstein Measures

Generalizing the work of Urban for GSp(4), the construction of p‑adic L‑functions for higher genus Siegel modular forms uses the Eisenstein measure on the Siegel modular variety of genus g. One considers the Siegel Eisenstein series Ek(Z,s) of weight k and complex parameter s, which converges for Re(s) ≫ 0 and admits meromorphic continuation. The pullback of Ek(Z,s) to the diagonal embedding of the product of two Siegel upper half‑spaces of genera g1 and g2 with g1+g2 = g yields a family of Eisenstein series whose Fourier coefficients are given by the Siegel–Weil formula in terms of representation numbers of quadratic forms. By integrating this family against a Hida family of ordinary Siegel cusp forms of genus g, one obtains a p‑adic measure on the weight space whose Mellin transform interpolates the critical values of the standard L‑function L(s,π,Std) for π a cuspidal automorphic representation of GSp(2g). The resulting p‑adic L‑function Lp(π,Std) satisfies a functional equation relating Lp(π,Std) and Lp(π̃,Std) where π̃ is the contragredient, and its interpolation formula involves the Euler factors at p removed from the complex L‑function. The construction requires the ordinary condition at p (the Up‑operator acts invertibly) and the existence of a Hida family passing through the given classical form, which is known for g = 2 and conjectural for g ≥ 3. Recent work of Liu–Zhang–Zhang (2023) establishes the existence of the Eisenstein measure for all g and proves the interpolation property for the standard L‑function under the assumption of the non‑vanishing of certain local zeta integrals. In examinations candidates should remember: (i) the Eisenstein measure is built from the Siegel Eisenstein series on the diagonal embedding; (ii) it interpolates critical values of the standard L‑function; (iii) the construction works unconditionally for g = 2 and conditionally for higher genus; (iv) the resulting p‑adic L‑function satisfies a functional equation compatible with the complex functional equation.

The Role of the Weil Representation in the Construction of Theta Lifts for GSp(2g)

The Weil representation ωψ of the metaplectic group Mp(2g,𝔸) on the Schwartz space 𝒮(𝔸g) is the fundamental tool for constructing theta lifts from orthogonal groups to symplectic groups. For a dual reductive pair (O(V),Sp(2g)) inside a larger symplectic group Sp(2g·dim V), the theta kernel θφ(g,h) = Σx∈V(ℚ) ωψ(g,h)φ(x) defines an automorphic form on O(V,𝔸)×Sp(2g,𝔸) for φ ∈ 𝒮(V(𝔸)). Integrating θφ against a cusp form on O(V) yields a Siegel modular form on Sp(2g) — the theta lift. The Rallis tower property governs the first occurrence of a given cuspidal representation of O(V) in the tower of theta lifts as g increases. For the pair (O(2n),Sp(2g)) the first occurrence index g0 satisfies g0 ≤ n, and the lift is non‑zero exactly when the standard L‑function L(s,π,Std) has a pole at s = 1. The local theta correspondence is described by the Howe duality conjecture (proved by Waldspurger, Gan–Takeda): for each local place there is a bijection between irreducible representations of O(V) and Sp(2g) that occur in the local Weil representation. The global theta lift preserves L‑packets and matches the Langlands parameters via the natural embedding of L‑groups O(2n,ℂ) → Sp(2g,ℂ). In the case of the split orthogonal group O(2n) the theta lift to Sp(2g) produces CAP representations when g < n and generic cuspidal representations when g = n. The Siegel–Weil formula expresses the integral of the theta kernel over O(V) as a Siegel Eisenstein series, providing the analytic continuation and functional equation of the standard L‑function. In examinations the key points are: (i) the Weil representation of Mp(2g) on Schwartz space; (ii) the theta kernel and its integration give the theta lift; (iii) the Rallis tower property determines first occurrence; (iv) the local theta correspondence is a bijection of L‑packets (Howe duality); (v) the Siegel–Weil formula identifies the theta integral with an Eisenstein series.

  • Confusing the genus‑five Schottky locus with a hypersurface; it is codimension three and defined by pullbacks of the genus‑four Schottky form together with χ35.
  • Assuming the p‑adic Langlands correspondence for GSp(4) is known for all weights; it is constructed only on the eigenvariety for finite‑slope overconvergent forms and matches the Gan–Takeda correspondence at classical points.
  • Thinking CAP representations for GSp(2g) are only Saito–Kurokawa type; for higher genus there are Siegel‑parabolic CAP lifts from GL(g) and Klingen‑parabolic CAP lifts from GL(2)×GSp(2g−2).
  • Believing the Eisenstein measure construction of p‑adic L‑functions works unconditionally for all genera; it is unconditional for g = 2 and relies on conjectural Hida families for g ≥ 3.
  • Misidentifying the Weil representation as a representation of Sp(2g) rather than its metaplectic cover Mp(2g); the theta lift uses the genuine representation of the metaplectic group.
  • Overlooking that the Rallis tower property gives an inequality g0 ≤ n for the first occurrence index, not an equality in all cases.
  • Confusing the standard L‑function of GSp(2g) (degree 2g+1) with the spinor L‑function (degree 2g); the Eisenstein measure interpolates the standard L‑function, not the spinor L‑function.

The advanced sections above complete the required coverage for this lesson segment.
